Abstract: Peer-to-peer collaborative learning is an emerging distributed deep learning paradigm that eliminates the need for a central aggregator and disseminator, avoiding the single-point-of-failure and bottleneck issues they can bring. Many such approaches use parameter averaging, requiring clients to have homogeneous model architectures, whereas others use knowledge distillation as an alternative while leaning on shared public data or centralized control infrastructure. Many of these approaches also make a one-size-fits-all assumption, i.e., jointly training a single, globally optimal solution for all clients, when in reality, clients' underlying data distributions might differ such that a globally optimal solution may not be locally optimal for all clients. To enable collaborative learning in environments with both model architecture and data distribution heterogeneity, we present SPADE-KD -- a fully decentralized model distillation framework that optimizes collaboration towards personalized rather than global performance. SPADE-KD leverages two mechanisms -- adaptive distillation and utility-driven peer selection. Adaptive distillation modulates the strength of each incoming model transfer by the performance advantage of the incoming model compared to the native model measured by their task losses, such that a client will distill more aggressively from incoming models that outperform it on its own local data. Utility-driven peer selection uses the loss-informed utility derived from adaptive distillation to rank prospective partners, focusing on exchanges that measurably improve the local model while suppressing low-utility transfers. Using federated classification tasks, we demonstrate that SPADE-KD consistent outperforms naive decentralized distillation with lower loss and faster convergence and that its individual components are more effective together than independently.

</details>

Abstract: Traditional neural video compression (NVC) methods predominantly follow strictly causal predictive-coding paradigm, treating zero-frame delay as a fixed constraint rather than delay as an explicit design parameter. From an information-theoretic perspective, allowing limited frame delay enables a codec to exploit correlations with future frames within the delay window, thereby reducing uncertainty that is inaccessible to strictly causal prediction and potentially improving the rate-distortion tradeoff. In this work, we formalize this intuition through a rigorous rate-distortion analysis builds on the clique factorization of Markov networks, and propose Graph-Factored Sequential Coding (GFSC), a principled framework for sequential video coding under limited delay. To our knowledge, we provide the first theoretical result showing that, for general $K$-order Markov sources with positive distributions, a $K$-frame delay is sufficient to attain joint-coding rate-distortion optimality over a nontrivial distortion region, a guarantee unavailable to zero-delay causal coding in general. In practice, we instantiate GFSC through lightweight neural modules, allowing existing causal predictive codecs to be augmented without modifying their core architectures. Experiments across multiple NVC models and datasets show that GFSC consistently improves rate-distortion performance over the corresponding causal counterparts. Under matched delay budgets, GFSC also achieves competitive performance compared with representative bidirectional codecs.

</details>

Abstract: Recent Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) approaches address missing modalities either by routing each missingness pattern to a dedicated expert, which scales poorly as the number of modalities grows, or by imputing the missing modality from representations contributed by multiple experts, which requires a costly retrieval mechanism. We show that routing induces expert-conditioned representation neighborhoods and derive an imputation-error bound controlled by their local feature dispersion. Pre-imputation generates a context-conditioned estimate in the encoder embedding space and radially soft-clips it using modality-specific statistics maintained in an observed FIFO queue, this ensures that the pre-imputed modality stays in empirically calibrated region of the target modality space. Post-imputation refines the missing representation after MoE by aggregating expert-conditioned observed representations within the same sample. Across four missing-modality benchmarks under natural, random, and asymmetric dropout protocols, TWIN achieves state-of-the-art performance while using substantially fewer parameters than competing MoE-based imputation methods.

Abstract: Standard self-attention compares tokens by similarity but does not account for how much of a past state survives to the present. We introduce Dynamical Kernel Attention (DKA), which derives attention from robust estimation of a latent state evolving under stochastic dynamics. As a past state decays toward equilibrium under the dynamics, the information it carries about the present vanishes, bounding the total evidence a head can draw. Unlike recency biases, DKA can abstain when relevant evidence is weak and guarantees a bounded error when old tokens are discarded. DKA preserves the parallel structure and computational cost of standard attention. We present the model and its theory; empirical evaluation is deferred to a subsequent version.

Abstract: RL agents trained on a single trajectory-level return face a credit-assignment problem: when a reward arrives many steps after the decision that caused it, every action gets the same signal regardless of causal responsibility. This paper studies Visual Causal-chain Bookmarking (VCBM), which concentrates credit on the decision points that determine an episode's return, via two instantiations. The first targets a fully observed, low-dimensional MDP (a delayed-reward watch-repair task), where VCBM statistically discovers which state components the agent causally controls and estimates per-decision-point action values directly, evaluated against ground truth and RUDDER, an LSTM-based baseline. VCBM converges to 95.07% correct decisions in a mean of 1,532.3 episodes across 20 seeds, versus RUDDER's slower, more variable convergence (mean 3,327.1 episodes; p<0.001); VCBM's discovered causal structure exactly matches ground truth in all seeds. The second targets a partially observed, language-based setting: an LLM agent trained with Leave-One-Out PPO (LOOP) in AppWorld, where causal discovery is intractable and a structural heuristic (state-mutating tool calls) substitutes for it, with credit distributed via retrieval over episodic memory and blended with LOOP's advantage. Over 200 iterations on identical Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct/LoRA/FSDP2 configurations, VCBM-blended LOOP improves task success over plain LOOP (0.354 vs. 0.458), reduces final-step max importance weight, and improves token efficiency, with a small KL exception favouring LOOP. The watch-repair result supports the claim that weighting causally relevant steps is more sample-efficient than an undifferentiated baseline or a learned return-redistribution network; the AppWorld result, limited to one seed per arm by a 2-GPU budget, is a compute-limited demonstration at a harder, natural-language scale, not a statistical confirmation.

Most failures are fits delivered with a systematic residual on their own fitting data, where the handover left open how close is close enough. In a follow-up, stating a threshold makes the agents compare against it, and more deliveries pass. The argument applies wherever a handover underspecifies a model meant to be read, not only run.

Abstract: This paper studies dynamic model routing under uncertainty in multimodal settings, where a system must select and potentially compose multiple models to answer a query. Standard reinforcement learning approaches for routing often suffer from premature convergence to suboptimal models due to noisy rewards and insufficient exploration. We propose a unified vision-language framework (VL-Scout) for efficient multimodal and multi-step reasoning with turn-based reinforcement fusion. First, VL-Scout combines Bayesian priors, policy gradient optimization, and sequential decision-making to enable structured exploration and adaptive inference. A context-dependent Dirichlet belief model allows for epistemic uncertainty over model quality and is updated online based on observed rewards and context-specific evidence, enabling Thompson-style exploration while serving as a prior for regularization. Second, a routing policy is trained via a KL-regularized REINFORCE objective by maximizing a reward-weighted evidence lower bound, where the policy acts as an amortized posterior, and the Dirichlet belief serves as a prior. Third, we introduce a fusion policy trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) or Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), which operates over intermediate model rationales and selects between two actions: CONTINUE or ANSWER, terminating with a generated output. We evaluate VL-Scout on four representative multi-modal benchmarks (MMMU, MMMU-Pro, OKVQA, and RealWorldQA), compared against 11 representative routing and policy optimization baselines. Our method provides consistent performance improvements while reducing premature policy collapse. These results highlight the importance of integrating uncertainty-aware priors with both amortized policy learning and sequential fusion for robust and efficient model selection in discrete action spaces

Abstract: Neuroimaging seeks brain regions whose imaging features correlate with variables of interest (e.g., diagnosis, age), usually via the General Linear Model (GLM). Region-Based Analysis dilutes or splits any effect that does not fill exactly one atlas region. Voxel-Based Analysis, with Cluster Extent Thresholding and Threshold-Free Cluster Enhancement built on it, fits a GLM per voxel and groups adjacent significant voxels. But neighboring voxels can share a statistic without sharing a GLM, which a per-voxel statistic cannot detect.
  GLOW (General Linear Models Optimized with Ward's method) instead builds a hierarchy of nested regions by greedily minimizing GLM error (provably a special case of Ward's clustering). Maximum-statistic (Westfall-Young) permutation controls family-wise error over all regions. Significance spreads to an effect's nested fragments and the regions containing it; a final pruning step seeks whole, pure effect extents.
  We plant synthetic effects of known extent and varying strength in Human Connectome Project (HCP) diffusion images and in white noise. Pruning delivers wholeness: GLOW returns a spatially consistent effect as one region on 57% of HCP trials, the best baseline on 35%; baselines recover comparable volume, but in many pure pieces. The cost is positive predictive value (PPV): GLOW is more sensitive (by up to 0.29), but its regions overrun the effect, mostly because no hierarchy region isolates it, not because of pruning. On HCP, GLOW's mean Dice gains 0.059 over the best baseline at weaker effects and gives back 0.005 at stronger ones.
  On white noise, GLOW trails by up to 0.61 Dice: its hierarchy holds the effect, but its permutation test does not certify it. Runtime grows roughly linearly with voxel count, about 4.5 times the voxel-wise methods' on the full HCP volume. The PPV loss may trace to the assumption of spatially independent residuals.

</details>

Abstract: Generalizing from small graphs to larger ones can require computation beyond the depth of a neural model used during training. We study target tasks that admit a common local iterative computation and whose effective dependency radius grows with graph size. The central idea is to learn a local update rule through GNN pretraining, then repeatedly apply the same learned processor beyond the training depth to extend information reach on larger graphs without retraining. Calling such parameter-shared architecture a \emph{looped GNN}, we categorize six schemes for looped GNNs to separate the effects of depth extension, fine-tuning, and freeze-and-steer adaptation. Our analysis establishes fixed-depth information limits and sufficient conditions under which accurate iterative reuse overcomes them. To improve beyond unadapted reuse, we introduce \emph{Loop-Freeze-Steer} (LFS), which freezes the pretrained processor and learns a small controller to steer its repeated application, using much fewer trainable parameters than full fine-tuning. Our finite-sample analysis gives sufficient conditions for adaptation to improve performance on larger graphs. Experiments on synthetic tasks and chip placement evaluate depth extension and adaptation, highlighting LFS's accuracy--trainable-parameter trade-off for size generalization.

</details>

Abstract: We develop a deep Pontryagin learning framework for high-dimensional continuous-time stochastic control problems that naturally accommodates jump-diffusions. In contrast to direct policy optimization, which repeatedly differentiates the objective through the entire time-discretized dynamics, our method separates adjoint learning from policy improvement by explicitly incorporating state-sensitivity information from the Pontryagin stochastic maximum principle. For a fixed Markov control, we first compute pathwise adjoint labels by backpropagating the reward through time along simulated state trajectories, and project these labels onto the Markov state through a supervised regression step. The control is then updated by enforcing the pointwise Hamiltonian stationarity condition. We prove that the resulting projected adjoint process satisfies a discrete-time Pontryagin principle and converges, as the time step tends to zero, to the solution of the corresponding continuous-time adjoint backward stochastic differential equation. Numerical experiments illustrate the accuracy, stability, and scalability of the approach.

</details>

Abstract: Synthetic control methods estimate counterfactual outcomes by weighting untreated donor units to match a treated unit before treatment. In recent applications, however, the donor pool can be large, highly correlated, and short in pre-treatment history, yielding many near-equivalent solutions with unstable and difficult-to-interpret donor weights. We propose Graph-Regularized Synthetic Control (Graph-SC), a convex estimator that augments the classical pre-treatment matching objective with a graph Laplacian penalty. The graph encodes similarity among donor trajectories, encouraging coherent weights over structurally related donors while preserving the transparency of synthetic controls. We provide theoretical results characterizing the bias-variance tradeoff induced by graph regularization and establish efficient convergence guarantees with an accelerated proximal gradient method. Across a controlled simulation under donor redundancy, the California Proposition 99 benchmark, and observational counterfactual analyses on the Waymo Open Motion Dataset, Graph-SC improves weight stability, reduces sensitivity to redundant donor pools, and maintains competitive pre- and post-treatment accuracy relative to classical and regularized synthetic control baselines. Our results suggest that graph regularization is most valuable not as a universal accuracy booster, but as a principled mechanism for selecting stable and interpretable counterfactuals from near-equivalent synthetic controls with comparable pre-treatment fit.

</details>

Abstract: Designing small-molecule drugs that selectively bind to a target protein while avoiding unintended interactions with other proteins is essential for reducing side effects that contribute substantially to clinical attrition. Although structure-based drug design (SBDD) has advanced rapidly with deep learning, most generative models still optimize binding to a single target and disregard selectivity due to limited training data and unknown off-target interactions. Motivated by this gap, we introduce SELECTBENCH, a comprehensive benchmark assessing the selectivity of generative SBDD models. SELECTBENCH provides three curated evaluation tasks reflecting different real-world drug discovery scenarios: literature-based case studies, safety screening panels used in pharmaceutical development, and large-scale structure-based evaluation sets. We further introduce a suite of metrics to quantify selectivity and conduct a systematic evaluation of nine representative SBDD models, including methods explicitly trained for selectivity and widely-used inference-time guidance techniques. Our results show that even selectivity-trained models achieve only modest off-target discrimination and inference-time guidance provides limited gains, underscoring the need for method improvement. SELECTBENCH provides both a characterization of where current generative SBDD stands on selectivity and an open, extensible platform to accelerate progress. Abstract: Solving algorithmic tasks with many sequential steps typically requires scaling up transformer depth or chain-of-thought length, incurring unfavorable parameter and inference-compute costs, respectively. Looped transformers, which reuse one block of layers across many iterations, are poised to address this, since they scale effective depth without adding parameters or tokens. Yet, it remains unclear what each iteration is actually computing, or whether the loop merely simulates a deeper untied stack. Here, we study directed permutation traversal, in which a model follows $H$ hops from a starting node and reports the node it reaches, so every intermediate hop has a known ground truth. We find that a minimum effective depth is necessary to solve the task, beyond which the degree of weight tying becomes the determining factor. For instance, a looped transformer with a four-layer block iterated eight times solves the task in every seed, while an identically-trained untied transformer of the same effective depth never does, succeeding only with a lower learning rate or supervision of its intermediate layers. Probing and intervening on the residual stream, we find that the looped model advances through the intermediate nodes gradually, forming a staircase that adapts to its iteration budget, whereas the untied model, at that lower rate, exposes them only in its final layers. Taken together, our results recast looped transformers as iterative program learners, which allocate their iterations to successive steps of the algorithm and solve tasks that identically-trained untied models of the same effective depth do not. Code is available at https://github.com/magnus-sesodia/insidetheloop.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly operate in open-ended environments, where they are expected to continually adapt to evolving tasks and distributions, often without access to external supervision. Existing continual learning approaches rely on external supervision, such as labeled data, demonstrations, or reward signals, which can be costly or unavailable in practice. Test-time adaptation removes this dependence by learning from self-generated supervision, but existing methods derive that supervision from the single policy being updated, so once the policy drifts, its own learning signal drifts with it, leading to policy collapse, degraded reasoning, and catastrophic forgetting. In this paper, we propose \textbf{Evolution Strategy Reinforcement Learning (ESRL)}, a continual test-time learning framework that draws its self-generated supervision from a population of policies instead of the single policy being trained. ESRL perturbs an anchor policy in parameter space to form a population of nearby policies, samples one trajectory from each, and aggregates their answers into label-free group-relative learning signals for the central student. The anchor is held fixed within each block and refreshed from the student at block boundaries, so the population stays centered on a stable yet current policy and continues to supply diverse and reliable supervision as the student moves. In single-task test-time adaptation across mathematical reasoning, general knowledge, and medical domains, ESRL improves average accuracy by up to 31.7% relative to the base model and 16.6% relative to the strongest test-time adaptation baseline. Notably, ESRL substantially reduces catastrophic forgetting and achieves the strongest final performance in continual learning.

</details>

Abstract: Biomechanical movement recordings are continuous, high-dimensional time series, and analyzing them without manually labeling them requires representations that capture how movements evolve over time. Most unsupervised representation-learning pipelines, however, treat each observation frame independently and discard this temporal structure, while energy-based models that could capture it have not extensively been adapted to biomechanical signals. So, this paper implements the Recurrent Temporal Restricted Boltzmann Machine (RTRBM), a recurrent energy-based model, into an unsupervised encoder that maps windows of movement data to fixed-length embeddings, and tests what its recurrent structure contributes. We evaluate the embeddings on simulated musculoskeletal reaching and on two real gait datasets, using clustering, reconstruction, and walking-speed classification of unseen subjects, and compare them against non-recurrent and non-probabilistic baselines, including a control that removes the recurrent connection. Clustering the embeddings recovers movement structure tied to progress toward the target without any labels, which also transfers to unseen episodes. On gait data, the embeddings outperform PCA and autoencoder baselines under a nonlinear probe. However, removing the recurrent connection does not yield a measurable loss on the reaching task, and simple per-channel summary statistics classify walking speed at least as well as the embeddings. The model is therefore a working, verified encoder for SIT-FUSE, while the evidence for a benefit of recurrence is mixed, and the learned embeddings did not outperform simple summary statistics on gait.

The constant is statistic-specific: the effective sample size is $n/τ_{int}(g)$, with $τ_{int}(g) \le 2t_{rel}-1$. When the dependence scale is unknown, a batch-means Student-t interval is valid up to an explicit remainder vanishing in the number of batches, with width governed by $τ_{int}(g)$ rather than the worst-case $t_{rel}$. Experiments show: the learned-vote certificate is nonvacuous once the block count is large; a two-timescale construction gives a batch-means interval 8.1x narrower than the worst-case interval at nominal coverage (0.945); block-bootstrap and batch-means intervals undercover under strong dependence while the certificate stays valid; and on real, ordered UCI HAR sensor data the dependence-agnostic certificate reaches 0.95 coverage where naive intervals reach only 0.38.

Abstract: EEG analysis and decoding underpin brain-computer interfaces, cognitive assessment, and sleep monitoring. Fixed pipelines and reward-driven search do not explicitly determine how signal structure, representations, predictor behavior, and training dynamics contribute to performance or generalization gaps, leaving intervention choice underdetermined. We introduce NeuroAct, an autonomous research agent framework for EEG analysis and decoding. NeuroAct implements dual-loop autonomous research across EEG foundation models, task-specific predictors, and model combinations. In an inner loop, research, development, and review roles use evidence on signal structure, representations, predictor behavior, and training dynamics to test hypotheses, select analyses or interventions, and refine candidates using performance, stability, and cost feedback. An outer loop distills outcomes into conditional, testable recommendations with task and model conditions, then validates and revises them on subsequent tasks.We evaluate four settings: (A) EEG General Analysis, (B) Cross-task Model Optimization, (C) Single-task Open Research, and (D) Collaborative Prediction. In Setting A, NeuroAct's 80.88 macro-average exceeds BrainAgent's 63.13 by 17.75 points. In Setting B, NeuroAct raises EEGPT's mean balanced accuracy (BA) by 7.34 points and HBN Pearson's (r) from 0.6894 to 0.7362; in Setting C, it improves ADFTD BA by 16.33 points; and in Setting D, it raises Workload BA from 79.69% to 85.02%. Experience reuse raises BCIC-IV-2a BA from 34.66% to 50.81% while reducing LLM tokens by 64.02%, indicating recommendations improve cross-task performance. NeuroAct enables evidence-guided EEG research improved by cross-task experience.

Abstract: Tree-based samplers offer a compelling alternative for scalable inference-time search, bypassing the per-step reward and gradient dependencies that introduce systematic bias into SMC-style frameworks. Despite this advantage, contemporary tree samplers fail to achieve broad exploration. Their reliance on learned, DDPM-like transitions inherently couples neighbor states in a trajectory, thereby restricting child-parent feature divergence in the tree sampler. Consequently, these samplers suffer from restricted coverage of the target marginal distribution, severely hindering the exploration critical for efficient search under sparse reward. To overcome these limitations, we introduce a simple yet effective Bootstrap Stochastic Interpolant Tree (BSIT) sampler that decouples child node generation from explicit parent dependencies. By expanding the degrees of freedom during child node sampling, BSIT encourages diverse trajectory rollouts and unlocks broader path-space diversity-the exact catalyst needed for efficient exploration. Crucially, we show that BSIT's improved exploration accelerates convergence to the target distribution while achieving broader distributional coverage than samplers that rely on DDPM-like transitions for child-node generation, all without incurring additional function evaluations or reward feedback, thereby providing a highly efficient basis for rare target search. Extensive experiments across diverse search settings, including rare-species discovery and target-aligned molecule generation tasks, demonstrate BSIT's superior search efficiency over the baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Policy gradients for autoregressive sequence models differentiate each token's log-probability while holding the conditioning history fixed. This discards information about how earlier decisions shape later conditional distributions, creating a credit-assignment bottleneck when rewards are sparse. We introduce History-Reparameterized (HR) policy gradients, which replace the frozen prefix in the score function with a differentiable reparameterized or relaxed history, allowing gradients of downstream log-probabilities to propagate backward through the generated trajectory. The resulting history-pathwise term is generally biased when added directly, so we derive Optimal HR (OHR), a centered control-variate estimator with a closed-form coefficient that minimizes the trace of the gradient variance at the population level. The variance reduction is governed by temporal structure in the policy dynamics: random or weakly structured policies provide little benefit, while policies with learned sequential dependencies yield informative history-pathwise corrections. For discrete tokens, the choice of relaxation is crucial: embedding-level relaxation yields substantial variance reduction that grows with horizon, whereas straight-through Gumbel-Softmax yields essentially none in our diagnostics. We validate the method on a Gaussian linear Markov model, a sparse-reward T-maze with a recurrent policy, and an autoregressive Transformer model for small Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) instances.

</details>

Abstract: Living plants, like animals, use electrical signals to carry information about their physiological state across their body, from roots to branches. These signals can be recorded, as they are in animals, from a pair of differential extracellular electrodes inserted into signal-conducting tissue. While machine-learning models have been trained to predict specific plant stress states from these signals, each of these models has been trained for only a single task and none have been designed to transfer across tasks, species, or environments. These approaches do not allow easy scaling across the wide range of species and environments that are relevant to agriculture, forestry, and horticulture for both commercial and consumer plant care.
  Here we present Bombadil, a foundation model for plant electrophysiology, pretrained without labels on over 4 million hours of electrical signals recorded from 100 plant species in ten distinct environments, including laboratories, greenhouses, commercial orchards, and natural forests. In this way, Bombadil is able to learn the fundamental features of plant electrical signals across a wide array of species and environments. Then, from those same electrical signals, Bombadil tracks changes in seven physiological indicators of plant stress, even among species and stressors with no labels in training.

Abstract: Test-time adaptation (TTA) adapts pretrained models to distribution shifts encountered during deployment. However, most existing methods rely on the weight representations of the final layer or signals derived from the model's output probability distribution. We study hidden trajectories: the ordered sequence of intermediate representations produced across a deep neural network. We observe that clean and corrupted trajectories become more separable as corruption severity increases. We introduce Drift-TTA, which adds zero-initialized low-rank adapters to a frozen backbone and trains them to align corrupted hidden trajectories with those from cleaner inputs. The approach supports two variants: a source-dependent version trained with clean targets, and a source-free setting that learns from corrupted images at different severities. We develop an online TTA approach that freezes the backbone and adapters while updating a scalar gate to control adaptation strength. We further cast trajectory alignment as MAP inference under a Gaussian observation model and derive a KL-based transfer bound characterizing test-time trajectory misalignment in terms of offline residual error, residual dimensionality, and distribution shift between offline and test corruptions. We evaluate Drift-TTA on CIFAR-10-C, CIFAR-100-C, and ImageNet-C using supervised ViTs and CLIP models. On CIFAR-100-C at severity 5, our source-free online approach improves accuracy over no TTA by 13.3 percentage points with ViT-B/16 and 11.6 percentage points with CLIP ViT-B/16, reaching 74.9% and 47.4%, respectively. Under the same setting, prior TTA baselines yield ECEs of 18.00% for ViT and 14.70% for CLIP, while our approach reduces them to 9.53% and 5.77%, respectively. These results demonstrate that hidden trajectory alignment provides an effective signal for both offline correction and lightweight online test-time adaptation.

</details>

Abstract: We study optimal transport between distributions when only a subset of the target population is relevant to the source. The goal is to map the source distribution to a reweighted target distribution whose density ratio with respect to the full target lies in $[0, c]$. This constraint enables the transported distribution to select an appropriate target subpopulation while limiting how strongly any region of the target can be emphasized. We propose a dynamic neural optimal transport framework for this setting using two neural networks. The first parameterizes a time-dependent potential whose spatial gradient defines the velocity field and whose terminal value enforces the density-ratio constraint. The second parameterizes an interpolation between the source distribution and the learned terminal distribution. Unlike prior neural dynamic optimal transport methods, which typically assume a fixed terminal distribution, our formulation learns the terminal distribution within the support of the target. We demonstrate the method on positive-unlabeled learning, unpaired domain translation, and trajectory inference, where meaningful alignments exist between source and target distributions, but direct distribution matching is inappropriate because the target contains additional or irrelevant components

</details>

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are increasingly deployed through black-box APIs and trained on large collections of real-world tabular datasets. As this training paradigm becomes more widely adopted, private or proprietary datasets may be incorporated into pre-training corpora, yet there are currently no dedicated methods for determining whether a given tabular dataset was used to train a TFM. As a solution, we introduce the first dataset inference method for TFMs, aiming to infer whether a suspect dataset was part of a model's pre-training data. We systematically analyze a broad collection of candidate signals that can be observed from a black-box TFM via input manipulation and find that individual signals provide only weak evidence of training data membership. However, by combining multiple complementary signals, we can infer dataset membership for several state-of-the-art TFMs trained on real tabular data, achieving up to 0.99 ROCAUC dependent on the TFM. We then study factors that influence dataset identification, including pre-training data composition, model capacity, and the use of real vs. synthetic data. Our results show that our dataset inference method is a practical auditing tool for detecting privacy leakage and the use of proprietary datasets in TFMs.

Abstract: Feedback-driven LLM optimizers are increasingly used for evolutionary search, Bayesian optimization, heuristic design, and scientific discovery. Yet rapid initial gains often give way to prolonged plateaus despite substantial remaining evaluation budgets. We investigate \emph{principle-level search collapse}, where an optimizer's search becomes confined to a narrow set of design principles, even though it may continue to generate distinct candidates and achieve small improvements. We assemble a diagnostic benchmark spanning molecular optimization, network sentinel-node selection, and GPU-kernel optimization, and introduce a decision-tree protocol combining principle narrowing, prolonged performance plateaus, and gaps to attainable reference solutions. Across 161 assessable runs on the three problem domains, 78.3\% receive support for collapse, including 55.3\% with strong support. Candidate-level variation can nevertheless conceal restricted search at the principle level. We then test \emph{diversity injection}, a common response to search stagnation, such as higher t, multiple LLMs, and web search, and show that it provides limited and inconsistent benefits: among 65 molecular runs, still 73.8\% and 53.8\% receive support and strong support respectively. We finally test reference-principle interventions and find that they improve some runs, but their benefits vary across models and tasks: access to a better principle does not guarantee its effective use. Our benchmark and diagnostic protocol provide a basis for evaluating future optimizers on principle-level collapse. Our findings highlight the need for online collapse detection and better mechanisms for generating and maintaining alternative principles during optimization.

</details>

Abstract: Learned representations often contain geometric structure induced by symmetries in the underlying data. We ask whether a known continuous parameter space can instead be used to \emph{induce} such structure when the corresponding symmetry is only approximate. We introduce a graph-Laplacian smoothness prior that encourages representations to vary smoothly over a physical parameter space without specifying a representation geometry; in the continuum limit it selects Laplacian eigenfunctions and therefore predicts the Fourier-like geometry associated with translation symmetry. On multimodal stellar representations spanning six astronomical surveys, the prior induces Toeplitz Gram structure and sinusoidal modes along stellar parameters, including in modalities whose native representations lack this geometry, while modality-specific residuals preserve structure outside the imposed coordinates. Of the exact-symmetry predictions, stationarity in a recoverable warped coordinate survives; diagnostics assuming exact quantization or asymptotic rates do not. The prior also improves downstream linear-probe performance and label efficiency, suggesting a general strategy: where a meaningful continuous parameter space exists, its local geometry can serve as a data-informed prior.

</details>

Abstract: Neural networks whose inputs are functions, as in operator learning, are usually shown to be universal by existence arguments, either by reduction to finite-dimensional networks or by Stone--Weierstrass-type density; these prove that approximants exist but say little about how the parameters depend on the target. We take a constructive route. We extend the ridgelet transform, an integral transform related to the Fourier, Radon, and wavelet transforms, to continuous maps on a separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, replacing the Lebesgue measure that is unavailable in infinite dimensions by Gaussian weights with a homogeneous mixture on the direction space. The resulting transform has a Plancherel identity and reconstruction formulas incorporating the frame operator, the correction induced by the weights, and it assigns to each target an explicit coefficient measure, one for every admissible analysis filter. From these representations we obtain uniform approximation on compact sets for all continuous non-polynomial activations of polynomial growth, including ReLU, and, by Monte Carlo sampling of the coefficient, finite-width networks with a dimension-free $O(N^{-1/2})$ error in the width $N$ for globally Lipschitz activations and coefficients with finite variation and second moments. Explicit examples include Gaussian targets and neural-operator layers. A companion Lean~4 formalization provides machine-checked proofs, with its correspondence to the manuscript documented at https://shosonoda.github.io/lean-operator-ridgelet/ .

</details>

A fine-tuned 3.8B-parameter model Phi-3.5-mini, trained with LoRA in 2.5 hours on a single Apple M5 Pro chip, achieves 69.9% accuracy on severity and 70.9% on CWE. It matches the official vector exactly for 48.8% of test CVEs, outperforming TF-IDF logistic regression by 2.8 to 4.7 points on all accuracy metrics based on zero-exclusion paired bootstrap intervals. Abstract: We present Torch-DPhys, a Torch-native framework implemented in PyTorch for differentiable physics simulation and hybrid physics-machine learning. It implements selected thermal-hydraulic modeling concepts and discretizations from MOOSE's Thermal Hydraulics Module and the System Analysis Module (SAM), with a preliminary extension to continuum mechanics. Native tensor residuals support CPU and GPU execution, compilation of selected numerical kernels, and sparse direct linear solves. Sensitivities are obtained by differentiating executed numerical operations or through discrete adjoints of converged residual equations. Verification combines analytical solutions, reference-code comparisons, and derivative checks. Demonstrations cover plane-strain elasticity, one- and two-loop thermal-hydraulic transients, and an axisymmetric molten-salt fast reactor. For the reactor, comparison with SAM-Lite gives a maximum normalized field discrepancy of 1.13 x 10^-5 over 200 adaptive time steps, demonstrating agreement between the tested discrete implementations. Selected transient sensitivities are assessed using tangent-linear calculations and finite differences on fixed time schedules. Two applications illustrate the framework's connection between simulation and optimization. A residual-adjoint parameter-identification calculation recovers nominal pump head and heating power with relative errors below 0.06% from noiseless synthetic MOOSE observations. A separate three-loop plant demonstration uses solver-in-the-loop training of a predictive controller by differentiating trajectory-level objectives through the thermal-hydraulic simulation. These examples illustrate a foundation for gradient-based inference, control, and coupled physics-machine learning models in nuclear engineering.

</details>

Abstract: We study the Muon optimizer using sharpness and provide the first empirical evidence that the Flat Minima Hypothesis also applies to Muon. Further, we derive Muon's implicit preconditioner to analyze its potential Edge of Stability behavior. However, Muon operates above the classical sharpness-based Adaptive Edge of Stability threshold that bounds diagonally-preconditioned optimizers like Adam and RMSprop, yet remains stable. Abstract: Probabilistic circuits (PCs) are generative models that support efficient probabilistic inference. Adding latent states to a PC increases its representational capacity, but yields diminishing gains in likelihood. We use mutual information to examine how the dependence represented by a trained PC changes with latent size. Experiments with Hidden Chow-Liu Trees (HCLTs) on ImageNet32 show that learned dependence grows slowly and remains well below both the latent-state upper bound and estimates of dependence in the data. To understand how learning uses these states, we examine how strongly each sample favors particular latent states and how broadly states are used across the dataset. Tracking these measurements during HCLT training distinguishes rapid early posterior concentration from the more gradual stabilization of sample-dependent state preferences. Early measurements also guide initialization selection in HCLTs and hidden Markov models (HMMs), as well as tree selection in HCLTs. The resulting choices improve the ultimate performance across the tested latent sizes, providing a practical benefit from our study of latent-state utilization.

Abstract: A universal forecaster is trained once and applied everywhere, so it must serve tasks whose schemas, forecast horizons, and temporal resolutions all differ. The number of targets and covariates changes from dataset to dataset, horizons vary by orders of magnitude, and sampling rates range from seconds to years. Existing time-series foundation models address these only in part, since they isolate each series or visit the two grid relations in separate blocks, and they fix the horizon and resolution before training begins. We introduce Morpheus-2.0, a compact encoder-only foundation model that represents targets and covariates on a single variable-time grid. Its dual-axis attention runs both relations side by side in every layer, one across variables at a timestep and one across time within a variable; a learned gate weights the two per layer, and an axis-aware rotary encoding preserves both grid coordinates. The forecast length is sampled during training, so one set of weights spans short to long horizons at no additional inference cost. Morpheus-2.0 Multiscale extends the model to several temporal resolutions at once, weighting them according to the series being forecast. Across GIFT-Eval, which spans several domains and frequencies, the two models give complementary operating points: Morpheus-2.0 attains 0.685 relative MASE with 81M parameters, while the larger Morpheus-2.0 Multiscale attains 0.646 relative MASE and 0.455 relative CRPS, the lowest of any published system on both metrics.

Abstract: We study realizable multiclass PAC learning with query feedback, where each training example provides a single binary response indicating whether its true label belongs to a chosen subset from a fixed query family. Our main contribution is the query dimension, a combinatorial parameter that captures both the structure of the hypothesis class and the ability of the available queries to distinguish labels. This parameter generalizes the bandit DS dimension, which it recovers under singleton queries. We establish a sample-complexity lower bound of $Ω(D/\varepsilon)$ and an upper bound of $\widetilde{O}(D^2/\varepsilon)$, suppressing factors polylogarithmic in the number of labels and the failure probability. Abstract: Sampling temperature can substantially reshape autoregressive generation, but it is unclear when temperature changes in long-range correlations reflect a genuine phase transition rather than a smooth change in statistics. We study this question in a controlled analogue of temperature sampling: a finite-depth probabilistic context-free grammar on a fixed hierarchical tree, where exact next-token conditionals are computed by belief propagation and then rescaled by a softmax temperature before autoregressive sampling. We measure two-point mutual information between tokens as a function of separation. Across the tested temperatures and grammar ensembles, mutual information remains well described by a power-law envelope. Temperature changes the correlation magnitude and fitted exponent, but within the accessible system sizes we find no long-distance plateau characteristic of an ordered phase and no clear crossover to short-range exponential decay. An approximate mapping to an Ising model on a tree explains how exponential decay in tree distance becomes a power law in token distance, allowing power laws to persist without tuning to a special decoding temperature. This controlled baseline clarifies which temperature effects can arise from hierarchical structure alone, before adding training, approximation error, or neural architecture.

</details>

Abstract: Uniform convergence asks how many random samples suffice for empirical averages to approximate expectations simultaneously over an entire class of functions. It is a central concept in probability and a foundation of statistical learning. For binary-valued classes, the Vapnik--Chervonenkis theorem characterizes uniform convergence by finiteness of the VC dimension. For dimension $d$, the sharp worst-case sample complexity for additive error $\varepsilon$ is of order $d/\varepsilon^2$. For real-valued functions, the analogous combinatorial parameter is the fat-shattering dimension, which depends on an approximation scale $γ$. The challenge is to achieve error $γ+\varepsilon$ with order $d/\varepsilon^2$ samples, matching the sample bound for binary classes, where $d$ is now the fat-shattering dimension at scale $γ$. Previous results achieved either the optimal scale or the optimal sample complexity, but not both. Our main result closes this gap, establishing the sharp sample complexity at the optimal scale. Along the way, we use several tools that may be of independent interest. The first concerns $\varepsilon$-nets with margin. A classical $\varepsilon$-net intersects every set of mass at least $\varepsilon$ in a prescribed family; we allow the net to intersect a slightly enlarged version of each such set. Remarkably, this relaxation permits bounds independent of the ambient dimension in natural geometric settings. Second, we derive improved covering bounds in both discrete and real-valued settings: we extend Haussler's covering lemma to partial concept classes and use this extension to obtain $\ell_2$ covers at the optimal scale for real-valued function classes. Third, we obtain a chaining bound at the optimal scale by aligning the covers so that successive approximations become increasingly close to one another.

</details>

Abstract: Before a new policy replaces a deployed one, we often want a certificate, computed from logged episodes, that it is better. We ask how many episodes such a certificate needs when the logging policy is known but history-dependent, and the error probability must stay below $δ$ in every environment consistent with the logs. The answer is an exponent $K^\star$: the smallest KL divergence from the logged law to a law with the same logged action probabilities under which the target does not improve. No sound certifier stops in fewer than $\log(1/δ)/K^\star$ expected episodes, at any $δ$, and whenever $0<K^\star<\infty$ a plug-in Kelly certifier attains this rate to first order as $δ\to0$. Coverage, computed by one backward induction, decides whether certification is impossible ($K^\star=0$), possible at a finite rate, or possible in a constant number of episodes ($K^\star=\infty$). By convex duality, the optimal certificate bets on the importance-weighted advantage and also places fair bets on the logged actions, so doubly robust control variates act as hedges rather than estimators. As a consequence, no choice of off-policy estimator changes $K^\star$, a coverage ceiling explains the curse of memory, and structural assumptions can be priced in exponent units. A cushion certifier extends the approach to trees too large to enumerate. In experiments the plug-in's median stopping time is 1.13-1.43x the lower bound on memory tasks, 1.13-1.51x on Tiger ($H\le5$) and 1.17-1.64x on random trees. The best scalar betting baselines need up to 2.65x (memory) and 4.1x (Tiger) the plug-in's episodes; only on a few shallow random trees is a doubly robust bet faster, by at most 18%.

</details>

Abstract: A low-precision agent can call its full-precision (BF16) model at selected decisions as a fallback. A learned precision router chooses where to call it from the disagreement between the two models and is judged by end-to-end success. An inserted BF16 action, however, can rescue a failing task or damage a succeeding one. We argue that a fallback's value is the number of tasks it saves minus the number it spoils, measured for the intervention that will actually run. From restored states, we run paired branches that cross which model supplies the current action with which model continues, separating replacement of one action, takeover of later execution, and both. On Qwen3 agents with simulated 4-bit weights in tau2-bench, replacing the first action where the two models differed in tool use changed many task outcomes, but rescues and damages nearly cancelled, and on held-out telecom tasks the action-only estimate was negative. Replacing both the action and the continuation had a positive net gain in two of five exploratory lower-bit stress settings. Two references changed outcomes at fixed bit width: replacing an action with that of a second 4-bit checkpoint changed about as many outcomes as replacing it with the BF16 action, and reordering tool-argument keys changed outcomes while the executed tool call stayed identical. At common states, the additional value of the BF16 action over that checkpoint remained unresolved. A frozen uncertainty trigger found more outcome changes than random selection, while its net benefit stayed within the range of random selection. We recommend evaluating each fallback by its signed task benefit under the intended continuation, against same-precision references, before attributing gains to more bits.

Abstract: We revisit smoothed online learning and develop algorithms with improved competitive ratios, optimal dynamic regret with switching cost, and best-of-both-worlds guarantees. Surprisingly, minimizing the hitting cost alone is $\max(1,\frac{2}α)$-competitive for $α$-polyhedral functions under the $\ell_2$-norm switching cost and $1+\frac{4}λ$-competitive for $λ$-quadratic growth functions under the squared $\ell_2$-norm switching cost, significantly improving existing guarantees. The polyhedral bound is tight for this naive algorithm, and we establish a matching lower bound for $α\geq1$. For convex $λ$-quadratic growth hitting costs, minimizing a suitably weighted sum of hitting and squared $\ell_2$-norm switching costs yields a competitive ratio of $1+\frac{2}{\sqrtλ}$. For dynamic regret with the $\ell_2$-norm switching cost, we develop Smoothed Ader (SAder), which achieves the optimal $O(\sqrt{T(1+P_T)})$ rate in the standard online convex optimization setting, where $P_T$ is the comparator's path-length. Its lookahead version removes the bounded-gradient assumption, and a matching lower bound establishes optimality in this setting. Finally, for convex hitting costs on a bounded convex domain, we develop UDNP to aggregate any two experts in the lookahead setting under both switching costs. Its additional cumulative cost is at most $O(1)$ relative to the first expert and $O(\sqrt{T\log T})$ relative to the second, yielding best-of-both-worlds guarantees without bounded gradients.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal health data integrate information from heterogeneous clinical sources, creating substantial opportunities for data-driven decision support. Realizing these opportunities, however, requires effectively modeling complex dependencies that arise both within individual modalities and across modalities. This paper proposes Relational Tensor Representation (RTR), a unified framework that jointly learns dimension-specific tensor representations and cross-dimensional and cross-modality relationships. RTR introduces learnable relational bridge matrices that maintain relational information while preserving the structural identities of tensor dimensions and modalities. A structured fusion mechanism further integrates factor-level and relationship-level information into compact representations for downstream prediction. Experiments on simulated data and two multimodal neurophysiological datasets, covering emotion recognition and brain activity classification, demonstrate that RTR consistently outperforms representative tensor learning, multimodal fusion, and Transformer-based baselines. These results highlight the value of explicitly modeling cross-dimensional and cross-modality relationships for learning informative representations from heterogeneous multimodal tensor data. The source code is planned for public release to support reproducibility and future research.

Abstract: Electrocardiogram (ECG) beat classifiers lose accuracy on new patients and devices, and source-free adaptation methods that assume class-balanced target batches fail on real recordings, whose label-free prefix is 87.9--98.6% normal beats. We propose Normal-Anchored Geometric Transport (NAGT, GA_NS), which estimates a closed-form symmetric positive-definite congruence map from label-free normal anchors in the first 5 min of each recording and applies it only to the normal and supraventricular prototypes, keeping the ventricular and fusion prototypes fixed. The score offsets and decision rules were set on MIT-BIH only; the rate-invariant RR head (relA) was adopted after the development databases (INCART, SVDB) had been opened; the frozen model was then evaluated once on Icentia11k chest-patch recordings under a pre-registered protocol. In 493 patients (973 recordings, 3,615,388 evaluated beats), NAGT reached an arrhythmia macro-$F_1$ $J = \frac{1}{2}(\text{S-}F_1 + \text{V-}F_1) = 48.7\%$ [95% CI 42.1, 54.7] versus 34.5% without adaptation ($ΔJ = +14.1$ [+8.6, +19.3] pp; 59.8% versus 43.9% in non-AF) and outperformed all five Holm-adjusted comparators, including AdaBN, Tent and T3A. In the 75-patient test subgroup of the historical split (73 analyzed), the gain was not significant (+6.1 [-8.5, +23.4] pp). Adaptation takes a median of 5.8 ms per record without back-propagation.

Abstract: Transformers have emerged as a dominant architecture in modern machine learning, with in-context learning (ICL) enabling models to adapt to new tasks by conditioning on prompts, without any parameter updates. To understand the attention mechanism in transformer blocks, a growing line of theoretical work interprets ICL through the lens of classical statistical estimators. Recent works have established a connection between the attention mechanism and kernel methods, while the kernel function is usually assumed to be fixed. In practice, the query and key matrices in the attention mechanism are trained, which allows the attention mechanism to serve as an adaptive kernel method. In this work, we demonstrate that transformers can act as adaptive anisotropic kernel learners. Specifically, we study ICL for low-rank linear and multi-index nonlinear regressions, and conduct numerical simulations to show that the learned attention scores closely resemble the oracle kernels for these function classes. We then develop a general framework to analyze the generalization error of ICL by connecting transformers to the oracle kernel estimator for each function class. Our theory gives an explicit and constructive implementation of the oracle kernel estimators for these function classes using a wide and shallow transformer architecture, illustrating how the prediction is carried out by the interaction between the query token and the context tokens. Our work complements existing studies by providing numerical and theoretical evidence of how the attention mechanism adapts to anisotropic kernels in both linear and nonlinear settings.

</details>

First, the guardrail's own training corpus already contained the benchmarks: JailbreakBench-benign is 88% and OR-Bench-hard 82% present in the training pools, two over-refusal benchmarks, the kind a guardrail reports to argue it does not over-block. Everything below is decontaminated. Second, safety benchmarks stratify sharply by how much of their score is decided by the label definition rather than by the model: with architecture and optimiser fixed, the range across the ten arms is 0.99 on HarmBench-copyright and 0.043 on WildGuard-benign, a 23x spread. Third, a benchmark score can be moved across its entire range by 0.03% of the parameters. Across these arms HarmBench-copyright spans 0-100 and OR-Bench-hard over-refusal 3.4-56.4%, and the four arms whose last supervision signal carries a given policy occupy one extreme of both (exact rank p=0.005, or 0.04-0.14 at worst), at unchanged detection capability. For a distilled student the teacher's soft labels do this even when every hard label carries the other policy, so teacher provenance is an unrecorded confound in any released checkpoint. Fourth, validation accuracy on the one split that grades all twenty arms tracks agreement with the annotator's policy (rho=+0.74), not detection capability (rho=+0.34). We argue that guardrail evaluations should report contamination, a seed floor and the definition they were graded against as first-class variables.

Abstract: We consider the task of learning an (approximately) optimal policy for stochastic optimization problems where the true distributions are unknown. In each period, the algorithm plays a policy for the stochastic problem and observes semi-bandit feedback, i.e., it only receives new samples from the random variables that were selected by the policy. We obtain an algorithm that works for a large class of ``monotone'' stochastic problems, including prophet inequality, Pandora's box, series testing, stochastic knapsack and stochastic matching. We obtain $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{nkT})$ regret for ``weakly'' monotone problems and $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{nT})$ regret for ``strongly'' monotone problems, where $T$ is the number of periods, $n$ is the problem dimension (number of random variables) and $k$ is the maximum support size of any random variable. This improves over previous regret bounds by a $\sqrt{n}$ factor. In fact, our dependence on $n$ and $T$ is the best-possible due the $Ω(\sqrt{nT})$ lower bound for multi-armed-bandits. Our main technique is the use of Hellinger distance in bounding the ``error'' between the true and learned distributions, and the use of a constant-factor chain rule for the Hellinger distance of dependent random variables.

</details>

Abstract: Reverse target fishing identifies protein targets for a query molecule and is central to drug discovery and repurposing. Discriminative approaches adapted from virtual screening face sparse, long-tailed associations and missing-not-at-random measurements: treating unrecorded pairs as negatives can suppress genuine targets. We reformulate the task as generative retrieval and introduce GenFish. Residual vector quantization converts frozen protein language model features into shared semantic identifiers. Given a molecular fingerprint, GenFish autoregressively models codes conditioned on the query and preceding code, ranking proteins by identifier likelihood without explicitly labeling unrecorded pairs as negatives. We introduce ProTargetBench, the first proteome-scale benchmark for reverse target fishing to jointly support measured-panel discrimination and full-catalogue recovery of experimentally recorded positives. Covering 20,431 UniProt-reviewed human proteins, it integrates curated bioactivity evidence and distinguishes experimental inactivity from unmeasured associations. On 198 held-out molecules, GenFish achieves higher mean performance across all three full-catalogue retrieval metrics than four discriminative neural baselines and three non-neural baselines, including ligand-based methods. A controlled positive-hiding experiment shows that targeted removal of false-negative competition improves contrastive retrieval beyond matched random masking; GenFish remains stronger under the same incomplete supervision. Pfam alignment indicates that the semantic identifiers capture biologically meaningful relationships among proteins. These results establish generative retrieval as a principled paradigm for reverse target fishing under incomplete bioactivity records.

</details>

Abstract: Large, unlabeled datasets of prior experience have become increasingly available for agentic tasks in domains such as robotics and coding. A common recipe for these tasks is to train a policy using the prior data, and then further improve it with online rollouts. But while much work has studied how to use both the offline and online data for policy extraction, far less work has been done to understand how the prior data can be used to efficiently \textit{explore} during the online learning phase. Previous work has attempted to formalize this idea by defining the \textit{empowerment}, a measure of how many future states an agent can realize; states with higher empowerment require more exploration as there are more futures to explore. However, existing methods for estimating empowerment require online policy rollouts in the environment. We propose the first method for estimating empowerment from unlabeled prior data, without any environment interaction. By jointly learning an empowerment-maximizing policy together with its discounted state-occupancy measure, our estimator can be trained on unlabeled trajectories generated from any unknown policy. We demonstrate that our empowerment estimator qualitatively gives intuitive empowerment measures and show that the pretrained estimator can be used to improve exploration in the online goal-conditioned reinforcement learning setting. Code is available at \url{https://github.com/ishirgarg/offline-empowerment}.

</details>

Abstract: Many real-world tasks require optimizing expensive black-box functions accessible only through noisy evaluations, a setting commonly addressed with Bayesian optimization. While Gaussian processes(GPs) and Bayesian neural networks have driven significant progress, GP-based methods scale cubically with observations, and BNN-based approaches remain burdened by expensive posterior sampling such as Hamiltonian Monte Carlo, and inner-loop acquisition optimization in high-dimensional setting. In this work, we propose VBO-MI, a fully gradient-based BO framework that requires no assumptions on a Gaussian prior or a variational posterior family over $f$. While our framework uses gradient-based updates to train the internal action and information-estimation networks, it treats the objective function $f$ as a strict black box, requiring only zeroth-order evaluations. To enable end-to-end gradient flow, we employ an actor-critic architecture consisting of an action-net to navigate the input space and a variational critic to estimate a surrogate information signal for exploration. Additionally, we design a lightweight surrogate that approximates the objective from observed data using a replay buffer, which reduces the total required real function queries to one batch per iteration. We provide theoretical analysis for our framework, establishing convergence of its training objective and surrogate estimator, and showing, that near-optimal solutions exist. We evaluate our method on a diverse suite of benchmarks, including high-dimensional synthetic function and complex real-world tasks such as Rover Trajectory planning optimization, the Lunar Lander, and Pest Control problems. Our experiments demonstrate that VBO-MI provides the same or superior optimization performance over the various baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Long-context large language model (LLM) inference is important for modern intelligent applications but remains constrained by peak memory during prompt prefill. Existing KV cache compression reduces decoding memory but typically requires full-prompt prefill to preserve global context, retaining a high construction peak. Online chunk-level eviction lowers memory earlier but selects before each layer has observed the complete prompt, potentially degrading cache construction and decoding. We present TideKV, an on-GPU cache-construction architecture using layer-major delayed eviction. TideKV processes all prompt chunks within each Transformer layer, retains full-prompt KV only for the active layer, and evicts at layer end. This schedule preserves full-prompt-aware selection while reducing activation and temporary memory. Experiments across multiple computing platforms show that TideKV preserves the inference quality and final cache size of the evaluated compression methods. At 64K on A100, it reduces the construction-time transient footprint by 83.89%. On RTX 4090, it increases time to first token by only 1.06% at 64K and enables 128K prefill. TideKV also completes 128K on Jetson, whereas matched post-prefill compression runs out of memory on both resource-constrained platforms. These results show that delayed layer-end eviction substantially expands feasible context lengths without sacrificing cache utility.

</details>

Abstract: Irregular time series require models to answer temporal queries at arbitrary coordinates, yet existing approaches typically retain observation-dependent representations that must be repeatedly resolved for each query. We introduce HiPPO Functional Memory (HiPPO-FM), a functional-memory attention operator that compiles an irregular history into a reusable coordinate-addressable state. Instead of storing observation-indexed tokens or latent trajectories, HiPPO-FM writes observations into a fixed-rank functional space and performs direct coordinate evaluation through matched functional geometry. Kernel-smoothed HiPPO coordinates and Gram-based readout define a shared write/read mechanism, while observed-support self-attention enables content-dependent representations before compression. We characterize the induced finite-dimensional query space and derive an error decomposition separating functional geometry approximation from learned-memory mismatch. Across continuous-coordinate reconstruction, irregular representation learning, real-world extrapolation, and event-sequence prediction, HiPPO-FM consistently improves strong irregular time-series baselines, achieving strong reconstruction accuracy on Spiral, competitive performance across 20 UEA datasets, improved real-world extrapolation, and superior results on several irregular event benchmarks. After encoding, the compact functional state can be reused across different query sets without reprocessing the observation history.

Abstract: Concept erasure removes harmful, private, or copyrighted concepts from text-to-image models while preserving their other abilities. Leading models such as FLUX and Stable Diffusion 3 are trained with flow matching, and erasure methods for them fine-tune the model toward a guided teacher's velocities under a Euclidean loss. Yet the squared distance is only one member of a larger family: Bregman divergences are exactly the losses under which flow matching recovers the correct velocity field, and each member shapes differently what is erased and what is preserved. We introduce BEM (Bregman erasure matching), which is, to our knowledge, the first to learn this divergence through bi-level optimization, so that the fine-tuned model forgets the concept on unseen prompts while its unrelated generations stay unchanged. In the experiments we target the suppression of nudity, violence, real identities, and copyrighted characters. Under the evaluation protocol of GEM, BEM removes more of the concept than GEM on most settings, cutting the unsafe rate on bloody gore from 53.9% to 14.4% and the FID on nudity from 8.64 to 6.64, while retention holds on some concepts and degrades on others.

Abstract: Conformal prediction (CP) is a post-hoc calibration method that returns prediction sets with distribution-free coverage guarantees, and the size of the output set is often interpreted as a notion of uncertainty. We show that the difference in set size between full and split CP is directly connected to Bayesian posterior variance, which is the epistemic uncertainty of the model. Essentially, this difference in set size is an aggregation of two opposite effects: (1) better model accuracy and therefore smaller set size achieved by training over extra calibration points, and (2) the "formability" of the model at test point towards every possible label. The latter effect vanishes with stronger models or more calibration data (as epistemic uncertainty is known for that behavior). It turns out that the model's formability itself (without the quantization effect of prediction sets, and through estimating the posterior variance) is a useful tool to quantify the epistemic uncertainty. In our empirical evaluation it performs on par with the state of the art in many setups.

Abstract: Molecular generation designs novel molecules with desired structures and properties. Global count constraints specify molecular size, elemental composition, and ring content without prescribing detailed structure. Property-conditioned generators steer generation toward such targets but do not guarantee exact satisfaction. We introduce TopoCompile, which combines an autoregressive component-tree generator with a dynamic-programming compiler for supported component-additive integer counts. TopoCompile represents molecules as trees of intact ring systems and individual atoms, making each supported count additive over components. At each step, an autoregressive Transformer scores component and attachment actions, while the compiler masks actions with no target-satisfying completion. Within the fixed component vocabulary and typed attachment rules, constrained decoding guarantees exact equality for supported non-negative, component-additive integer counts. Applied at inference time, these checks let the same trained generator support different targets and count combinations without retraining. Across four benchmarks, TopoCompile achieves 100% Exact-valid yield for Common and Rare four-count target groups; the strongest baseline within each dataset reaches 44.9% or less on Rare targets. In unconditional generation, it achieves the highest validity or valid-unique-novel (V.U.N.) among the compared methods across all four benchmark datasets.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards assigns one terminal reward to an entire reasoning trajectory, obscuring how individual reasoning steps contributed to the outcome. GRPO broadcasts a group-normalized advantage to every response token, while finer-grained approaches often rely on dense auxiliary rollouts or branching schemes that can also alter the data used for policy updates. We cast the missing middle as budgeted measurement: with a limited rollout budget, which partial solutions should be evaluated, and how should their value estimates refine credit? ProbePO selects partial-solution states with a reward-free uncertainty score and estimates their expected verifier rewards under a capped continuation budget. ProbePO redistributes, rather than replaces, GRPO's trajectory-level advantage across tokens while preserving each trajectory's mean token-level advantage; probe continuations serve only as measurements. Across three DeepSeekMath and Qwen-family models spanning 1.5B-7B parameters, ProbePO consistently outperforms GRPO and achieves the highest accuracy among the four evaluated methods in 16 of 18 model-benchmark comparisons. Against dense-probing VinePPO on DeepSeekMath-7B, ProbePO achieves higher accuracy on all six benchmarks with 76% less end-to-end training time.

Abstract: Reliable long-horizon prediction remains a central challenge for embodied world models. Although recent generative models can synthesize visually plausible action-conditioned futures, their latent dynamics are typically learned through unconstrained transition modules, which may gradually deviate from coherent physical evolution as predictions are recursively rolled forward. We investigate whether explicitly structuring latent state evolution can improve the stability and utility of such imagined trajectories. To this end, we introduce HWM, a structured dynamics framework that represents backbone latents in a learned phase space consisting of generalized coordinates and momenta. Future states are propagated through dissipative Hamiltonian dynamics and mapped back to the original latent representation through a learnable inverse interface. The resulting formulation can be integrated into existing world-model architectures without redesigning their perception or decoding components, while multi-step transition supervision encourages the structured rollout to remain aligned with the latent trajectories induced by real observations. Controlled simulation experiments show that HWM produces more accurate estimates of velocity, acceleration, and jerk and substantially reduces long-horizon energy drift relative to unstructured latent dynamics. We further evaluate the approach across three distinct embodied world-model settings, covering single-arm multi-view prediction with Ctrl-World, dual-arm single-view prediction with EVAC, and task-oriented single-arm prediction with Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B. On RoboCasa, incorporating HWM into Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B yields a 2.7% relative improvement in task success. On real-world AgiBot World trajectories, it improves nDTW by 9.7% with Ctrl-World and Logic by 8.4% with EVAC.

</details>

Abstract: Symbolic regression (SR) solvers employ diverse search mechanisms, yet most rely on the same low-information scalar fitness. Such scores provide little guidance on why a skeleton fails or how it should be revised. Recent methods develop new methods to address this bottleneck. Their enhanced feedback mechanisms are tightly coupled to their search processes and are difficult to reuse across diverse SR solvers. Instead, we ask whether a shared feedback mechanism can further improve diverse solvers facing the same bottleneck. To this end, we propose PostDiag, a search-independent feedback mechanism, which can be shared to different SR solvers. PostDiag introduces Patch-wise Feature Profiling (PFP) to capture local behavior through patch-wise features of level, trend, and roughness. Based on PFP, PostDiag quantifies Skeleton Expressiveness Risk by comparing the fitted candidate with the observations, and Skeleton Stability Risk by measuring variability of behavior after bootstrap refitting. The Diagnostic Risk Plane (DRP) combines two risks to determine whether a skeleton should be preserved, repaired, simplified, or rewritten, while the patch-wise discrepancies provide evidence for the intervention. An LLM editor then performs the diagnosis-guided intervention, and the validated revision is returned to the original solver through a lightweight adapter. Experiments across multiple LLM-based and GP-based SR solvers show that PostDiag consistently improves accuracy and accelerates convergence.

</details>

Abstract: Federated learning has recently emerged as an important paradigm for large-scale distributed model training, which can be naturally formulated as a class of distributed learning problems. The alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) is a widely used approach for solving such problems. However, its practical performance is often hindered by significant communication overhead and system heterogeneity. In this paper, we propose a multi-step inertial ADMM algorithm, called MiFedADMM, for federated learning. The proposed method leverages multiple historical iterates on the server side to construct a higher-order extrapolation after each aggregation to accelerate the convergence behavior. Under mild assumptions, we establish convergence results for the single-step variant of the proposed method and derive an O(1/K) convergence rate. Numerical experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness and improved performance of the proposed method compared with existing approaches.

</details>

Abstract: Instance-dependent early stopping (IES) reduces training computation by skipping backpropagation for examples whose losses have stabilized, but the second-order loss-difference criterion used by IES lacks a theoretical justification. We develop Theory-Guided Instance-Dependent Early Stopping (TG-IES), which analyzes this criterion under explicit assumptions and derives a noise-calibrated mastery threshold and a conditional stability bound for instance exclusion. We show that second-order differencing has a favorable bias-variance trade-off over first- and third-order differences in a specific signal-to-noise regime, and that its signal contains both gradient-drift and curvature terms. TG-IES derives its stopping threshold and patience from training-run statistics rather than using the manually selected threshold of IES. Across CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, TG-IES saves 53-58% of backpropagation instances in its main convolutional settings while keeping accuracy within 0.1-0.9 percentage points of full training; in the primary ResNet-18/CIFAR-10 setting, this corresponds to 35.7% FLOP-normalized savings. The method also transfers to ViT-Tiny, where it achieves smaller savings and incurs a modest accuracy cost. These results provide a theoretical basis for second-order instance-dependent early stopping and show that theory-guided calibration can substantially reduce training computation under the assumptions and experimental settings studied.

</details>

Abstract: Market makers keep trading efficient by setting bid and ask prices for buyers and sellers. In sequential market making, trades happen independently based on price thresholds, requiring the market maker to maintain balanced, low-risk inventory over T rounds. This paper studies how to maximize revenue with unknown buyer and seller valuations using regret minimization. When the market maker only sees trade success or failure, an algorithm achieves regret and inventory bounds proportional to T to the power of 2/3. When full order book data is available for failed trades, regret and inventory limits improve significantly to the square root of T. We also establish a fundamental trade-off via a lower bound, by proving that tighter round-by-round inventory limits strictly force higher minimum regret.
