Abstract: Recent work has shown that deep neural networks exhibit an implicit low-rank simplicity bias. In this paper, we investigate the questions that naturally arise from this observation. What are the temporal consequences of low-rank simplicity bias during optimization? How does representation geometry evolve throughout optimization rather than only at convergence? Do different neural architectures exhibit distinct geometric optimization behaviors? To answer these questions, we conduct a large-scale empirical study tracking representational geometry throughout training. We find that neural networks follow three recurring geometric trajectories: persistent high-rank, collapse-and-recovery, and persistent low-rank. We further identify Destructive Representation Collapse (DRC) as a pathological regime in which representations collapse to rank-one and fail to recover, leading to a sustained loss of representational capacity. While collapse-and-recovery is consistently associated with successful optimization in controlled settings, experiments on real-world models show that the relationship between geometric trajectories and optimization outcomes is configuration dependent. Overall, our findings suggest that representation geometry evolves dynamically during training and provide a new perspective on how deep neural networks learn.

</details>

Abstract: Delirium affects a large fraction of intensive care unit (ICU) patients and foreshadows longer stays, lasting cognitive decline, and higher mortality. Flagging the patients who will become delirious within the first day of admission would give clinicians a window to intervene. Yet state-of-the-art predictors rely only on the structured electronic health record (EHR) of vital signs, laboratory values, and medications, while overlooking the free-text clinical notes written in the same window, which carry information the numbers cannot encode. To this end, we present DeliMF, a multimodal framework that fuses the structured time series with clinical text. It encodes the structured signals with a temporal Transformer and the time-stamped radiology reports with a clinical text encoder, and fuses the two through a gated cross-attention module. The gate learns a per-patient weight on the text; when a patient has no note the gate closes and DeliMF reduces to its structured-only form, giving robustness to missing text by construction. On an incident-only MIMIC-IV cohort, DeliMF improves significantly over the baselines, particularly on patients who carry clinical text, while losing nothing on those without. Ablation studies confirm the contribution of multimodal fusion and gated cross-attention, and a case study further shows that fusing clinical text with the structured stream recovers delirium cases that the structured data alone misses. We hope DeliMF encourages early ICU prediction to make fuller use of the overlooked clinical text and contributes to the early identification and timely intervention of delirium.

</details>

Abstract: Adam is widely used for training large language models (LLMs) owing to its strong optimization performance and training stability, but its element-wise estimation for the first and second moments incur substantial memory overhead. Although numerous memory-efficient optimizers have been proposed, many incur additional computational overhead or require additional tuning of hyperparameters. Consequently, Adam remains a dominant and reliable choice for LLM pre-training. We propose AdamPM (Adam with Pooled Moments), a simple and memory-efficient variant of Adam that groups adjacent moment entries along the row or column dimension and represents each group by a single shared mean. AdamPM can well inherit Adam's hyperparameter settings and is readily integrated into existing Adam optimizer implementations, with less memory overhead. We systematically investigate pooling of both first and second moment estimates and identify second moment pooling as the effective design. With a group size of $s$, AdamPM reduces second moment memory to $1/s$ of the original requirement. Extensive experiments on language model pretraining, fine-tuning and multimodal learning demonstrate that AdamPM maintains model performance and training speed while substantially reducing optimizer-state memory. Our code is available at \url{https://github.com/jixiaowei95/AdamPM}.

</details>

Abstract: Traditional Chinese medicine (TCM) research connects classical texts, medicinal entities, molecular assays, computational predictions, and clinical studies. An autonomous research agent must preserve the inferential boundaries between these evidence types while coordinating analysis across heterogeneous resources. We introduce TCMScience, a scientific knowledge-discovery agent that separates language-model proposals from execution and release authority. Its architecture combines a trusted kernel, a scientific compiler, and claim-support contracts: plans are compiled into typed operations, execution is bounded by explicit permissions, and candidate findings are checked against source provenance, study design, and claim scope before release. The full capability registry contains 117 public-source connectors with 444 typed operations across 29 domains and 150 native tools across 12 domains; the TCM data hub catalogs 136 databases, 76 dataset specifications, and 84,294 formula-source records. These resources support model-independent workflows through browser and local execution interfaces. In a network-pharmacology analysis of Gegen Qinlian Tang, replacing the whole-proteome background with the assayed-protein background changed the number of significant pathways from 66 to zero; incorporating inactive screening records identified nine pathways, including drug-metabolism signals retained as mechanistic hypotheses. A governance ablation over 121 constructed erroneous outputs spanning 26 classes released 121 errors without governance, 57 with the domain layer, seven with the kernel, and zero with both layers. All 10 compliant controls were retained. These results establish complementary control of known failure modes and show how explicit evidence contracts change the conclusions an agent is permitted to report.

</details>

Abstract: One powerful way to safeguard AI systems is to monitor their internal computations--where signs of undesirable behavior might be more easily detectable than from models' inputs and outputs alone. However, recent studies begin to question the premise that activations provide a unique window into model behavior. In this paper, we study how much of activation monitors' success depends on access to the original behavior-generating computations. Within a fixed choice of target model and monitoring architecture, we compare how well monitors trained and evaluated on trajectories that differ in computational context can predict the behavior of the original execution, after the transcript is changed in a controlled manner (e.g., its exact text, or chat formatting). We first find that much performance can be recovered without access to the original behavior-generating trace (in up to 4 models across 2 counterfactual monitoring tasks), but a mild yet consistent advantage to native-trained monitors remains, outperforming strongest controls by up to 0.014 AUROC. Interestingly, we find monitors trained with native access have notable uplift on original examples whose behavioral label changes under paraphrasing. We further find this a useful lens to expose limitations of baselines that use simple forms of privileged information (i.e., answer logit margins). While our results show the advantage of native monitor access is modest in aggregate, they highlight the importance of evaluations that identify if and when original model computations provide additional predictive value.

Abstract: While hierarchical structures are intrinsic to organizing human knowledge, current representation learning on text-attributed graphs predominantly operates on flat semantic spaces, overlooking the rich coarse-to-fine granularity inherent in real-world data. To bridge this gap, we propose SHiFT, which synergizes the reasoning power of LLMs with the structural representation capability of GNNs within an Expectation-Maximization (EM) paradigm. In the E-step, we harness LLMs as expert taxonomists to induce an explicit semantic tree, introducing a lightweight Mapping & Diagnosis strategy that reduces inference costs by surgically updating the hierarchy only when anomalies are detected. In the M-step, we align the GNN encoder with this induced hierarchy from complementary perspectives, including clustering partition, topological skeleton, and semantic concepts. Theoretical analysis interprets SHiFT as a generalized Variational EM algorithm. Abstract: Recent works show that LLMs can generate kernels with strong performance on server GPUs. Edge deployment presents distinct challenges because the hardware is heterogeneous, the software stacks are fragmented, and the resource budgets are tight. LLM kernel generation across diverse edge backends has yet to be studied systematically. We introduce EdgeKernelBench, which combines a unified IR deployment framework with a hierarchical benchmark for edge workloads. The unified IR captures reusable computation, scheduling, and memory decisions and connects to each target backend through a compiler interface. The benchmark spans progressively larger program scopes, from individual operators to compact model graphs, across representative edge application domains. We instantiate the design with TVM as a reference compiler and validate it across heterogeneous edge backends. With a single generation attempt, the best model achieves 64.5% Correct@1 but only 25.5% Fast@1.0. This gap shows that functional correctness does not consistently translate into performance gains. We therefore introduce the Layered Edge Kernel Agent (LEKA), which organizes optimization through a layered IR. On the agent evaluation subset, LEKA achieves 43.5% Fast@1.0, compared with 13.0% for direct generation and 26.1% for Claude Code. LEKA and Claude Code use the same base model and optimization budget. Together, EdgeKernelBench and LEKA support unified evaluation and layered optimization of kernels generated by LLMs across edge backends.

</details>

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are effective for modeling relational data but are vulnerable to label poisoning, where a small number of corrupted training labels can propagate errors across the graph via message passing. Despite this risk, defenses against label poisoning remain underexplored: existing methods are primarily designed for label noise and often rely on robust losses or heuristic data cleaning that fail to distinguish adversarial poisoning from naturally hard examples. In this paper, we propose CoLAT (Contrastive Label-flipping for Adversarial Training), a novel contrastive adversarial training framework tailored for robust node classification on graphs. The core of our approach is a structure-aware detection model that uses contrastive learning to identify label-structure inconsistencies. Unlike prior contrastive methods that focus on representation learning, CoLAT leverages contrastive embeddings to select high-risk nodes that guide adversarial label-flipping during training. This alternating optimization not only performs structure-aware adversarial training but also implicitly sanitizes some corrupted labels, improving robustness against diverse attacks from the literature. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks show that CoLAT consistently outperforms existing defenses under various poisoning intensities while scaling efficiently to large graphs.

</details>

Abstract: Datamodels predict how a model's behavior changes when different parts of its training data are included or removed. However, linear datamodels assign fixed contributions to training units and cannot represent non-additive joint effects. We introduce Tensor Network Datamodels (TNDMs), which use low-rank tensor networks to model dependencies among training units while keeping the representation compact. TNDMs learn a map from subsets of the training data to the outcome of training on those subsets, allowing them to represent interactions among several training units. We prove that complementary and redundant training groups can induce high-degree subset functions with compact low-rank representations. A single fitted TNDM can then be used for several training data questions, including predicting the effect of removing data, measuring the importance of individual training units, identifying interactions between training units, and selecting useful subsets of the training data. We evaluate TNDMs on controlled tasks designed to contain strong interactions, as well as standard fine-tuning settings for classification and reasoning. On a controlled reasoning task, TNDMs outperform every baseline while observing only half as many training subsets, and TNDM-TT reduces prediction NMSE by $89\%$ relative to the linear datamodel after observing approximately $0.0061\%$ of all possible training subsets. In factual-association fine-tuning, a single TNDM-CP raises held-out $\mathrm{R}^2$ from $0.579$ to $0.668$ over the linear datamodel. These results demonstrate that compact low-rank models improve prediction when training data effects depend on combinations of training groups.

</details>

Abstract: Spreadsheets are among the most widely used tools for computations, and formulas are their core building blocks. Besides agentic spreadsheet tools, proactive formula suggestions enable significant productivity gains and are now available in popular spreadsheet software (Microsoft Excel and Google Sheets). Such suggestions pose interesting challenges: the right context must be collected and represented for a machine learning model, which must then be able to write a correct formula, in a way that is fast and cheap enough to trigger on every = (or subsequent keystrokes for auto-completion) without taking users out of their editing flow. Surprisingly, there is no public benchmark for formula suggestions on real spreadsheets. In this paper, we therefore (1) introduce fxBench as a manually curated benchmark of 503 formula suggestion tasks based on Sheetpedia, (2) break down the formula suggestion problem into four capabilities (gather context, understand context, understand intent, write formula) that a formula suggestion system requires, and (3) evaluate existing and new approaches to understand bottlenecks in these capabilities that pose interesting research directions. Specifically, we annotate each benchmark with the cells that describe intent to determine the theoretical performance limit of a given context selection strategy, we evaluate performance of small and large models on different representations of context, including a dense format with high theoretical coverage at low token counts, and we fine-tune smaller language models to show that we can learn to understand such dense formats. Additionally, we perform quantitative and qualitative analysis on failure modes to understand bottlenecks in the formula writing capability; the best configuration only achieved 58.8% correct suggestions.

Abstract: Training deep neural networks requires optimizers that balance computational efficiency with formal data privacy. While orthogonalized update rules, such as the recently introduced Muon optimizer, have shown strong empirical performance and scalability, their behavior under Differential Privacy (DP) is not yet understood. In this paper, we propose DP-Muon, a differentially private variant of the Muon optimizer. By analyzing Muon's orthogonalized updates through the lens of a Linear Minimization Oracle (LMO), we show that the leading effect of per-example clipping is a positive rescaling of the gradient, to which the LMO is blind, so that only a small directional residual controlled by the centered gradient variance enters the optimization error. This yields an $\mathcal O(K^{-1/4})$ convergence rate, up to logarithmic factors, to a clipping-dependent neighborhood at a fixed noise scale and, with noise calibrated to a fixed $(\varepsilon,δ)$ budget, the standard $(\sqrt d/(n\varepsilon))^{1/2}$ privacy--utility bound of DP-SGD, without assuming bounded gradients. Furthermore, we investigate the hyperparameter landscape of DP-Muon and document how the optimal learning rate, momentum, and batch size change under privacy. Empirically, on image classification DP-Muon matches or improves on DP-SGD and DP-Adam, whereas on language modeling the best optimizer depends on the batch size, with DP-Muon best at small batches and DP-Adam best near the privacy-optimal batch size.

Abstract: Bayesian optimization (BO) is widely used for expensive black-box optimization, yet observation noise makes each evaluation less informative and slows posterior learning. Standard BO learns from costly outcomes gathered during optimization. In many applications, however, low-cost auxiliary data are available beforehand. We introduce Auxiliary-Informed Gaussian Process Bandit (AI-GPB), a variance-reduction method that combines an offline estimate of the auxiliary mean with a correction coefficient learned from paired online observations. Notably, it exploits correlation between paired observation errors without requiring the auxiliary mean to approximate the objective. Abstract: Genetic programming symbolic regression (GPSR) often returns expressions that are larger than necessary, with dormant branches and finely tuned algebraic configurations that fit sampling noise. Existing complexity-control strategies, such as multi-objective size-accuracy search or information criteria embedded in the fitness function, act on syntactic representations during model search. We introduce Fisher-Geometric pruning (FGP), a post-hoc model-reduction framework that acts on fitted models and can complement these techniques.
  FGP combines local information geometry at the maximum-likelihood solution with explicit compatibility tests for interpretable parametric boundaries. A taxonomy of parametric directions separates local sensitivity from proximity to reducible boundaries, enabling the treatment of unidentifiable directions and reductions through zero constraints, asymptotic limits, and signed parameter identifications. Candidate reductions are realised by symbolic contraction and refitting, then accepted only when their cross-validated predictive risk remains within a prescribed tolerance.
  We evaluate FGP on controlled diagnostic problems and Pareto fronts generated by GPSR. Across both settings, FGP substantially reduces structural complexity while preserving, and often improving, out-of-sample accuracy. Comparisons with competing pruning strategies and ablations show that, by combining geometric screening, boundary compatibility, and predictive validation, FGP yields the most consistent joint gains in parsimony and generalisation.

Abstract: Torque-limited spacecraft attitude capture requires adapting the maneuver speed to uncertain braking authority. Abstract: On-device fine-tuning lets a large language model (LLM) learn from its user's private data without uploading it. Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) reduces the trainable parameters, but each first-order training step still multiplies by the frozen base-model weights during the forward pass, activation recomputation, and input-gradient computation. A phone's neural processing unit (NPU) already holds these weights for inference; using it for training also requires computing input gradients, which its inference interface does not support. We present a system that runs all three frozen-weight products on the NPU while keeping the training graph, adapters, and optimizer on the CPU. Its input-gradient kernels reuse the resident weight tiles and the forward dequantizer, avoiding a transposed weight copy and ensuring forward and backward use the same effective weights. To preserve usable gradients, the system selects precision by gradient sensitivity and applies a per-row range guard for half-precision (FP16) outputs. Its runtime fuses the remaining CPU operators and overlaps NPU execution with adapter computation, while resumable execution and thermal pacing let training proceed as a background job. We evaluate it on two Qualcomm Snapdragon platforms using four LLMs from the Qwen and Llama families. Our workloads cover nightly personalization with labels supplied on the phone and the public LaMP-2 benchmark; comparisons include an optimized CPU trainer, a phone-GPU trainer, forward-only training, and GPU-trained references.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are vulnerable to adversarial inputs that induce attacker-specified malicious behaviors, commonly referred to as jailbreaking. Existing optimization-based jailbreak attacks primarily employ first-order (FO) methods that leverage gradient information or zeroth-order (ZO) methods that rely on function-value feedback. However, their complementarity and optimization ordering remain underexplored. We systematically investigate four strategies: pure ZO, pure FO, ZO->FO, and FO->ZO, and develop a ZO-FO framework that explores adversarial prefixes through ZO optimization and refines their corresponding suffixes through FO optimization. To coordinate the two processes, we introduce Pair-aware optimization, which retains multiple prefix candidates, conditionally refines their suffixes and selects among the resulting prefix-suffix pairs. We further investigate agent jailbreaking from a more general stochastic-generation perspective, where the same adversarial input can induce diverse reasoning trajectories and actions that a single greedy output cannot capture. Accordingly, we adopt consistent stochastic decoding during both optimization and evaluation. Experiments across malicious-instruction and malicious-environment benchmarks demonstrate that hybrid optimization improves attack effectiveness over single-optimizer methods, while optimization order substantially influences performance.

</details>

Abstract: Most long-horizon forecasters emit the complete future in one pass. We ask whether a compact operator can instead be reused to emit contiguous future blocks, trading stored parameters for sequential computation. Recursive Functional Decomposition (RFD) factorizes the horizon mapping into three parameter-shared block predictions. The blocks occupy disjoint future intervals and are concatenated, not added as trend, seasonal, or residual components, and each emitted block joins the rolling context that conditions the next application. In controlled three-seed experiments, removing forward feedback hurts all four evaluated datasets while stopping gradients through it changes little, so the useful signal is the evolving forecast state itself; reapplying the shared operator helps consistently, but by less than widening it. The model uses 0.08--0.46M parameters, yet its sequential channel-wise execution is slower and more memory-intensive than an iterative baseline at high channel count. In a frozen long-context evaluation against ten strong baseline families, RFD records lower test error in 23 of 24 cells; the comparison is cross-protocol, with RFD using 512/720-step context and the baselines using 96.

Abstract: We provide new statistical insights into designing stochastic optimization methods. By modeling the gradient via a normal distribution, we argue that the stochastic gradient should be whitened using its covariance. We give an online method for estimating the mean and variance of the gradients based on regularized likelihood or, equivalently, through a maximum a posteriori estimate. We then show how to use structured approximations to the covariance within this same framework, which includes diagonal and Kronecker-factored approximations. The diagonal approximation results in a method that is very closely related to Adam and performs as well in practice. The Kronecker approximation results in the new MN method, which is a variant of Shampoo and KL-Shampoo. We also recover Muon by applying this same statistical framework to the gradient direction rather than the full gradient. By combining this with a whitening step, we arrive at MN-Muon, a novel preconditioned version of Muon. Our experiments on GPT-2-type models at Chinchilla scale indicate that MND, the diagonal implementation of MN, outperforms both the Distributed Shampoo and the KL-Shampoo implementations in terms of stability, final validation loss and time taken. MND-Muon, the diagonal implementation of MN-Muon, improves on Muon in 21 of 24 paired GPT-small runs and achieves a lower best validation loss on GPT-medium, where it remains stable over the six tested learning rates. However, we found that NorMuon was comparable to MND-Muon and achieved a slightly lower validation loss on GPT-medium and at GPT-small batch 512 on FineWeb.

</details>

Abstract: Analytic continual learning (ACL) methods such as ACIL and GACL fit a ridge-regression classifier head on frozen features via the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury identity, achieving exemplar-free, exact updates. A separate line of work, Progressive Neural Collapse (ProNC), shows standard continual classifiers benefit from a progressively expanding Simplex Equiangular Tight Frame (ETF) target. It is natural to ask whether fusing the two ideas, exact analytic updates and ETF targets, would yield a better closed-form continual learner. We show this is not the case as the fusion turns out to be identical to plain one-hot ACIL. Further, Simplex ETFs are not nested across class counts, so the literal "progressively expand the ETF" construction stops yielding an ETF as new tasks arrive, and it introduces catastrophic forgetting ($4\times$ that of a classic ACIL head). We therefore separate an ETF's two defining properties, equiangularity and equinormality, and apply each on its own to the classifier weights rather than the targets, where the invariance above does not hold. Equinorm post-processing of the fitted weights recovers both tail-class and overall average accuracy across the long-tailed regime from 5:1 to 100:1 imbalance (e.g. +32.0 tail-accuracy points and +16.0 average-accuracy points at 100:1 on CIFAR-100/ViT), transferring in sign across two backbones and two datasets, at a mild cost of 5.4 average-accuracy points on CIFAR-100/ViT on near-balanced streams (below $\approx 3{:}1$). Equiangular geometry, by contrast, is exactly inert on the target side but actively harmful on the weight side, usually losing to plain one-hot. Hence, the generally useful part of Neural Collapse geometry in Analytic Continual Learning is equinorm post-processing of the weights, becoming more useful as class imbalance grows; and equiangularity is at best a no-op and induces catastrophic forgetting at worst.

</details>

Abstract: Heterogeneous optimization problems require an optimizer to decide not only which candidate solutions to evaluate, but also which objectives and constraints should be evaluated under different costs and fidelities. Existing SOTA methods such as MFE-NSGA-III address this setting with manually designed selection metrics, but their behavior is largely determined by predefined heuristic rules and hyperparameters. In this work, we propose REP-HO, a reasoning-based evaluation planner that replaces the rule-based mixed-fidelity selection step with a large language model (LLM) planner. The LLM receives the same primitive optimization-state information as the heuristic planner, such as surrogate predictions, uncertainties, evaluation costs, and budget status. We first show that REP-HO achieves competitive performance compared to the human-designed metric-based method (MFE-NSGA-III). We then further enhance each decision with an adaptive nearest-neighbor summary, leading to better and more stable results. We evaluate REP-HO across five heterogeneous regimes covering cost imbalance, constraint difficulty, surrogate fidelity, budget level, and objective conflict. Across these regimes, one of the two variants obtains the best mean ranking score in every regime, REP-HO(Deepseek) is first or second throughout, and the planner shows adaptive allocation behavior, such as prioritizing constraint evaluations under tighter budgets and reallocating constraint evaluation budget when surrogate reliability changes. These results suggest that LLMs can serve as flexible evaluation planners for heterogeneous optimization, offering a general alternative to fixed hand-designed metrics.

Abstract: Generative AI workflows execute user tasks by composing sequences of LLM calls, where each call performs a sub-task (e.g., summarization or translation), forming a workflow. Executing such workflows requires decisions over workflow structure, model assignments, and deployment configurations, which jointly determine latency, cost, and accuracy under dynamic workloads and resource constraints. Existing systems optimize these decisions in isolation or fix them early, leading to suboptimal cost and goodput. We make two key observations. First, efficient serving requires jointly exploring all combinations of workflows, model choices, and deployment configurations under dynamic cluster state. Second, reuse of deployed LLMs is critical for reducing cost, but must be performed on-the-fly, as reuse depends on current load and resource availability. Together, these make selecting a workflow-variant-a combination of workflow, models, and deployment configuration-a time-critical problem. We present FlowMaker, which exposes a task-level interface and selects workflow-variants on behalf of users. To realize this, we design a constraint-guided load-aware pruning algorithm for fast, on-the-fly decisions. FlowMaker improves goodput by up to 10.14$\times$ and reduces cost by up to 2.77$\times$ compared to state-of-the-art baselines, including Murakkab.

Abstract: Temporal graph neural networks (TGNNs) have shown strong performance on dynamic graph learning tasks by continuously updating node memories from evolving interactions (i.e., events) and leveraging these memories for downstream predictions. As graphs grow rapidly in modern applications, training requires processing an increasing number of events; meanwhile, each event can update node memories used by later events, creating dependencies that constrain parallel execution and limit the benefits of additional computing resources. These costs motivate an upstream question: Are all events, and the dependencies they introduce, necessary for learning? We investigate this question through two roles of temporal events: prediction targets and historical events sampled as inputs. We observe that targets involving stable node memories tend to exhibit lower prediction loss, while filtering historical events associated with stable memories can retain similar training-loss trajectories in several evaluated settings. Together, these observations suggest an opportunity to identify input redundancy through changes in node memories. Building on this opportunity, we propose RAFT, a redundancy-aware input-filtering framework for TGNN training. RAFT tracks memory changes during training and uses them to guide two filters: a root-event filter removes selected prediction targets before training, and a support-event filter excludes selected historical context during sampling. Filtering inputs before their downstream preparation and processing reduces the work they trigger. Across three TGNN models and five datasets, RAFT reduces training inputs by 35.23% on average and up to 97.11%, while reducing recorded training time by up to 52.56\% with negligible changes to model precisions.

</details>

Abstract: Reward fine-tuning is an effective way to adapt pretrained diffusion and flow-matching models to downstream objectives, but aggressive optimization often leads to mode collapse and distributional drift. Existing methods attempt to control this drift with reverse-KL regularization, implemented as an $\ell_2$ penalty on the velocity field; however, this functions by indirectly constraining sampling dynamics rather than directly acting on the generated distribution. To address this, we propose DFR (Distributional Functional Rewards), which augments the task reward with a distribution-dependent, per-sample reward that acts explicitly on the generated distribution. Formally, we generalize the KL-regularized objective from expected rewards, which are linear in the model's distribution, to convex functionals of it. The first variation of this objective defines an effective, distribution-dependent reward that directly penalizes deviation from the pretrained distribution. This formulation yields additive per-sample rewards for the Fréchet distance (FD), maximum mean discrepancy (MMD), and DreamSim diversity, recovering several recently proposed rewards as special cases. Across various text-to-image fine-tuning tasks, DFR matches the peak reward of KL-tuned baselines while increasing diversity by up to 29% at equal or lower drift.

Result: V正则化提升了相对与绝对的低尾响应，减少了响应不平衡，且对常规文本重构几乎无影响。JumpReLU和MDL目标保持与标准SAE相近。实验显示即使在未见家族的零样本评估中，V正则化依然提升了弱尾响应，证明特征基准已向安全相关变化倾斜。

Conclusion: V正则化的稀疏自编码器能将特征基准重新定位，使语言模型在安全关键任务中对语义差异输入更敏感，从而降低误判风险。

Abstract: Language models can treat semantically distinct inputs as interchangeable at the representation level, creating blind spots that standard sparse autoencoder (SAE) objectives do not target. In safety-critical settings such as clinical dosage extraction, legal clause interpretation, or financial amount verification, such failures can propagate silently into downstream decisions. We study this problem as one of feature-basis orientation. Building on a symmetry-matching view from algebraic error-detection theory, we add a differentiable $V_{\mathrm{Gini}}$ regularisation term to SAE training that penalises uneven perturbation response within a specified family. Across GPT-2, Gemma 2, and Qwen 2.5, a frozen multi-seed evaluation shows that $V$-regularisation raises both relative and absolute lower-tail responses while reducing response-profile imbalance, with no material general-text reconstruction degradation. JumpReLU and minimum description length (MDL) objectives remain close to the Standard SAE profile under the same protocol. Family-geometry controls show that the effect is not explained by template duplication or a single dominant perturbation family, and held-out probes plus null-calibrated separation tests evaluate how far the representation-level change carries to downstream discriminability. A zero-shot audit on out-of-distribution clinical radiology minimal pairs finds the relative weak-tail lift in all evaluated model-by-family cells, not perfectly rank-aligned with the trained statistic, and a held-out family audit finds it on family kinds never used in training. Together, the results show that perturbation-aware SAE training can reorient feature bases toward safety-relevant input changes while leaving downstream validation as a separate empirical step.

This testbed exposes failures that early reward gains conceal and guides the integration of established components: gated reward, importance-weight control, aligned training and inference engines, and consistent state across policy updates. Abstract: Radio frequency fingerprinting (RFF) models can achieve accuracy under source domain conditions but become unreliable when the receiver, distance, collection day, or environment changes. Incorrect predictions in unseen domains may receive high softmax confidence, making confidence thresholding insufficient for trustworthy deployment. We propose TrustAgent-RFF, a prediction level reliability framework for RFF that operates with a frozen in-phase and quadrature (I/Q) classifier without target domain labels. For each device prediction, the framework acquires radio frequency evidence: counterfactual stability, prototype consistency, acquisition domain similarity, and signal quality. A source calibrated trust estimator combines these diagnostics with classifier confidence, prediction margin, and evidence availability indicators. Source derived lower tail penalties and cross module interactions reduce trust when diagnostic signals indicate weak support. A large language model (LLM) based planner adaptively selects the next diagnostic action based on evidence, trust state, action costs, and remaining budget. A deterministic source calibrated decision layer updates the prediction state as Accept, Reject, or Defer. Experiments across five RFF datasets and classifier backbones reveal an average source to target accuracy drop of 38.7% and show that many incorrect target domain predictions remain highly confident.

Abstract: CSS3 @font-face rules with unicode-range descriptors partition web fonts into downloadable slices. While Latin fonts need ~20 KB in WOFF2, Pan-European (LGC) and East Asian (CJK) fonts contain 2,297-30,759 codepoints (158 KB-5.9 MB in WOFF2), where heuristic partitioning causes unused-glyph downloads, high HTTP RTT, CSS bloat, or missing-glyph fallbacks. We present MLFont, a machine learning and optimization framework that learns font slicing strategies from corpora via three stages: (1) a Zipfian High-Frequency Tier (most) isolating top-frequency codepoints; (2) a Latent Co-Occurrence Topic Tier using Online Variational Bayes LDA (Online LDA) or a Deep Neural Network Topic Model (DNNTM) with Shannon Entropy Difference Loss and Sinkhorn-Knopp normalization; and (3) a Memetic Range-Compact Genetic Algorithm (GA) with Potts-Viterbi dynamic programming and island bridging that minimizes topic discrepancy and CSS fragmentation. Across five Google Noto fonts (LGC, KR, JP, TC, SC), MLFont cuts 3G per-glyph latency by 56.7%-75.3% over single-file delivery and 46.1%-66.3% over equal-bin baselines on CJK with 100% coverage, reduces CSS intervals by 17.9%-41.7%, and achieves 1.56x-6.09x cold-cache speedups (65.4%-84.9% CJK and 44.7% LGC bandwidth savings) across 20 websites in simulator and Headless Chrome benchmarks (45/45 exact slice-set parity).

Abstract: Muon has recently emerged as a strong optimizer for large language model pretraining by exploiting matrix structure through polar orthogonalization. However, it processes each parameter matrix independently, whereas recent tensor-aware extensions of Muon jointly update groups of eligible matrix parameters through matrix unfoldings. In contrast, we propose T-MUON, a t-SVD-based generalization that groups structurally related Transformer gradients into third-order tensors and defines the corresponding Muon-style update directly through the $M$-transform. We show that the exact T-MUON update is a steepest-descent direction under the $\star_M$-spectral norm and establish a convergence guarantee. Across GPT-2-style and LLaMA-style models from 51M to 1.3B parameters trained on FineWeb and FineWeb-Edu, head-wise T-MUON with the identity transform consistently achieves lower validation perplexity than AdamW, Muon, and variants using DFT, DCT, or DST transforms. These gains persist in additional experiments with longer context lengths and larger token budgets, although the preferred tensorization mode varies across settings.

Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is widely used for offline Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback. However, recent works have shown that DPO can be easily manipulated by adversarial preference label flip attacks, which are specifically designed to mislead the learner toward an attacker-chosen target policy. To address this issue, we design robust DPO methods that can defend against such attacks. Building on the property that lable flips induce parameter-independent gradient shifts in log-linear DPO, we propose Trim-$K$ Robust-DPO, which removes the $K$ highest-impact pairs, and Soft-Trim-$K$ Robust-DPO, which smoothly downweights them. We establish deterministic gradient perturbation bounds uniformly over all attack sets containing at most $K$ flips, explicitly separating defense-induced bias from surviving attack effects. Under bounded-region assumptions, these bounds yield time-uniform parameter and policy deviation guarantees. We further quantify how quadratic regularization improves curvature while introducing additional bias. Experiments on Stanford Human Preferences, HH-RLHF, and UltraFeedback-Binarized validate our analysis.

Abstract: Next-token prediction leaves hidden-state trajectories largely unconstrained, while existing geometric regularizers primarily control the direction rather than the magnitude of trajectory deviations. We introduce \emph{ControlJEPA}, a trajectory regularization framework based on a discrete-time Lyapunov contraction condition from practical Input-to-State Stability theory. Its Lyapunov Tube Loss penalizes violations of this condition on a normalized, scale-invariant measure of transversal deviation. When the contraction condition holds at every step, the hidden-state trajectory remains within an explicit tube around the reference secant. In the partial-ISS regime, we bound mean transversal energy, temporal variance, and average tube width using the Lyapunov loss and contraction parameters, with horizon-independent variance bounds under uniformly bounded violations. ControlJEPA requires no architectural modifications. Across six benchmarks and six model families, it consistently improves accuracy and data efficiency over standard fine-tuning and prior trajectory regularizers. The resulting smoother transversal profile also persists empirically during inference, when the regularizer is inactive. Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is a promising way to improve the clinical reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs), but current pipelines often rely on expert-labeled questions or costly synthetic-data generation. Meanwhile, the medical literature offers a wealth of high-quality unlabeled documents, including clinical case reports that trace the full clinical course from initial presentation to final treatment decisions. We study whether document-grounded self-play can convert these reports into effective RL training signals. We identify two challenges that limit existing self-play methods in this setting: knowledge-point fixation}, where the proposer repeatedly asks about a narrow set of salient clues in a source report, and difficulty-quality mismatch, where appropriately difficult questions may still be poorly formed or shallow, limiting their value for reasoner training. To address these challenges, we propose CasePlay, a self-play framework with two key components: (a) a Knowledge-Conditioned Proposer, which anchors question generation to case-specific knowledge points so that questions draw on a broader range of evidence from the source report; and (b) a Rubric-Judge Reasoner, which augments answer-accuracy feedback with rubric-based quality feedback on clue integration, reasoning depth, and option quality, guiding the proposer toward reasoning-intensive questions that better support reasoner training. Extensive experiments across seven medically related benchmarks demonstrate that CasePlay outperforms existing self-play baselines and improves the SFT warm-start model by 3.1 points on average, highlighted by a 6.3-point gain on LiveClin. This approach offers an effective path toward continually improving medical reasoning ability through self-play RL.

</details>

Clinically, without label-space compliance, cross-site accuracy comparisons misattribute annotation-standard differences to model skill; Luminal A/B and claudin-low boundary errors map to distinct treatment decisions; and subtype accuracy is a weaker portable endpoint than survival discrimination (external C-index 0.577-0.584). Reporting the cohort's allowed label set and applying label-space compliance are therefore prerequisites before any cross-cohort performance claim.

Abstract: Fully synthetic pretraining data generated by symbolic programs can bootstrap downstream training on naturalistic tasks, such as language modeling, but the generating programs have so far been designed by hand. We introduce PRECOCIAL, a method for automatically optimizing programs that generate synthetic pretraining data. Starting from an initial generator program, an LLM iteratively rewrites the program, and each rewrite is scored by pretraining a randomly-initialized transformer on its data and fine-tuning it on the downstream task. Because every score requires training, PRECOCIAL keeps a single network for the entire search, continuing its pretraining for each accepted program instead of training a new network per candidate, making it significantly more efficient. On four language and code datasets, at a fixed fine-tuning budget, optimized programs reach lower perplexity than hand-designed curricula, published procedural generators, random program mutation and the program the LLM writes in the first iteration of the search, and reach the same perplexity as the hand-designed curricula with 9-33% fewer fine-tuning steps. PRECOCIAL also ends with lower perplexity than FunSearch and AlphaEvolve, which train a new network for every candidate, at the same number of evaluations and a lower cost. We also observe that an optimized program generator from one domain transfers to datasets in other domains. Additionally, optimized programs allow for better downstream performance on four different reasoning and problem-solving tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Sparse attention speeds up long-context decoding by reading few keys of the KV cache, but existing methods do not control the error of a query, and on real KV caches their errors have heavy tails. We show that perturbing the score of every key with an independent Exp(1) variable turns threshold retrieval into sampling keys with probability proportional to their attention weights. Lowering the threshold along a geometric grid gives nested samples, a Horvitz--Thompson estimate of the output at every level, and confidence bounds that hold simultaneously over all levels, so each head stops at the first level whose certificate meets the target error $\varepsilon$. With fresh perturbations at every decoding step, each certificate fails with probability at most $δ$ during generation. At a suitable threshold, the estimator reads $O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log(1/δ))$ values regardless of the context length, and no adaptive method reads fewer, up to constants. A 4-bit scan with deterministic bounds, shared across a grouped-query-attention group, generates the candidates exactly but reads every key. On three LLMs and ten settings at 16K--128K tokens, no certificate is violated, and at equal keys read our 90th-percentile error is 4.6--7.9$\times$ lower than that of the best of 15 baseline families. At equal bytes, the best baselines have 0.6--1.6 times our 90th-percentile error and at least 4.9 times our largest. End to end, our method stays within one point of dense attention on tasks and within 0.1\% in PG-19 perplexity, and the strongest baselines at equal bytes score as well. On CPU, decoding steps at 32K--128K tokens run 1.1--1.4$\times$ faster than dense attention on the same 16-bit cache.

Abstract: Recursive Transformers depend not only on how many parameters are shared, but also on how shared parameters are organized across depth. Existing static schemes adopt two extreme reuse topologies: layer-wise repetition, where each layer is reused independently, and global cyclic reuse, where the entire stack is repeatedly applied as a single shared function. We argue that sharing granularity is an important factor in recursive parameter sharing. This study proposes Block Recursive Transformers (BRT), which partition layers into contiguous blocks and recursively reuse each block before moving to the next. This intermediate topology enables local refinement within blocks while preserving depth-wise transitions, without adding routing modules, auxiliary states, or extra training objectives. Across decoder-only models up to 1.7B parameters, with additional encoder-decoder and encoder-only studies, BRT improves validation negative log-likelihood and downstream accuracy over static recursive sharing baselines. Ablations and representation analyses further show that intermediate block granularity better balances local refinement and depth-wise differentiation. Project website: https://aim-skku.github.io/BRT/

Abstract: Looped large language models trade parameter storage for recurrent computation, but most are trained from scratch. In practice, pretraining with looped depth is expensive, making post-training upcycling of a pretrained non-looped LLM an attractive alternative. Instead of looping a whole model or a middle block, UpLoop loops each selected layer locally, preserving the pretrained depth hierarchy and avoiding feature-space mismatch. To make loop execution useful, we add hidden-wise gates on the attention and MLP residual branches, so later loops act as progressive refinements rather than redundant rewrites. Empirically, UpLoop breaks the commonly observed saturation at two loops ($R=2$) in prior looped models. Starting from Qwen3-4B Base and applying SFT, UpLoop improves the average accuracy across 20 benchmarks by 6.17\% at $R=3$. Abstract: As LLM adoption surges, scaling model serving requires unlocking stranded GPU capacity alongside deploying more GPUs. Speculative decoding brings two models into the serving process: a small draft model proposes tokens that a larger target model verifies. Yet compiling these models individually leaves their shared execution outside the compiler's optimization boundary. We introduce CONJUGATE COMPILATION, which reframes speculative decoding as a cross-model compilation problem and jointly optimizes the draft and target models within their shared execution rather than treating each in isolation. Our key insight is that cross-model compilation should be informed by the measured performance of the co-located shared execution it produces: an optimization that improves a kernel in isolation need not improve the models when they execute together. To expand cross-model opportunities, we devise operand layouts that interleave blocks from both models within common tiles, co-locating GEMM and non-GEMM work while preserving their mathematics. We realize CONJUGATE COMPILATION through a self-improving agentic search system in which an LLM edits cross-model optimization plans using the compiler and GPU execution as its environment. Each proposed plan is compiled and evaluated through its co-located shared execution, whose measured score, together with kernel-level verdicts and compiler feedback, informs subsequent proposals, while Monte Carlo tree search accumulates rewards to guide exploration. Across six single-GPU speculative-decoding configurations on NVIDIA B300 and RTX 5090 GPUs, CONJUGATE COMPILATION achieves a geometric-mean per-round speedup of 1.37x over conventional execution. Interleaved kernels raise this speedup to 1.45x, reaching 1.59x in the best case. CONJUGATE COMPILATION also improves GPU utilization on average by 1.46x on RTX 5090 and 1.50x on B300.

</details>

Abstract: Latent world models such as LeWM plan by searching for an action sequence whose predicted terminal latent is closest to the goal latent under an ambient norm, such as the Euclidean distance. Planning with such a cost assumes that proximity in the ambient latent space reflects proximity to the goal in the environment. Studying LeWM, we find that this assumption holds only in a small neighborhood around the goal, whereas farther away the L2 cost forms spurious low-cost basins that trap the planner. Yet neighborhood-based embeddings show that the latents retain the underlying physical state. We therefore hypothesize that the latents lie near a curved, low-dimensional manifold, and that the terminal cost should measure the distance along this latent manifold rather than through the ambient space. To estimate this manifold distance, we propose the Neighborhood Graph Cost (NGC), which computes shortest-path distances on a mutual nearest-neighbor graph constructed over training latents. NGC changes only the terminal cost and requires no retraining. This single change improves the planning success rate of LeWM across all four benchmark tasks and nearly solves TwoRoom and Reacher: even at the farthest goal offsets tested, NGC reaches 95% and 100% success, whereas the L2 cost falls to 27% and 81%. These results support the manifold hypothesis and our argument that latent world models should plan with distances along the latent manifold.

</details>

Abstract: Multifidelity Monte Carlo (MFMC) reduces the cost of estimating the statistics of quantities of interest (QoIs) computed via expensive high-fidelity model by using cheaper low-fidelity models as control variates. We extend MFMC to vector-valued QoIs by replacing the scalar control-variate coefficients with matrices and minimizing a weighted mean-squared error. The optimal full-matrix coefficients are available in closed form, and their variance reduction is summarized by an effective correlation that takes the place of the squared Pearson correlation in the closed-form MFMC design. Restricting the coefficients to block-diagonal, diagonal, and scalar classes gives a nested hierarchy of estimators with increasing variance reduction and increasing pilot-data demand, with closed-form coefficients for compatible weightings. For a scalar QoI obtained by linearly projecting the state, correcting the full state before projecting is never worse at the population level. Experiments on a Gaussian problem, a reduced-order model of an advection--diffusion problem, and a coarse-grid two-phase flow problem show that the best class depends on the pilot size relative to the number of covariance quantities it must estimate, and that reusing the pilot samples lowers the mean-squared error.

Abstract: Decentralized Online Convex Optimization (D-OCO) is a fundamental framework in which a set of local agents aims to minimize a sequence of global loss functions using only local computations and communications. While recent work has established near-optimal regret guarantees for convex and strongly convex losses, the case of exp-concave losses remains an open challenge: specifically, whether logarithmic regret is achievable. Concretely, our algorithm achieves a regret bound of $O\left(Nd(1-σ_2)^{-1/2}\,\log^2(T) \right)$ where $N$ is the number of agents, $d$ is the dimension, $T$ is the horizon, and $1-σ_2$ represents the spectral gap of the network. To our knowledge, this is the first logarithmic regret bound for decentralized online exp-concave optimization. We further establish a nearly matching lower bound of $Ω\left(Nd (1-σ_2)^{-1/2}\,\log(T/N) \right)$ showing the near optimality of our upper bound. Finally, we extend our algorithm to decentralized online linear regression in which the feasible domain is unbounded and features and labels are locally observed. By gossiping covariance and label-feature statistics, we also achieve logarithmic regret.

Abstract: Predicting single-cell responses to genetic and chemical perturbations is important for understanding regulatory mechanisms and prioritizing therapeutic interventions. Existing methods have made progress by modeling perturbation-conditioned expression shifts with neural networks, graph priors, foundation-model representations, or distributional generative models. However, combinatorial perturbation prediction remains challenging because much of the response can be explained by additive single-perturbation effects, while the biologically informative signal often lies in a smaller non-additive residual induced by perturbation interactions. Directly generating the full post-perturbation expression profile may therefore allocate substantial capacity to the dominant additive component and underemphasize synergy-specific structure. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{SynGeneFlow}, a synergy-aware flow matching framework that anchors the source distribution at an additive prior and learns a residual flow toward the true perturbed state. SynGeneFlow further incorporates perturbation-conditioned gene interaction modeling, where a static gene-gene mask provides biological structure and a perturbation-dependent deformation bias dynamically modulates gene-gene coupling. To preserve population-level response structure, we align both endpoint distributions and biological residual distributions with MMD-based regularization. We evaluate SynGeneFlow on genetic and drug perturbation benchmarks under observed-single, held-out, and cross-modality settings. The results demonstrate that residualizing flow matching around additive perturbation effects provides a biologically grounded and robust framework for single-cell perturbation response prediction.

</details>

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains agents on their own trajectories using supervision from a stronger teacher. We uncover a counterintuitive phenomenon in multi-turn agentic tasks: a smaller, initially weaker student can substantially outperform its teacher during training, only to regress as distillation continues. We term this phenomenon distillation overshoot. Through decision- and task-level analyses, we trace overshoot to two competing effects of OPD: repairing weak student decisions and eroding successful student choices that the teacher disfavors. Early in training, repair outpaces erosion, allowing the student to combine the teacher's corrections with its own strengths and surpass the teacher. As training continues, erosion overtakes repair, causing these gains to vanish. Motivated by this finding, we propose Success-Preserving On-Policy Distillation (SPOD), which selectively masks conflicting teacher supervision on successful student choices and introduces a soft probability floor to counteract their erosion, while retaining teacher guidance elsewhere. SPOD requires only binary episode success and model probabilities already available during OPD. Across two model families and three agentic benchmarks, SPOD consistently outperforms ordinary OPD and evaluated variants, improving final success by up to 15.2 percentage points and surpassing the teacher by up to 20.0 points. Moreover, SPOD's final checkpoints outperform even ordinary OPD's best intermediate checkpoints in all six settings, demonstrating that beyond-teacher gains can be not only preserved but further extended. Code: https://github.com/Jike338/SPOD

</details>

Abstract: Fine-tuning language models on consumer GPUs is constrained by computation and memory. Although LoRA reduces trainable parameters and optimizer state, frozen projections remain expensive, and activations retained for backward can dominate memory usage. We present PACT, a LoRA fine-tuning system that accelerates frozen projections with native NVFP4 GEMMs while reducing quantization and dequantization overhead and limiting quantization error. To reduce activation memory, PACT reuses quantized RMSNorm outputs and saves gate/up activations in NVFP4 for backward. To limit numerical error, it preserves BF16 inputs for forward nonlinear operations and calibrates frozen weights offline using GPTQ adapted to NVFP4 scales and quantized activations. Fused nonlinear kernels and cached forward and backward weight layouts reduce quantization and dequantization overhead. Evaluations across multiple models and tasks on a 16\,GB RTX~5080 show lower memory usage and faster LoRA fine-tuning with competitive accuracy, enabling model adaptation on resource-constrained consumer hardware.

</details>

Abstract: Flow Matching (FM) is an efficient simulation-free approach for training flow-based generative models, making it a promising foundation for scalable time-series anomaly detection (TSAD). However, standard FM learns a monolithic vector field in which nominal transport and local density-changing behavior are entangled. This creates a central challenge for TSAD; local expansion and contraction carry crucial anomaly-sensitive information, yet isolating this signal requires expensive Jacobian-trace estimation over the entire field. We propose \emph{Helmholtz Component Flow Matching} (HCFM), an unsupervised TSAD framework that parameterizes the vector field prediction network with separate transport, compression, and residual heads. The transport head is constrained to be divergence-free, so divergence estimation is restricted to the compression head, while a lightweight residual head absorbs the remaining mismatch. This structured parameterization improves scalability and detection performance by separating volume-preserving nominal transport from density-changing compression, yielding cleaner anomaly signals from distinct deviations of nominal behavior. Experiments on large-scale univariate and multivariate TSAD benchmarks show that HCFM consistently improves detection accuracy while maintaining efficient inference compared with 16 baselines. The code is available at https://github.com/TrustAIoT/HCFM__time-series-anomaly/

</details>

Abstract: Pointwise replicability asks that two independently trained predictors, using the same random seed, agree at each fixed query with high probability. We determine its optimal agnostic sample complexity on finite and countable domains. For every nonempty binary class of finite VC dimension $d\ge1$, the required number of independent examples is $Θ\!\left(\frac{d}{ρ^2α^2}+\frac{\log(1/β)}{α^2}\right)$, uniformly over independent accuracy, failure, and disagreement parameters $α,β,ρ\in(0,1/10)$. The learner returns one binary function, may be improper, and uses unrestricted shared randomness. Its sample budget is fixed before observing data, and the replicability guarantee includes queries of zero marginal mass. The main construction combines an ensemble of empirical-risk minimizers with a smooth, signal-dependent risk decomposition. A single shared phase selects the final binary predictor. Record times certify its weak-signal risk, while a uniform bound protects the strong-signal risk at that same selected time. A phase-comparison theorem controls the actual shared-seed execution under simultaneous changes in the marginals and risk coefficients. A rare repair step then makes the confidence cost additive. Together with matching lower bounds, this removes the remaining logarithmic overhead and parameter restrictions in the general countable-domain sample-complexity problem. This extended version also retains the earlier explicit constructions and their proofs, including proper learning for VC-one classes, the full finite-atom class, a faster realizable bound, and counterexamples that separate restrictions of particular algorithms from restrictions of the learning problem.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous agents are increasingly capable of developing machine learning (ML) models end to end. In medical practice, however, strong performance on the target metric does not fully characterize how good the models are in real-world use cases. For example, medical ML models should also provide well-calibrated risk estimates and remain robust across patient populations. Yet autonomous model development is typically guided by task objectives that emphasize simple predictive discrimination (e.g. AUROC). This raises a key question: are these objectives sufficient to guide agents toward models that meet the broader requirements of real-world medical use? In this study, we introduce Auto-MD-bench, a benchmark of 15 real-world medical tasks from 10 clinical datasets, spanning survival analysis, classification and sequential prediction, with external validation across cohorts, hospitals and time periods. We evaluate four agents: AIDE, AI Scientist v2, Codex, and Autoresearch. The strongest agents achieve competitive primary scores, yet their predicted risks are less calibrated than those of the simple baseline models, which could lead to severe medical accidents. Moreover, models with nearly identical primary scores can exhibit markedly different calibration, while performance varies substantially across population shifts. These findings show that primary task performance alone is insufficient to assess autonomous clinical modeling and multiple different metrics widely used in healthcare practice need to be taken into consideration.

</details>

Abstract: Model--harness co-evolution has emerged as a prominent paradigm for recursive self-improving (RSI) agents, jointly evolving their execution scaffolds and intrinsic capabilities. We study the persistent yet often overlooked brain--body mismatch problem in this paradigm, where an evolving harness (body) demands behaviors its model (brain) has not learned or restricts capabilities it already possesses. We introduce CoEvo-RSI, a RSI framework that advances model--harness co-evolution through agent-driven, bidirectional historical inheritance. Drawing on a shared evolutionary archive and execution feedback, a meta-agent coordinates harness development from historical designs with model checkpoint selection or merging and subsequent adaptation under the selected harness, aligning inherited capabilities with evolving execution requirements to reduce brain--body mismatch. Using only supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on its own rejection-sampled trajectories, CoEvo-RSI makes Qwen3.5-9B competitive with larger models across diverse vertical applications, including multi-agent failure attribution, role-aware text-to-SQL, and smart-home instruction following. It surpasses DeepSeek-V4-Pro on TraceElephant (+14.16 agent accuracy) and GLM-5.3 on HomeBench VM (+4.90 success rate), underscoring the need for brain--body alignment and the potential of bidirectional historical inheritance to advance agent co-evolution.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning improves the reasoning capability of large language models through verifiable rewards for mathematical and coding tasks. In token-level methods such as GRPO, clipping acts on individual token ratios while the reward and advantage apply to a complete response. Recent sequence-level methods align these units but still use fixed clipping bands, whose tail probabilities change as the score distribution evolves. We propose Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization (CSPO), which adaptively calibrates sequence-level clipping bounds using recent training statistics. The resulting band targets a specified marginal tail budget and adapts to policy drift; calibration conditional on response length requires additional assumptions. We bound the probability that a fresh score falls outside the band by the target miscoverage level plus explicit CDF discrepancy and drift terms. Under additional moment conditions, this bound also controls the objective and gradient error relative to the unclipped GSPO power surrogate. Experiments with the Qwen3-1.7B series show improvements over GSPO on difficult mathematical reasoning benchmarks and ALFWorld, with clipping endpoints that adapt during training.

Abstract: The explore/exploit trade-off is the core problem of reinforcement learning (RL). We show that a minimal foraging swarm approaches its optimum - the ideal-free / Nash allocation of a multi-agent resource-sharing game (an equilibrium, not a return maximum) - with no learner and no reward signal. Agents explore by heavy-tailed (Levy) flights, recruit neighbours to a discovery through a threshold cascade on their proximity network, and disperse as a patch crowds; this cascade cycle balances exploration and exploitation and drives foragers toward the ideal free distribution. The swarm attains the shape of this optimum (committed foragers track richness with slope 0.94) but not its exact equilibrium - it stays measurably exploitable - reaching it dynamically (~150 steps) through the coupling of recruitment to movement: remove the coupling and matching collapses (0.94 -> 0.08). The RL sits at the level of the problem and the allocation reached, not the micro-rule: no step evaluates payoff (reward-free by construction, though the crowd signal is informationally tied to payoff), and the swarm reaches the allocation without learning it (quality plateaus and re-settles after resources move, no cumulative gain). Against a trained baseline the comparison is two-sided: in an easier, disembodied game a learner is more precise, while in the same embodied environment the reward-free swarm reaches ~65% of a validated oracle ceiling at zero training and leads until trained PPO and centralised-critic (CTDE) learners overtake it (~74-75%, 16 seeds, all above the swarm mean) after ~4,000 of 15,000 training steps - its advantage is in budget, not asymptote. In this one setting we read it not as beating RL but as a strong zero-shot, reward-free operating point: a demonstration that the self-organizing physics of a minimal swarm can carry a collective close to the optimum RL seeks, with none of RL's machinery.

</details>

Abstract: We study bias-free, one-hidden-layer ReLU networks with nonnegative output weights under standard Gaussian inputs. Hidden directions may be arbitrarily close or linearly dependent. The labels have the target as their conditional mean, and only the unconditional second moment of the noise is bounded. We present a candidate real-arithmetic learning guarantee: a width-$k$ target with total output weight at most one can be approximated to squared prediction error $\varepsilon$ by a nonnegative ReLU network of width at most $k+\min\{k,d\}$, with sample and operation cost $(1+σ^2)\operatorname{poly}(d,k,\varepsilon^{-1},\log(1/δ))$. The argument combines implicit high-order moments with a direction cover that is complete for heavy atoms and sound for every output. Exact Gaussian soft localization refines this cover while pruning keeps at most $k$ centers at every scale. Clipping the query multipliers controls heavy-tailed, input-dependent noise without assuming a uniform conditional variance bound. A final convex fit and an exact sparsification step give the stated output width. We also obtain a parameterized projective direction-recovery guarantee. The result concerns real-arithmetic complexity; no bit-complexity or practical running-time guarantee is asserted.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory to accumulate past experiences for continual adaptation. However, effectively reusing memory requires accurately assessing its value, as retrieved experiences may become unreliable when transferred across task contexts. Despite its importance, memory valuation remains largely underexplored, with existing methods relying on historical retrieval success rates that overlook the context dependency of memory usefulness and the varying reliability of estimates under different amounts of evidence. Inspired by the Bayesian brain hypothesis, we propose BEAM (Bayesian-style Evolutionary Agentic Memory), a framework that reformulates memory value evaluation as a parameterized cognitive inference process, where memory value is represented as a context-conditioned dynamic belief rather than a passive historical statistic. Specifically, BEAM infers memory value beliefs from current task contexts and continuously calibrates them using execution feedback as empirical evidence, transforming memory from passive storage into an adaptive cognitive mechanism for continual self-evolution. Extensive experiments across reasoning and code generation benchmarks demonstrate that BEAM consistently improves memory utilization and enables continual self-evolution of LLM agents.

</details>

Abstract: Offline sepsis treatment aims to learn treatment policies from retrospective ICU trajectories. Decision Transformer-based methods provide a natural sequence modeling framework for this task, but they mainly rely on raw physiological states and historical clinician actions. Such raw trajectory tokens may not explicitly capture clinical semantics that clinicians use in practice, such as patient severity, recent treatment response, and current risk priorities. To address this limitation, we propose ClinSDT, an LLM-assisted Decision Transformer framework for offline sepsis treatment. ClinSDT uses a frozen LLM as a semantic bridge rather than a treatment decision maker. It constructs three clinical semantic views from ICU trajectories, namely Profile, Context, and Advisory, and inserts their encoded representations as independent tokens into a Decision Transformer policy. To support policy refinement and rollout, ClinSDT further trains a Treatment-Response Forecaster to estimate the next physiological state, SAPS-II change, and predictive uncertainty from state-action inputs. In the second training stage, the forecaster screens local counterfactual treatment candidates, and only low-uncertainty candidates with lower predicted SAPS-II increase are distilled into the policy after semantic regeneration. During inference, ClinSDT performs closed-loop treatment rollout by refreshing semantic inputs from the currently available patient state. Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV show that ClinSDT achieves consistent relative improvements over representative imitation learning, offline reinforcement learning, and Decision Transformer baselines under the same rollout protocol. Code and data are available at https://github.com/KAG778/ClinSDT.

</details>

Abstract: Skill-augmented agentic RL has emerged as an effective approach to improving LLM agents on long-horizon tasks. Despite this progress, individual skills may follow distinct trajectories as the policy evolves, with some becoming redundant after the model internalizes their capabilities, others remaining valuable as external scaffolding, and still others turning harmful. However, existing skill library systems fail to identify each skill's true contribution due to free-riding among co-retrieved skills, difficulty confounding across tasks, and the absence of skill-specific counterfactual evidence, which prevents reliable skill lifecycle management. We propose TrackRL, an agentic RL framework where skill tracking drives skill library full lifecycle management. Specifically, within each GRPO group, we turn grouped sampling into online randomized controlled experiments. Independent random injection and within-group contrasts on the same task isolate each skill's true contribution, and reliability-weighted aggregation accumulates evidence across groups. As evidence accumulates, we continuously track skill utility, which measures the skill's causal contribution as external scaffolding, and model mastery, which reflects how far the corresponding capability has been internalized. Along this line, we formulate skill lifecycle management as an evidence-gated state-transition process that governs each skill from generation and admission through protected incubation to retention, retirement, or removal, while a soft capacity bound controls library growth. Experiments show that TrackRL outperforms recent skill-augmented RL baselines in both library-assisted and skill-free evaluation, with further analyses revealing how skill value shifts during policy learning and how evidence-driven state transitions shape skill library evolution. Our code is available at https://github.com/xizhu1022/TrackRL.

Abstract: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) replace fixed scalar weights by learnable univariate maps, yet most spectral KANs still rely on Chebyshev polynomials that live on a bounded interval and therefore require a saturating squashing of the pre-activation. We introduce the Fractional Hermite Kolmogorov-Arnold Network (FHKAN), a neural architecture that equips KANs with an adaptive, trainable fractional-order generalized Hermite basis. With a nonnegative shape parameter, the underlying generalized Hermite functions are orthonormal on the entire real line and uniformly bounded under a Gaussian envelope, so they act directly on unbounded pre-activations without any saturating map. We derive closed-form derivatives of the generalized, fractional, and scaled basis away from the origin and determine its order of differentiability at the origin, where the fractional power map creates a singular point. On synthetic regression, FHKAN attains the lowest test error at every network depth among nine fixed activations and the spline-based KAN. On image classification, image denoising, and sentiment analysis, the best-performing fractional Hermite block yields the highest accuracy or reconstruction quality. On physics-informed problems, FHKAN reaches mean absolute errors of order 10^-6 to 10^-5 on the Lane-Emden equation, residuals of order 10^-4 on the Burgers equation, and absolute errors below 10^-4 on a fractional delay differential equation.

Abstract: Catastrophic forgetting in sequential deep learning arises from a fixed-capacity architectural constraint: parameter updates encoding new tasks perturb weight subspaces supporting prior representations. We introduce the Knowledge Fusion Network (KFN), which mitigates the stability-plasticity trade-off in class-incremental learning through novelty-gated structural expansion rather than parameter negotiation within a shared capacity budget. KFN decouples task acquisition from knowledge consolidation: each incoming task is learned in an isolated lightweight specialist network (2.8M parameters) and integrated into the global model via a Structural Fusion Module that allocates dedicated channel capacity conditioned on a quantitative novelty score. Dual structural isolation--channel-space separation with temporally phased gradient unfreezing--protects consolidated knowledge. On ten-task Split CIFAR-100, KFN achieves 11.94% minimum task accuracy and 71.16% task-10 accuracy, whereas shared-parameter baselines degrade severely: LwF to 9.07% average accuracy (0.00% min), Replay++ to 8.91% (85.08% forgetting), and Replay to 16.21% (73.44% forgetting); KFN's forgetting (57.92%) is 15.5 pp lower than Replay. Furthermore, KFN prevents logit-scale drift, maintaining near-symmetric task performance. Across CIFAR-100 and EuroSAT satellite imagery, KFN's class-incremental retention (72.7% and 42.5%) substantially exceeds the best baselines (29.6% and 0.94%). Crucially, KFN achieves parameter-efficient linear scaling, adding 25% of the base parameter count per step and requiring 25.1% of naive backbone duplication over ten tasks (28.1M vs 112.0M). With a 37.1% peak GPU memory reduction over full retraining, KFN establishes novelty-gated expansion as a practical path beyond fixed-capacity architectures.

The answer changed with the grain. For an item by store and an item across stores, the average had the lowest error on seven and eight of nine data sets, and the 90% confidence set held a median of 1 and 2 models. For a category by store, a category and the chain total, the lowest-error model differed between data sets and a median of 4 of nine models were not separated by the four to six windows available. Every trained model used the plan, and on the weekly promotion-driven data pretrained models were less accurate without it.
