Abstract: Medical vision-language models often exhibit hidden stratification, where high overall performance masks failures on subtle visual findings and overlapping clinical conditions. We trace this failure mode to modality imbalance during fine-tuning, where strong linguistic priors can suppress visually grounded learning and induce plausible but weakly grounded rationales. Existing data selection methods usually score image-text pairs as holistic units, without separating visually necessary samples from shortcut-solvable ones. To address this problem, we propose ViCoR, a counterfactual-residual-based data selection method for Med-VLM fine-tuning. ViCoR estimates visual necessity through image-dependent optimization residuals, calibrates the resulting scores with medical supervision compatibility, and constructs a diversity-aware fine-tuning subset. Across two backbones, four external generalization benchmarks, and one multi-disease robustness benchmark, ViCoR-selected 20\% subsets achieve the strongest average performance compared with full-data fine-tuning and selection baselines. Gradient-level analysis and perturbation audits further show that ViCoR enriches image-dependent training signals and strengthens image-level and evidence-region dependence. The code will be available at https://github.com/htgan-ai/ViCoR.

Abstract: Hybrid architectures offer a promising approach to efficient language modeling by combining a small number of full-attention layers with a larger number of efficient sequence layers, such as sparse attention and recurrent layers. While full-attention layers maintain a KV cache that grows linearly with sequence length, efficient layers typically rely on separate, fixed-size states rather than accessing this richer existing memory. Recent work has begun exploring ways to allow efficient layers access to the KV cache, but the theoretical benefits of such access, and the extent to which existing approaches can realize them remain poorly understood. We investigate these questions by analyzing the expressive power of individual hybrid blocks on function composition, a well-studied task in sequence-model expressivity associated with multi-hop reasoning. We first establish that both conventional hybrid blocks and those with top-$κ$ KV-cache access require $Ω(\sqrt{n})$ capacity to compute even two-function composition over a domain of size $n$. To overcome this limitation, we introduce the \emph{Multi-hop Retrieval Attention} (MuRA) hybrid block, a simple architecture that enables multi-hop retrieval from a shared KV cache while preserving asymptotic computational and memory efficiency. We prove that a single fixed size $m$-hop MuRA block can compute $m$-function composition, whereas computing $(m+1)$-function composition with the same retrieval depth requires polynomial model size, establishing a strict expressivity hierarchy in retrieval depth. Synthetic experiments support this hierarchy, showing that increasing retrieval depth enables learning progressively deeper compositions, substantially outperforming existing hybrid architectures. Together, our results identify multi-hop retrieval as a promising mechanism for improving the expressive power of efficient hybrid architectures.

</details>

Abstract: Recent test-time scaling methods improve model performance by increasing inference-time compute. One practical form of this is parallel scaling, where a model samples multiple independent reasoning streams and aggregates their answers using methods like voting, reranking, or verification. While effective, this approach leaves the streams uncoordinated, and a discovery made in one rollout cannot influence any other. Multi-Sequence Verifiers jointly score candidate answers after generation, but the reasoning streams remain independent during inference. ParScale takes a step toward model-internal parallel scaling by learning to aggregate multiple forward passes, but its streams still do not exchange information while processing a sequence. We introduce BatchParScale, an attention mechanism that allows parallel streams to exchange information through a small set of communication positions. We evaluate BatchParScale on a new Parallel Needle-in-a-Haystack retrieval benchmark and in pretraining, fine-tuning, and cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning. Across these settings, BatchParScale outperforms ParScale on language-model loss and remains competitive on coordination-sensitive MARL tasks. Masked pretraining runs beat an unmasked single-sequence baseline despite local sequence corruption, and LoRA fine-tuning shows the mechanism can be added to a pretrained Transformer. At the same time, fact retrieval performance for pretrained and finetuned BatchParScale models remains unclear.

Abstract: EasyClassifier showed that researchers who do not program can obtain honest classification results when the correct evaluation is the default. Most applied studies, however, predict a number, forecast a series or classify recordings, and each task has its own way of producing scores that do not hold on new data. We present EasyResearch, open-source analysis cores for regression, time-series forecasting and signal classification, and EasyResearch Desktop, a Windows application that runs these three tasks and EasyClassifier's classification without code. All four follow the same rules: everything learned from data is learned on training data only, models keep their published defaults with fixed seeds, the selected model receives a separate final score, forecasts are never evaluated on shuffled data, and every run writes a report with a Methods paragraph and the references for every method used. We measured whether the reported scores are honest by keeping part of each dataset untouched. In regression (9 public datasets, 90 runs), the common practice overstated R^2 by +2.2 points and the best comparison score by +2.5, while EasyResearch's final score was -1.0; screening predictors on all rows before cross-validation, which EasyResearch does not offer, reported R^2 = 0.27 on pure noise against -0.44 on new data. In forecasting (15 series), shuffled cross-validation over windows of past values promised errors 19% smaller than its chosen models achieved in the untouched future, while EasyResearch's final-period score was unbiased to within 1.5%; the models EasyResearch chose had 14% lower future error, and on random walks shuffled cross-validation chose a machine-learning model in 5 of 5 series, each worse in the future than repeating the last value, which EasyResearch chose 4 times. In signal classification (4 UCR datasets) one method won every comparison and neither estimate was biased.

Abstract: LLM-based search agents demonstrate strong capabilities in long-horizon information seeking and reasoning, typically trained via reinforcement learning. However, existing approaches predominantly rely on trajectory-level outcome rewards, providing limited supervision for complex search and reasoning processes. Although step-level credit assignment offers a promising solution, obtaining reliable and task-grounded supervision for open-ended search remains challenging. In this paper, we propose Evidence-Guided Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO), a framework that derives step-level supervision from the evidence annotations available in synthetic deep search data. EG-GRPO identifies the first supporting citation for each required evidence claim and uses the newly acquired evidence to construct step-level process rewards. By representing the accumulated evidence as an evolving knowledge state, EG-GRPO further performs state-conditioned relative advantage estimation and combines the resulting step-level signal with the standard trajectory-level outcome advantage. Experiments on five challenging deep search benchmarks show that EG-GRPO generally outperforms standard GRPO and existing reward-enhanced and step-level RL baselines across different model scales. These results demonstrate that task-grounded evidence annotations can provide effective process supervision for credit assignment in deep search RL.

</details>

Abstract: Forecasting how species and ecosystems respond to climate change is a critical challenge in ecology, particularly in hyper-diverse tropical biomes. While traditional phenological models often rely on preconceived functional forms, this study introduces a data-driven hybrid approach that integrates dynamical systems theory with machine learning to forecast flowering and fruiting cycles.
  Using a robust 35-year dataset from a Venezuelan tropical cloud forest, we reconstruct the system's state-space using delay vectors inspired by Takens' theorem, incorporating climate forcing variables such as temperature and precipitation. The model was trained using Gaussian Process Regression, providing both point predictors and explicit uncertainty estimates.
  Our results clearly suggest that the synergy between dynamical systems and machine learning effectively captures the non-linear complexities of ecological time series. Specifically, the model achieved higher predictive accuracy for flowering and fruiting. This hybrid framework offers a scalable tool for anticipating phenological mismatches and informing conservation strategies in the face of global environmental change.

Abstract: This paper presents an original mathematical and computational framework for modeling the efficacy of methylphenidate as an optimal control policy acting on the nonlinear dynamics of attention deficit hyperactivity disorder. Neural processes in ADHD are conceptualized as stochastic processes with trajectories that inherently converge towards stable, yet dysfunctional patterns associated with clinical symptoms. The pharmacological intervention is mathematically formalized as an external control signal that reassesses the system's drift and redirects these trajectories towards neurotypical regimes. To bridge these theoretical stochastic models with empirical clinical data, latent representations are inferred using a novel deterministic hybrid deep learning architecture, combining a one-dimensional convolutional neural network and a Vision Transformer, trained on preprocessed functional magnetic resonance imaging data.

Abstract: Emerging Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) are moving beyond representation learning towards latent world models that support prediction, planning, and control. Yet their latent worlds naturally lack a human-interpretable interface. In this Perspective, we argue that as JEPA latents become internal states for prediction and action, interpretability must move beyond what they encode to how latent variables interact, evolve, and causally drive prediction. To this end, we envision a four-stage research agenda, involving Ground, Structure, Verify, and Act, to guide this shift toward actionable latent-world understanding. Within our framework, existing JEPA interpretability efforts provide early evidence for Ground, while systematic analysis of latent structure, causal verification, and intervention remains largely unexplored. Overall, this Perspective aims to call for a broader latent-world interpretability agenda that shifts attention from decoding representations toward understanding, validating, and acting on latent worlds.

Abstract: Planning does not by itself imply the ability to recognise errors, but recognising errors can improve planning capacity. In this paper, we study this phenomenon in Transformer planning. To analyze the inner mechanism, we follow the approach of Wang et al. (2024) and abstract planning as path-finding in graphs. In this setting, we train the model to detect the error of invalid transitions, i.e., after the model generates a next node that is not adjacent with the current node, the model should then generate a special abort token x. For expressiveness, we construct a Transformer with feed-forward width $O(|V|)$ for any fixed graph with node set $V$. It can detect all possible invalid transitions, and select a valid next node if the prefix is valid. We then propose a worst-case lower bound on feed-forward width, which makes the detector's linear width near-optimal up to logarithmic factors. In trained models, we demonstrate that invalid-transition detection increases previous-node attention, and most of the valid--invalid separation locates in the feed-forward network's output. This accords with our theoretical insights. Finally, we use this error detection to improve the model's performance: at inference, generating the abort token x will directly cause an external procedure to discard the attempt and restart from the original prompt. Experiments show that error detection combined with retry improves model's planning reliability on graphs, Countdown, and mazes.

</details>

Abstract: Group relative policy optimization learns from a prompt only when the rewards in the sampled group disagree. With a binary verifier, a group that is entirely correct or entirely incorrect has within-group variance zero and a zero advantage. Uniform sampling continues to spend rollouts on prompts that are unanimous now and stay unanimous as training goes on. Pass rate is a weak forecast of that future. Two prompts at the same pass rate can move apart: one into the range where a finite group splits, and one toward a ceiling where the group becomes unanimous again. Aperture estimates the later pass rate from a short probe. A small subset of the pool is trained with the same recipe, every prompt is scored on the base model and on the probe model, and the two pass rates are plugged into the probability that a group of the training size is all wrong or split. The resulting weight is a static prior. It can be used alone or multiplied by an online scheduler, and the GRPO update is left as it is. On mathematical and logical reasoning with 1.7B and 4B models, the prior improves greedy accuracy over uniform GRPO and raises the fraction of groups with a nonzero advantage. The same prior still helps when it is combined with dynamic sampling or with selective rollouts.

</details>

Abstract: Group relative policy optimization learns from a question only when the sampled responses disagree. A group that is entirely correct or entirely incorrect has zero reward variance and a zero advantage. On the Web, that group has already spent its searches and page fetches. Gaussian curricula aim the next batch at pass rates where a finite group can still mix. A Gaussian on the latest empirical rate treats every all-fail history as the same point, and a center stepped by the average signed change of those rates is pushed by prompts already stuck at 0 or 1. RIDGE keeps the Gaussian curriculum and changes the coordinate, the center, and the width. Each prompt carries a Beta posterior. The sampling weight is the marginal of a Gaussian target under a Gaussian approximation of that posterior, divided by a prediction of tool-call cost. The center and the width are an exponential smooth of the posterior means of groups that just came back mixed. All-correct and all-incorrect groups refresh the posterior and do not move the center. The GRPO update itself is unchanged. On two math backbones and a retrieval-augmented question answerer, RIDGE raises average accuracy by 3.4 and 2.9 points over GRPO and average exact match by 3.7 points, at 29.6\% fewer math rollouts and 32.7\% fewer tool calls than dynamic sampling.

</details>

Abstract: Class-conditional conformal prediction calibrates a separate score threshold for every class and therefore requires labelled calibration examples of every class. In long-tailed deployments these are exactly what is missing: rare classes are left with few or no held-out examples, and existing methods are either undefined for them or fall back to the shared marginal threshold. We ask whether the per-class correction can instead be predicted from descriptors of the class that require no labels at all. We propose Predicted Class Correction (PCC), which regresses the offset between the per-class and the marginal conformal quantile on geometric descriptors of the classifier head, shrinks the prediction by a scalar selected on the labelled classes, and refits a single marginal offset. The descriptors depend only on the model's weights, so a class with zero calibration examples still receives its own threshold, and since only one scalar is calibrated, split conformal's marginal guarantee is retained. On ImageNet, PCC improves worst-class coverage on such classes by 0.058 [0.006, 0.110] at matched average set size, 42% of an oracle that sees the test labels. Of eight baselines on the same splits, six change the statistic by exactly zero and the best reaches 0.016 with an interval containing zero, so PCC is the only one distinguishable from no improvement; the gain persists with only 100 labelled classes out of 1000. A second finding concerns evaluation: worst-class coverage becomes unmeasurable before it becomes unachievable. Below roughly 35 test examples per class no improvement can be shown even for an oracle, so a null result there reflects the evaluation set, not the method. Restricting evaluation above this depth recovers a significant improvement on Pl@ntNet-300K, where the effect had appeared absent.

</details>

Abstract: We present CHSM8, a 210M-parameter chess transformer that plays moves conditioned on a free-text description of a playing style, e.g. "go for the Sicilian Defense", "solid, safe, risk-free chess" or "You are Magnus Carlsen in his prime". The model is pretrained without search on 1.07B filtered Lichess games, using a factorized move representation and legal-move masking. It reaches 1837 Elo against a strength-limited Stockfish and, in 1,000-game matches, beat every other public model we tested that was likewise trained without preference tuning, rewards or search, including one with 13 times as many parameters. We then fine-tune it to follow text on a synthetic dataset: Gemma-4-31B describes the playing style of 3M game sides, a 1B Gemma-3 model distilled from the 2.4M of those descriptions that contradict nothing on the board labels the rest, and every one of 30M selected game sides gets five descriptions. Supervised fine-tuning attaches a frozen text encoder through gated cross-attention, and direct preference optimization (DPO) uses the same moves under a mismatched description as the rejected sample. After DPO, style adherence rises on every prompt we test (for example, queens traded early 53% -> 67%, pawn moves 29% -> 32% of all moves, queenside castling 97.3% -> 99.7%), and strength without a prompt stays within noise of SFT (1792 vs. 1797 Elo). Design choices at every stage were made by fixed-budget automated search loops on top of standard architectures and training recipes. We document the full pipeline and the decisions behind it, report the ablations that failed, and evaluate the models across many style prompts and kinds of games, with 1,000 games per setting. The work is fully open source and reproducible: weights, datasets with all training labels, prompts, configurations, inference code and playable demos are public and free to build on.

Abstract: Teacher takeover in on-policy distillation (OPD) can produce incorrect responses even when the student and teacher independently answer the same problem correctly, a failure we quantify using Collaboration Failure Rate (CFR). To address it, we propose Contextual Digest for On-Policy Distillation (CD-OPD). CD-OPD structures the earlier context of teacher guidance through a teacher-generated dependency graph, curating the required problem conditions and preceding reasoning as node summaries alongside the unchanged guidance in a Contextual Digest before student continuation. We further develop online meta-reweighting, which compares Digest segment gradients with a task gradient from independent student responses to improve student-only performance, favoring updates predicted to reduce task loss. Across eight benchmarks, CD-OPD improves average accuracy by 1.32--7.05 percentage points over representative existing OPD methods. Abstract: World models are increasingly judged by how well they rank plausible futures, yet plausibility alone is the wrong target. The same scene can evolve under many latent rules, and a future that is smooth and physically valid under one rule can be incoherent under the rule actually in force. We formalize the resulting failure, operator-blindness, and show that it is systematically hidden by standard evaluation: a scorer can attain near-perfect generic ranking while separating current-world from other-world futures no better than chance. We introduce Operator-Mismatch Contrastive (OMC) learning, whose semantic hard negatives are valid-but-wrong-operator trajectories, and a matching protocol that measures world-relative coherence directly. On a controlled synthetic benchmark with in- and out-of-distribution operator parameters, across two trajectory-scoring backbones, and on feature-level external validation with CoPhy ballsCF, OMC raises operator-sensitive scoring far above a chance baseline that nonetheless achieves 0.99 generic top-1. We prove that the wrong-operator negative is necessary both for the protocol to detect the failure and for the objective to fix it, and position OMC as an auxiliary coherence signal (a trajectory-coherence scorer, not a generative model, planner, or control agent) that can be attached to existing world-model representations.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate day-ahead wind-power production forecasting is increasingly important for energy system operations, market activity and balancing Great Britain's grid as wind generation grows. In this study, we train multi-site deep neural networks (DNNs) using pooled historical production and reanalysis weather data from 88 onshore wind Balancing Mechanism Units (BMUs). Forecasts are produced at 09:00 UK time for the following midnight-to-midnight delivery day, matching NESO's operational day-ahead forecasting schedule. We use reanalysis weather as future covariates during training and numerical weather prediction (NWP) forecasts during inference, extending training beyond the available historical NWP archive. Our multi-site recurrent neural network outperforms site-specific RNNs and a multi-site XGBoost model, while processing the full future-weather trajectory for the forecast horizon further improves accuracy relative to hour-aligned approaches. Our best model, an RNN with a bidirectional weather encoder (BiRNN) for NWP trajectory processing, reduces MW mean absolute error and root mean squared error relative to NESO by 17.94% and 12.51%, respectively, across 638,246 matched BMU-hour observations under conditions approximating unconstrained wind farm operations. In 40 leave-one-out trials, the BiRNN achieves lower MAE than NESO at 32 excluded sites, indicating transferable forecasting ability despite reduced accuracy when sites are omitted from training. Our capacity-estimation, data-cleaning and reanalysis-based training approach provides a basis for scaling NWP-aware multi-site forecasting to additional wind farms, longer production histories, and increased forecast accuracy beyond that seen in this study.

Abstract: Classical data mining treats data as generated outside the learner. Emerging AI-native infrastructure invalidates this assumption: as agents observe system state, act, update retrieval stores and models, and promote new artifacts into the serving path, their own decisions reshape the future logs, traces, corpora, and evaluation records used for later training and release. We call this new research object a self-rewriting system and the resulting system-induced distribution shift endogenous drift. Our central, falsifiable claim is that cross-surface causes are not identifiable in general from marginal per-surface monitoring: different coupled failures can produce the same individual signals while differing in their joint behavior, requiring joint analysis for reliable attribution. This agenda comprises four foundational research problems: fusing heterogeneous drift signals into calibrated gating and attribution (C1); mining agent traces whose activity alphabet evolves (C2); maintaining knowledge fabrics through online reindexing, faithfulness, and compression decisions (C3); and safely promoting concurrent learning streams through one shared evaluation gate (C4). These remain fragmented across concept drift, performative prediction, process mining, streaming retrieval-augmented generation, and MLOps, without a unified data-mining agenda. We outline public benchmarks and evaluation criteria, using AI-native telecom only as a motivating exemplar with analogues in finance, software CI/CD, and clinical decision support.

</details>

Abstract: What a newborn sees, hears and feels, and how this changes month by month, has been measured sense by sense, with different methods and in different units. We bring these measurements together for the first two years of life and ask whether small neural networks tuned to a given age can turn them into a quick, inspectable simulation of sensory perception. Each sense's immaturity is written as a fixed, age-indexed filter on the input, and a small prediction network learns behind it over the first year. The filters alone reproduce the order in which the senses mature, by construction. The networks add what filters cannot: in vision they extract far less than their input allows at early stages (at the newborn stage a toy's position cannot be read out, $R^2 = 0.00$, against 0.57 for a network trained directly on the same input), and a critical period and perceptual narrowing emerge without being built in; in hearing they track what their input allows. The same filters drive the Baby Senses Lab, a free website that shows a user's own images and sounds as a baby of a chosen age might sense them. It is a research and teaching tool, not a diagnostic test.

Abstract: We introduce radial basis function (RBF) kernels for Gaussian processes that use structured hyperparameter sharing for image-valued inputs. For high-dimensional inputs such as images, a fully anisotropic RBF kernel assigns a separate lengthscale to each input dimension, which can introduce many hyperparameters and may lead to overfitting and poorly calibrated predictive uncertainty when data are limited. We propose structured relevance determination by tying lengthscales across groups of adjacent pixels and within each colour channel. This yields patch-wise and channel-wise constrained anisotropic RBF parametrisations that reduce the number of independently learned lengthscales. We further introduce channel-additive Gaussian processes for multi-channel images and combine them with structured relevance determination, resulting in a parameter-efficient decomposition of the latent function into components defined on channel subsets, enabling some non-local generalisation. The proposed kernels are evaluated on MNIST and a compact binary aircraft-detection dataset of low-resolution RGB satellite image chips. We also show how channel-wise structured relevance determination can be incorporated into convolutional Gaussian processes. We observe improved predictive performance compared to isotropic and fully anisotropic RBF baselines.

Abstract: Empirical studies of electricity prices routinely state that their models use only information available at the time of the trading decision. For weather-driven forecasts drawn from commercial archives, this condition is harder to meet than it appears, and whether it holds can change over time. We identify three timing pitfalls. A forecast run is labelled by the issue time of its weather model, but becomes available hours later and keeps changing after publication. Auction gates follow local clock time, while archives use UTC. Outturn series are revised after publication without earlier values being kept. Using the timestamps of 128,656 forecast runs from ten weather-model configurations, matched to the day-ahead and first intraday auction gates in Great Britain over 2022-2026, we find that selecting "the latest run issued before the gate" picks a run not yet published at the gate on more than 99% of delivery days for six of the ten configurations at one of the two gates. For GFS at the day-ahead gate, the share is 0.6% in winter and 99.8% in summer. The exposure also shifts as publication delays drift, and an action margin of a few minutes can cost a full six-hour forecast cycle. The wrongly selected inputs differ materially from those available at the gate, but their apparent accuracy gain ranges from -9% to +24% across variables, models, gates and seasons, so its effect cannot be assumed and must be measured. We argue that point-in-time correctness is a dynamic property of the data pipeline, and propose a protocol that audits it from timestamps before any model is fitted.

</details>

Abstract: Explicitly modeling periodicity has proven to be an efficient approach for time series forecasting, where the residual, obtained after removing the periodic component from the time series, is typically treated as unstructured noise. However, in this work, we show that such residuals still exhibit structured, \textit{phase-dependent} deviations, as supported by both empirical observations and theoretical analysis. This suggests that residuals should not be treated merely as unstructured noise, but instead as \textit{phase-conditioned signals} whose variations arise in both direction and magnitude. Motivated by this perspective and inspired by phase and amplitude modulation in classical signal processing, we propose \textit{Paloma}, a simple yet effective framework for modeling residual deviations. Specifically, Paloma comprises two complementary modules: \textit{a Phase Rotation module}, which performs phase-conditioned rotations to capture directional changes in the feature space, and \textit{an Amplitude Scaling module}, which applies phase-conditioned affine transformations to model magnitude variations. We further abstract the two modules into a general-purpose plugin, termed the \textit{Paloma technique}, which can be readily integrated into existing forecasting architectures to modulate input sequences in a phase-aware manner, enabling backbone models to capture phase-dependent variations in both direction and magnitude. Extensive experiments on twelve datasets demonstrate that Paloma achieves state-of-the-art and robust forecasting performance. Additional results show that the Paloma technique consistently improves a wide range of baseline models, validating its effectiveness as a general-purpose component. Our Code is available at \url{https://github.com/TSTS13/Paloma}.

</details>

Abstract: The Key-Value (KV) cache is the main memory bottleneck of long-context LLM inference. Token eviction methods such as SnapKV, H2O, AdaKV and L2Norm shrink it, but they give every transformer layer the same budget, although layers differ in which tokens they need. We present MOSAIC, a multi-objective neural architecture search that learns per-layer KV-cache budgets for any eviction method. One search maps the trade-off between average budget and task score; its best allocation transfers to every target budget without further search, and an optional budget-constrained search refines it. On both models, it reaches the score of uniform allocation with up to 4x less KV cache (75% smaller). Against EvolKV, MOSAIC explores a ~10^40 x larger search space with 4x fewer evaluations and 18% fewer GPU-hours per budget, and scores higher in 5 of 6 settings. Abstract: Masked diffusion language models (MDLMs) generate text by iteratively unmasking tokens with bidirectional context. As these models reach real-world deployment, machine unlearning becomes essential. This process aims to remove the influence of a designated "forget set" without retraining from scratch, so that sensitive, copyrighted, or restricted content can no longer be elicited while preserving retained capabilities. Masked Diffusion Unlearning (MDU) is the first publicly available unlearning method customized for these models: it tunes the model $p_θ$ to match a frozen prompt-masked anchor $p_{\mathrm{anchor}}$ on the forget set by minimizing the anchor-last divergence $\mathrm{KL}(p_θ\Vert p_{\mathrm{anchor}})$. We identify two failure modes of MDU. First, random answer masking can leave key forget tokens visible to the anchor even when the question is fully masked. Second, at a memorized (near-one-hot) prediction, the anchor-last logit gradient vanishes while the anchor-first divergence $\mathrm{KL}(p_{\mathrm{anchor}} \Vert p_θ)$ logit gradient does not. As a result, MDU training stalls on the tokens we most need to forget. Backed by theoretical and empirical analyses, we propose Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning (EMDU): anchor-first KL divergence toward a frozen anchor whose entire answer span is masked. On TOFU and RWKU benchmarks using LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B, our EMDU matches or exceeds the forgetting of MDU and adapted AR baselines, while better preserving retain utility.

</details>

Abstract: Java applications reach large language models (LLMs) through an integration framework, and rely on it to handle a failing provider: to retry what can be retried and nothing else, to wait as long as the provider asks, never to present a truncated answer as complete, and to record each failure in the application's metrics. We state these expectations as a six-clause reliability contract and measure Spring AI 2.0.1 and LangChain4j 1.21.0 against it with a scripted OpenAI-compatible provider: fifteen failure scenarios, synchronous and streaming, three repetitions. LangChain4j meets it in 47 of 77 applicable cases with its defaults and in 51 of 83 when configured explicitly, Spring AI in 72 of 77 and 76 of 83; both deliver a stream that ended without its terminator as a complete answer. We then place one implementation of the contract beneath both frameworks, at the HTTP boundary - an OkHttp interceptor for Spring AI, an HttpClient for LangChain4j, and a java.net.http transport for code without a framework - sharing a 725-line core that depends only on the JDK and the Micrometer Observation API. With it, all three clients behave identically and meet the contract in 90 of 91 cases; the remaining case is a policy trade-off between retrying a hung provider and waiting for a slow first token. Against a local model server the layer rejected none of 700 calls, and it adds a median of 32-217 microseconds to a call.

Abstract: Modeling limit order book (LOB) dynamics is central to market forecasting and execution-strategy evaluation. While diffusion models achieve strong empirical performance, existing LOB snapshot models typically lack a learned recurrent encoder and formal statistical guarantees. We introduce LOBGrad, a conditional DDPM framework for probabilistic LOB forecasting that combines an autoregressive RNN encoder with a diffusion-based generator. In particular, we establish an excess-risk oracle inequality for the one-step DDPM noise-prediction objective under exponential mixing and regularity assumptions. We further show that, under contractive dynamics, the dependence on the conditioning-window length can be reduced to a dependence on the latent-state dimension. Experiments on BTC/USDT, GOOGL, and META show that LOBGrad improves on its encoder-free diffusion ablation across all reported queue metrics and obtains competitive results against other baselines. It also reproduces the key stylized facts of LOB microstructure, and yields reasonably calibrated queue-based predictive intervals. A stylized downstream task illustrates the potential value of the generated forecasts.

Abstract: Continuous-time generative models, from diffusion models, flow matching, and now flow maps, owe their practical value to high-fidelity generation and steerability. In many applications, a ground-truth latent ``oracle'' reward $R^*$ is unavailable or expensive to evaluate, e.g., human judgments or experimental measurements. Although the oracle reward is not directly observable, it can often be approximated through multiple cheaper surrogates that, individually, provide incomplete characterizations but collectively reveal substantial information about the downstream task. In this paper, we present a method for composing such surrogates to steer a pretrained model toward the distribution tilted by an unavailable $R^*$. Given a small feedback set of oracle rankings or numerical rewards, we introduce Mixing And Reward Tilting for INference-time Improvement (MARTINI), which learns a surrogate composition for plug-and-play use with standard inference-time steering methods. Our theory characterizes surrogate information limits and relates population recovery error to ideal-target KL at small tilt strength. For preferences, the bound additionally accounts for an irreducible mismatch between the Plackett-Luce population target and the conditional mean. Experiments demonstrate improved oracle-tilt fidelity in language generation and ImageNet alignment, and improved joint pose accuracy and validity in protein-ligand cofolding, without inference-time oracle queries.

Abstract: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) has enabled sample-efficient learning of continuous control policies from high-dimensional observations through learned latent dynamics models. These models are typically trained with observation reconstruction objectives, which provide strong supervision but can bias representations toward visual fidelity leading to an undertrained prior and unreliable long-horizon predictions. We propose a stochastic gating mechanism that induces controlled partial observability during training by sampling a spatial binary mask from a Beta--Bernoulli random field and applying it to the raw observation. The gate probability is resampled at each time step, exposing the encoder to conditions ranging from near-total occlusion to near-full visibility. Integration of our method requires minimal architectural changes from existing dynamics models: the gated observation and the binary mask are channel-wise concatenated and passed to the encoder as a single augmented input, with no modifications to the broader training pipeline. We evaluate the proposed approach on a set of pixel-based continuous control tasks selected from the DeepMind Control Suite. Using model predictive control for evaluation, which relies solely on open-loop model rollouts, we show that stochastic gating improves planning performance and data efficiency across tasks. Further analysis demonstrates that performance is robust to the spatial scale of the mask, and a balanced observability during training outperforms configurations biased toward either sparser or denser observations.

Abstract: Test-time scaling (TTS) improves large language model (LLM) reasoning by sampling multiple answers, but fixed sampling budgets ignore both query difficulty and the changing energy cost of inference under GPU contention. We measure up to a 4.1-fold increase in energy for the same four-sample query as GPU load rises. We present ATEST, a resource-aware TTS framework that jointly considers the expected value of additional samples and their current GPU cost. ATEST-Online, its default mode, requires no offline calibration: it incrementally generates samples, estimates the stability of the plurality answer, monitors live GPU conditions, and learns step energy cost online to decide when to stop. ATEST-Calibrated instead selects a per-query budget using offline accuracy and cost models, favoring deployments with stable loads. We evaluate both modes on four reasoning and question-answering datasets using Phi-3-mini on an NVIDIA A100 under controlled varying load, and test transfer to an NVIDIA L40S and Qwen2.5-3B. Across the four A100 datasets, ATEST-Online answers every query without exceeding the energy budget, while achieving the highest or statistically tied accuracy and reducing sampling as load increases. On GSM8K at high load, it achieves 60.0% accuracy with no deferrals, versus 44.0% accuracy and 42.4% deferrals for ATEST-Calibrated. Ablations show that online cost learning drives adaptation to load. Including offline profiling, ATEST-Online uses 42 times less GPU time and 48 times less energy than ATEST-Calibrated for the evaluated workload. Online control trades higher tail latency and lower throughput on short tasks for robustness under varying load; offline calibration remains competitive under stable conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Time series are ubiquitous across different domains, and the growing use of synthetic data for privacy-preserving sharing, data augmentation, and stress testing makes time series generation increasingly important. Early generative approaches are predominantly autoregressive, which can suffer from low sample diversity, error accumulation, and difficulty modeling long-range dependencies. In this paper, we introduce FracTS, a hierarchical, coarse-to-fine autoregressive model for multivariate time series generation. Inspired by fractal generative modeling developed for images, FracTS adapts the paradigm from 2D to 1D sequences by learning a multi-scale representation that captures global trajectory structure at coarse scales and local dynamics at fine scales while following the autoregressive nature of time series. In contrast to unstructured images, time series are structured, allowing easy automated or designated temporal feature processing optimized for the model, and global (static) feature extraction describing the instances that generate the corresponding time series in the collection. We incorporate them in the generation process to further improve the generation quality. The design naturally handles multivariate dependencies and improves long-horizon temporal coherence without requiring fixed-length inputs. Experiments on five datasets (two conditional, three unconditional) conducted against seven baselines show that FracTS consistently outperforms baselines across marginal distribution and predictive ML utility, and correlation preservation in the unconditional setting. Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is increasingly proposed as a fast solver for unit commitment (UC), but standalone agents remain far more expensive than mixed-integer linear programming (MILP). Consistent with this, injecting exploration noise during fine-tuning enlarged a lean policy's fleet, a reward without shaping terms did not remove the over-commitment, and under their training noise the agents outperformed the rule subjected to comparable noise. Imitating the MILP solutions and fine-tuning conservatively brought PPO within 2.6% of the optimum, and these policies had the lowest penalised cost of any evaluated (about 5%). Fixing the fine-tuned policy's confident decisions made the MILP 3.8-8.5 times faster within 0.10-0.25% of the best solution, with a smaller worst case than an imitation-only policy fixing as many variables (1.1% against 8.9%). Because dispatch method and benchmark scope alone shift the reported optimality ratio by up to 3.6% and 9%, we propose a reporting protocol.

Increasing underprediction on progressively more extreme observed subsets can also arise through outcome selection, even for an ideal conditional-mean forecast. They caution against generalizing the extrapolation failure reported for deterministic AI forecasts to probabilistic systems, and motivate common verification conventions that separate comparative tail skill, performance on realized extremes, and probabilistic calibration.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are becoming a powerful alternative to conventional tabular learning, adapting to new datasets in context without task-specific training. Current architectures, however, face a trade-off: models that preserve cell-level representations are expensive because they repeatedly attend across rows and columns, while faster models collapse each row into a fixed embedding and lose this fine-grained state. We introduce Factorized Tabular Foundation Models (FTFM), which preserve cell representations while factorizing their computation through one contextual latent per row and one per column. Cells no longer attend to one another directly; instead, each cell is updated through the interaction of its row and column latents, reducing repeated attention while retaining feature-specific information. FTFM achieves competitive predictive performance in tabular classification on major benchmarks such as TabArena and BeyondArena.

</details>

Abstract: Identifying which visual interventions interact in a vision-language model requires comparing their joint and separate effects. The resulting four-output difference can be small enough for rounding error to obscure it. At each selected linear projection, FIL decomposes each candidate's input into a native state, two single-intervention components, and a full-dimensional mixed residual. It projects the single-intervention components at higher precision and reuses the same numerical contributions in the joint candidates, while retaining every mixed residual and nonlinear trajectory. We derive a local cancellation identity and a conditional bound on error propagation through a smooth nonlinearity. One image accounts for most of the absolute gain in each COCO study. Higher-accuracy multi-product BF16 arithmetic on all candidates gives lower aggregate error at similar total query times. Single-product BF16 already recovers the reference choices in both candidate-selection tasks we tested. On these development inputs, FIL improves interaction fidelity at a fixed nominal precision budget. Its practical value depends on a task that needs the extra fidelity.

Abstract: Irregularly sampled multivariate time series arise in many real-world domains such as healthcare, climate science, and large-scale monitoring systems. Characterized by asynchronous observations and heterogeneity across variables, such time series pose significant challenges for forecasting. In this work, we introduce Cross-Variable Temporal Attention (CVTA), a short-term, continuous-time forecasting framework that learns prediction functions from its compact, interpretable state summarizing recent temporal dynamics across variables. CVTA uses Variable-Conditioned Embeddings to project heterogeneous variables into variable-specific latent spaces, and applies temporal attention to capture cross-variable dependencies from this state. Abstract: Alignment of large language models typically relies on pairwise preference feedback, though ranked feedback can capture more comprehensive information across multiple responses within a single annotation. However, ranked-preference alignment is underexplored in practice compared to simpler pairwise approaches, potentially resulting in suboptimal behavior. Concurrently, recent research has conceptualized large language model alignment objectives as divergence estimators between aligned (preferred) and unaligned (less-preferred) response distributions, resulting in a family of objectives with an interpretable theoretical foundation and strong empirical performance. This work unifies these perspectives by introducing $f$-RPO, a $f$-divergence objective for ranked preference optimization that extends divergence-based alignment to ranked feedback and recovers pairwise preference alignment as a special case. Our theoretical analysis establishes rank-order consistency, showing that $f$-RPO preserves the underlying preference ordering at convergence. Empirical results indicate that total-variation and reverse-KL variants of the proposed objective consistently outperform ranked-alignment baselines in both ranking and generation quality across Qwen, Llama, & Gemma models ranging from 0.5B to 8B parameters.

Abstract: Policy learning from observational data is fundamental to applications ranging from personalized medicine to welfare-program targeting, but standard approaches optimize empirical performance and may fail when the deployment distribution differs from the training distribution. We study \emph{distributionally-robust policy learning}: given observational data, we seek a policy $π\in Π$ that maximizes the worst-case policy value over an $f$-divergence ball of radius $ρ$ around the data-generating distribution. We propose an estimator built on cross-fitted doubly-robust scores for the conditional treatment effect, paired with a DRO solver that handles \texttt{KL}, $χ^2$, and \texttt{CVaR} ambiguity sets. We establish two convergence guarantees. First, the estimated policy attains the standard $\widetilde{O}(n^{-1/2})$ rate on its DRO regret, and inherits the doubly-robust property -- the rate continues to hold whenever either the outcome model or the propensity model is consistently estimated, even at slow nonparametric rates. We then propose a sample-splitting bias-correction algorithm derived from the Lagrangian dual, and show that under a Tsybakov-style margin condition on the treatment-effect, the estimator attains a fast rate of $\widetilde{O}(n^{-(1+α)/(2+α)})$ , interpolating between $\widetilde{O}(1/\sqrt{n})$ and $\widetilde{O}(1/n)$ as the margin parameter $α$ ranges over $[0,\infty)$. To our knowledge, this is the first work to incorporate a margin condition into the analysis of distributionally-robust policy learning, and the first to obtain fast rates in this setting. Empirically, on synthetic and semi-synthetic experiments calibrated to a welfare-to-work program, distributional robustness yields meaningful improvements over empirical welfare maximization when training and test distributions differ.

</details>

Abstract: Solubility depends on intermolecular interactions between solutes and solvents, and is central to drug discovery, chemical and materials process engineering. Its prediction requires out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization to unseen solutes, solvents, and solvent mixtures, where supervised methods suffer from data scarcity, and existing self-supervised methods do not explicitly learn chemically meaningful relations. Learning chemically meaningful relational information without labels can alleviate data scarcity and improve OOD generalization, but remains challenging. We introduce Prior Relational Information for Self-supervised Modeling (PRISM) and apply it to solubility OOD generalization. PRISM is a label-free pretraining framework that converts expert-defined chemical priors into relational teachers. For solubility prediction, it trains four encoders to preserve the population-level relational information from priors capturing hydrophobic, electrostatic, polarizability, and substructural effects, using Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence to match distance-based neighborhood distributions during pretraining. Across scaffold, solute, and solvent OOD settings, PRISM matches or surpasses supervised and self-supervised baselines. Under mixed-solvent OOD, freezing the four PRISM encoders and training a lightweight fusion-and-composition head outperforms strong baselines and substantially reduces run-to-run variation. Ablations and control studies confirm that gains stem from chemically meaningful relational structures baked into the encoders by PRISM. We expect this strategy of turning relational priors into self-supervision signals to extend beyond solubility prediction to domains where chemically or biologically meaningful relational priors are available.

</details>

Abstract: Safety risks from fine-tuning are commonly characterized by the harmfulness of the training data:harmful fine-tuning is considered dangerous, while benign fine-tuning is often assumed to preservealignment. We argue that this view misses a more fundamental factor-the behavioral transition inducedby supervision. Harmful fine-tuning and safety-degrading benign fine-tuning can both be understoodas learning the same refusal-to-compliance transition, but on different data distributions. We studywhether such a transition learned on benign inputs can generalize to harmful requests. We constructbenign examples that an aligned model initially tends to refuse and pair them with their originalharmless responses, creating a discrepancy between the model's initial refusal and the supervisedanswer. Fine-tuning on these examples increases harmful compliance, but only when two conditionshold together: the discrepancy is large enough, and it cannot be resolved through a narrow surface-levelshortcut. With the induced discrepancy matched, varying only whether it is localized changes harmfulcompliance by an order of magnitude; raising the discrepancy does not help while a shortcut remainsavailable, and removing the shortcut does not help while the discrepancy is small. Building on thisobservation, we introduce SAFEOFF, a dataset of benign instructions rewritten to raise the alignedmodel's refusal likelihood and paired with their original answers. Across three aligned LLMs and threesafety benchmarks, SAFEOFF substantially weakens safety alignment despite containing no explicitharmful supervision, suggesting that fine-tuning safety depends not only on what data a model sees, butalso on which behavioral boundary the supervision moves.

</details>

Abstract: LLMs "reason" by externalizing intermediate steps as tokens through chain-of-thought (CoT), yet even frontier reasoning models hit abrupt performance cliffs on long-horizon tasks. Implicit reasoning (reasoning within a single forward pass) is useful during CoT generation, as the model can "search ahead" to decide the correct next move without explicit search. Models with weak implicit reasoning are more likely to commit to wrong paths and must attempt to recover via backtracking, while models with strong implicit reasoning identify the correct proof strategy directly, shortening the CoT. However, prior work shows LLMs struggle to reason implicitly beyond shallow depths, with no relief from scale. We investigate whether curriculum learning can extend this depth by fine-tuning on examples with progressively increasing vocabulary size and lookahead (the number of steps a model must search ahead to identify the correct next move). At matched compute, curriculum-trained models achieve lookaheads where no-curriculum training fails. Qwen3-0.6B reaches 98% accuracy on problems with lookahead up to 256 with curriculum, compared to 45% without. However, progress slows as fine-tuning continues, following a saturating exponential that suggests a maximum intrinsic lookahead to which models can learn to reason implicitly. Larger models reach higher fitted ceilings, fine-tuning pretrained models far outpaces training from scratch, and curriculum step sizes that are too large stall progression. Finally, the capability acquired on synthetic data transfers to natural language reasoning, with curriculum-trained models outperforming models trained without curriculum at matched compute on 11 natural language reasoning benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: State space models (SSMs) combine fast inference and fixed state size with competitive language-modeling performance. Recently, state souping has been proposed as a procedure whereby an SSM independently encodes chunks of tokens like documents, tool definitions, or memory records. The resulting state encodings may be cached and recombined to cheaply construct a context for downstream queries. However, both state souping and vanilla SSMs deteriorate on long, crowded contexts, limiting their utility for long-horizon domains. To address this issue, we propose weighted state souping, a simple method that learns query-dependent weights for composing cached states. The resulting models improve long-context performance substantially across both single- and multi-hop document question answering, needle-in-a-haystack retrieval, variable tracking, and tool use. Our method can be further adapted into an efficient sparse variant, yielding near-constant encoding cost with respect to context length while preserving (and sometimes improving) performance. We also present simple theoretical arguments that illustrate why souping works and when it may fail, and discuss informally the advantages of weighted souping. Weighted state souping thus combines reusable context representations with selective retrieval, offering an efficient approach to language modeling over large collections of documents, tools, and other forms of structured context that increasingly dominate modern agentic workloads.

</details>

Abstract: Source labels tell us where a sample came from, but not how that source oriented its coordinates. In a sparse Gaussian multi-source model with source-specific sign gauges, that distinction changes the learning problem. Without calibration, pooling across sources erases the mean signal, and the source-labelled experiment reduces to sparse PCA. With task-independent relative-gauge metadata, synchronization aligns sources and the same observations become a sparse mean problem. We formalize the value of metadata through AlignedMass, which quantifies how much first-order signal a metadata-induced alignment restores. Source labels create only limited AlignedMass, whereas valid calibration can create AlignedMass proportional to the full sample size. This yields a statistical separation in micro-source regimes and, under standard sparse-PCA hardness assumptions, a computational separation as well. Abstract: Layer-level pruning is attractive for large language model (LLM) deployment as it reduces the model size while preserving a dense architecture that can be efficiently executed on standard hardware. Current methods typically follow a one-shot paradigm, which first removes selected layers to reach a target model size and then performs additional training to recover model capabilities. However, pruning directly to the target size can substantially degrade reasoning capabilities, making subsequent recovery more difficult under a limited training budget. Moreover, this paradigm separates layer selection from recovery, limiting the use of recovery information to guide selection and potentially hindering the recovery of reasoning capabilities. To address these limitations, we propose LOOP, a Layer-level Recovery-Oriented On-Policy Pruning framework that interleaves pruning and recovery across multiple shots. In each shot, LOOP removes a single layer and performs short on-policy distillation, mitigating the abrupt degradation in reasoning capabilities associated with one-shot pruning. To guide layer selection in subsequent shots, LOOP reuses on-policy trajectories and optimization signals from recovery to jointly assess each remaining layer's reasoning contribution and recovery sensitivity, thereby making layer selection recovery-aware. Evaluations on several benchmarks show that LOOP consistently outperforms one-shot baselines in average accuracy across different model scales and pruning ratios.

</details>

Abstract: As deep neural networks are required to continuously adapt to evolving data, class-incremental learning (CIL) has become an active research area. However, most existing works primarily focus on improving accuracy while overlooking confidence calibration, which measures the trustworthiness of the model's predictions. Although temperature scaling (TS) is effective for static calibration, it has rarely been explored in continual settings where data distributions evolve over time. In this paper, we introduce Multi-Group Temperature Scaling for Continual Calibration (MT-CC), a novel post-hoc calibration framework that addresses the asymmetric calibration behavior in CIL, where the rate of confidence changes does not align with the rate of accuracy degradation across tasks, leading to different levels of miscalibration. Our key contribution is to design multiple temperature parameters through a lightweight grouping network that separates samples by confidence statistics and task-aware signals in CIL. Furthermore, a Groupwise Difference-between-Confidence-and-Accuracy (GDCA) regularization is incorporated to promote intra-group consistency. Experiments demonstrate that MT-CC significantly improves calibration performance while maintaining accuracy, offering a principled framework for reliable continual learning.

Although preference-based fine-tuning can leverage suboptimal rollouts to improve performance, existing approaches heavily rely on expensive human annotations. Abstract: Linear stochastic approximation (SA) is widely used to estimate fixed points from noisy observations, but most finite-time analyses assume that the underlying operator remains fixed. We study linear SA with time-varying affine operators, where the fixed points change over time and the observation noise may be correlated across rounds. For constant-stepsize updates, we establish a bound on the cumulative expected squared distance between the iterates and the corresponding fixed points. The bound accounts for the initial error, observation noise, and changes in the fixed points over time. We also establish lower bounds for scalar Gaussian models, showing that, under specified conditions, a suitably chosen constant stepsize achieves the smallest possible worst-case cumulative error up to constant factors, even compared with estimators that use future observations. These results further quantify the effect of temporal correlation on estimation accuracy. Since choosing an appropriate stepsize generally requires knowledge of the noise level and how much the fixed points change, we develop an adaptive method that uses an additional bounded, unbiased observation of the fixed point to achieve an almost optimal algorithm without needing the knowledge of the noise level and the fixed point variation over time. Finally, we apply our results to value-function estimation using synchronous tabular temporal-difference learning in changing environments, assuming generative-model access and a common invariant distribution.

Abstract: Diffusion language models accelerate generation by predicting multiple tokens in parallel, but factorized denoisers treat simultaneous predictions as conditionally independent. Decoding heuristics reduce the resulting errors by controlling which tokens are committed, but leave the underlying distribution factorized. Joint modeling addresses the source of the problem, yet existing methods attach separately learned layers to frozen denoisers, leaving the backbone unadapted to the joint objective and the coupling parameters fixed across contexts. We propose Codpula, a context-parameterized denoiser combining a Transformer with a tractable probabilistic inference layer. Given a partially masked input, the Transformer produces token potentials that shape per-position predictions and compact gates that modulate the layer's coupling parameters, yielding a context-dependent joint distribution while retaining exact conditional inference. We jointly optimize both components under a conditional denoising objective using a dual-flow multiplicative update that preserves valid probabilistic parameters. At inference, Codpula supports standard heuristics and uses conditional queries to adapt commitments to dependencies among proposed tokens without extra Transformer evaluations within a step. Codpula sets new state-of-the-art results across a broad suite of reasoning benchmarks while advancing the accuracy-latency Pareto frontier in mathematical reasoning and code generation.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has become a central tool for large language model (LLM) post-training, where policy gradient methods are routinely deployed off-policy, even though vanilla policy gradient assumes on-policy sampling. We study when such off-policy deployment is justified, and what roles its core ingredients, including importance sampling, KL regularization, and baselines, play in handling the resulting distribution shift. We show that for a general class of off-policy policy gradient updates, consistency, in the sense that the optimal policy remains a stationary point, requires one of two structures at the optimal policy: trajectory-level importance weighting, which suffers from the well-known curse of horizon, or KL regularization with a baseline equal to the optimal soft value, instantiated by the grouped mean. Focusing on the KL route, we identify it with the group-relative policy gradient (GRPG) update, establish an equivalence to a trajectory-level Bellman residual minimization objective, and obtain a finite-sample regret bound under all-policy coverage. This also gives a theoretical account of a variant of the empirically successful GRPO objective. We further uncover a hidden role of baselines in the off-policy setting: a constant shift in the baseline implements pessimism, the standard remedy in offline RL for partial data coverage. We instantiate this via the asymmetric REINFORCE objective, and connect it to the game-theoretic offline RL framework, showing a regret bound with single-policy coverage. Together, these results offer a unified theoretical view of off-policy policy gradient in LLM post-training and connect it to classical ideas in offline RL.

</details>

Abstract: Constraint pruning saves queries to a large language model (LLM) or another noisy oracle by skipping each yes/no question implied by answers accepted earlier, for example through transitivity. Entity-resolution systems and LLM-based rankers use this idea. However, their analyses typically assume a correct oracle. We argue that pruning should be judged by its risk when the oracle errs, since an accepted wrong answer spreads to every question it implies. When each answer is wrong independently with small probability $\varepsilon$, the first-order risk is set by how many facts one wrong answer corrupts on average. We consider two kinds of constraints. For implication rules, as in taxonomies, pruning in a random order adds no first-order risk: a wrong answer that would change $k$ facts is accepted with probability $1/k$, so it costs one fact on average. Rules with $s$ premises break this balance: a wrong answer at a premise shared by $r$ rules can cost order $r^{1-1/s}$ facts. For entity resolution with $m$ records of one entity, the first-order risk equals $\varepsilon$ times the Wiener index (the total pairwise distance) of the spanning tree built by the query order. Pivot orders, which first compare one record with all others, amplify errors by less than 2 and are optimal; random orders by order $m^{1/3}$; the common most-similar-first order is the worst in one dimension and, for large $m$, worse than random in two. With several entities, the cost of wrongly merging two does not depend on how many others exist. Simulations agree with every predicted constant within sampling error.

</details>

Abstract: Scientific model discovery requires proposing candidate models of an unknown system and designing experiments that discriminate among them. However, LLM-based approaches typically rely on in-context learning to predict outcomes and select experiments, lacking explicit model evaluation to systematically guide hypothesis search, while Bayesian approaches enable principled model comparison but are confined to a predefined candidate set which results in limited search spaces. To this end, we introduce BMD, which closes the loop between LLM-driven hypothesis generation and Bayesian experimental design. Specifically, an LLM proposes model families, while a numerical engine fits their parameters, weighs them by model evidence, and averages their predictions. Then, BMD chooses the next experimental conditions by maximizing the joint expected information gain over families and their parameters, while feeding the updated beliefs and residual diagnostics back to the LLM to guide its next proposals. Across four scientific discovery benchmarks, BMD achieves lowest final prediction error than in-context learning and LLM-based scientific agents while recovering exact functional forms more often, with ablations supporting numerical feedback and the joint objective.

</details>

Abstract: Catastrophic forgetting is a central obstacle in single-stage fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs) on a new task, especially when the original pretraining data are unavailable. Synthetic data offers a practical mitigation path, but candidate pools are often noisy and redundant, making effective selection essential. Existing methods often rely on indirect proxy signals whose alignment with actual training benefit can be limited, while utility--diversity selection over large candidate pools remains computationally demanding. To address these challenges, we propose NAvigator-guided Data Selection (NADS), a fine-tuning framework for LLMs. NADS first follows the target fine-tuning recipe without preservation regularization to obtain a navigator model, whose deviation from the pretrained model exposes recipe-induced capability drift. It then uses the predictive divergence between the navigator and the pretrained model to score forgetting-aware utility for each synthetic candidate, and constructs a constraint set through an efficient utility--diversity selection strategy. Finally, NADS fine-tunes on the new-task data while distilling pretrained behavior on the selected constraints to preserve general capabilities. Experiments show that NADS delivers a stronger balance between new-task performance and general capability preservation, while reducing utility--diversity selection overhead. Code at: https://github.com/zjh-vinky/NADS.

Abstract: Self-consistency answers a question by generating multiple responses and selecting the most frequent answer. Its evaluation requires both correctness labels and information about which responses agree; correctness labels alone generally determine neither voting accuracy nor the benefit of additional votes. When answer-category counts are available, we introduce Plurality-U, a count-based dynamic program that exactly averages voting accuracy over all fixed-size subsets, eliminating finite-pool Monte Carlo error. Abstract: In-context reinforcement learning (ICRL) enables agents to adapt to new tasks without parameter updates, relying on memory to retain what they learn from experience. For agents operating continually, existing approaches using memory trade off bounded cost against access to past experience. Approaches using growing memories, such as full-history attention and accumulating summaries, incur storage and per-decision costs that increase with task length. Fixed-size memories bound storage but restrict access: sliding windows discard older steps, while recurrent networks compress even recent interactions into a hidden state. We introduce Recurrent Summary Memory (RSM), a Transformer policy that attends exactly to a short working buffer of recent interactions and carries a fixed-capacity summary of earlier experience. When the buffer fills, RSM rewrites the summary from its previous contents and the buffer, then clears the buffer, so deployment memory and per-decision cost stay $O(1)$ in task length. On long-running partially observable tasks, frozen RSM policies sustain their adapted performance for eight times the training horizon with about 1% of full-history memory, ending ahead of every evaluated baseline while growing memories deteriorate. When the task configuration changes mid-run, RSM re-adapts better than growing and recurrent memories. Clearing the summary or blocking the policy's access to it removes its benefit, while swapping in another task's summary steers behaviour towards that task. Together, these results show that our fixed-capacity summary can sustain in-context adaptation far beyond the training horizon.

</details>

Abstract: Language-model agents often reason before they act, and the value of monitoring that reasoning depends on how it relates to their actions. We ask how an agent's actions depend on its reasoning when the correct action depends on a changing state. In a small file-system task, background events announce which file's lock has been toggled but not its new value. The agent therefore has to reconstruct the current locks from its history before it can move files safely. With Qwen3-8B and Qwen3.5-9B, we separate two roles of reasoning: generating it before each action, and retaining earlier reasoning in the prompt. Most of the reduction in lock-violating commands comes from reasoning written at the moment of action. Retaining earlier reasoning adds little to rule-following, although it helps one model complete the task. The benefit depends on what the reasoning says. Filler text, reasoning written for another history and an accurate table of the state do not reproduce it. When we give the agent reasoning written for a history that differs in one lock value, the agent acts on that reasoning even against its own history. Without reasoning, linear probes recover the state from the model's activations about as well as a text classifier recovers it from the transcript. However, decoding accuracy does not predict which interventions change the agent's actions. In this setting, the benefit of reasoning for rule-following comes mainly from generating it at each step. The state that can be decoded without reasoning is a limited guide to how the agent acts.

</details>

Abstract: We study the problem of non-stationary bandits without knowledge of the environmental changes. We propose Detection Augmented Learning (DAL) a black-box paradigm that comprises three modular components: a bandit algorithm, a change detector, and a forced exploration schedule. This modular design enables its applicability across a broad spectrum of bandit problems, from classical to highly structured settings. First, we provide theoretical insights into DAL's strong empirical performance: a single regret bound shows that DAL transfers the regret of its stationary input to the piecewise stationary setting, attaining or improving the best known bounds in every setting considered. We further identify conditions under which DAL performs well in drifting environments. Then, we showcase DAL's practical performance and versatility through extensive experimentation in which DAL consistently surpasses all state-of-the-art methods, both prior-free and prior-based, across diverse non-stationary scenarios in nine bandit settings. Finally, we investigate the effectiveness of the forced exploration and show that a bandit-driven strategy improves performance and shortens detection delay.

Abstract: In reservoir computing, performance depends on whether the fixed dynamics of the reservoir suit the task, and the appropriate memory length and frequency response differ across tasks. The reservoir settings are found by a search on each task, and driving the reservoir with the input and collecting its states for every candidate setting dominates the cost of the search. In a reservoir with a linear recurrence, the dynamics decompose into modes, one per eigenvalue, and the eigenvalue distribution becomes a design variable. We propose the Sector-Ring Linear Reservoir (SRLR), in which the eigenvalue angles are fixed at evenly spaced positions around a circle and one decay timescale, chosen from a few values, is assigned to each sector of consecutive angles. To keep the scale of each mode's state equal across candidates, the input weights are scaled using the power spectrum of the training input. To reduce the cost of the search, the states of the modes for each decay-timescale value are computed once and reused across candidates. On prediction and reconstruction benchmarks built from chaotic systems, the SRLR achieved, in all eight cases, a lower mean test error than a reference model in which all modes share one decay timescale, and was competitive with nonlinear echo state network and leaky-integrator echo state network baselines. The selected decay-timescale profiles changed with the prediction target and horizon. The state reuse reduced the number of computed mode trajectories in the search by 99.8% relative to per-candidate recomputation.

</details>

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) is a distributed paradigm for Internet of Things (IoT) edge networks. However, deploying Vision-Language Models (VLMs) over wireless edge networks presents distinct challenges: large model parameters increase uplink transmission latency over bandwidth-constrained fading channels, and non-independent and identically distributed (Non-IID) data exacerbates client drift. Existing feature alignment solutions often introduce auxiliary computations that consume time slots allocated for wireless parameter transmission, increasing the likelihood of straggler dropouts under system delay constraints. To address these issues, we propose DPGR-GCAM, a communication-efficient federated domain generalization framework. By employing a dual-parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) architecture, we design a channel-aware tiered transmission mechanism that adaptively truncates payloads to maximize node survival rates under fading conditions. To mitigate client drift, we introduce the Gradient-Conflict Aware Modulation (GCAM) mechanism. GCAM operates within the low-dimensional feature manifold to dynamically resolve multi-objective optimization conflicts with minimal edge computation latency, thereby reducing computation-induced network outages. Experiments demonstrate that DPGR-GCAM improves cross-domain generalization and sustains convergence under network fading scenarios with a 10\% client participation rate.

</details>

We test this on their procedural counting task with Qwen3-4B-Base and LoRA, comparing GRPO to rejection-sampling fine-tuning (RFT) on the same prompts, verifier and token cap. Six primary arms cross the two methods with three prompt sets (Easy, Mixed, and a Curated subset of prompts the base model sometimes solves, which makes GRPO's implicit filter explicit). We use three seeds per arm. Hypotheses and falsifiers were registered before training. To rule out a poorly tuned baseline, RFT receives a 27-run hyperparameter sweep, while GRPO uses Bauer et al.'s recipe unchanged. GRPO outperforms RFT at every prompt set: by 10.9 points on held-out test problems with curated prompts, and, in post hoc comparisons with matched rollout budgets, by 8.4 and 9.2 points on the easy and mixed prompts. On test the advantage survives the least favorable treatment of truncated completions; out of distribution, truncation prevents a conclusion. Curating prompts does not help RFT (-2.7 points). Mixed-difficulty data helps RFT (+3.7 points), while for GRPO the evidence is inconclusive. A secondary iterated-RFT arm, which resamples from the current policy, recovers 3.2 of the 10.9-point gap (about 30%). These are controlled measurements on one synthetic task family and one base model with three seeds per arm.

</details>
