Abstract: Training high-quality diffusion models is computationally expensive, and inference-time guidance often adds further sampling cost. In this work, we introduce Swift, a simple yet effective self-guided training method that uses a frozen weak denoiser to improve a stronger model without adding inference overhead. For each noisy input, Swift extrapolates the clean regression target away from the weak denoiser's prediction, amplifying components that remain underfit. The weak guide can be an early training checkpoint, requires no backpropagation during main-model training, and is discarded afterward. The resulting model retains the original sampler and supports both conditional and unconditional generation without label dropout or an unconditional branch. We explain this mechanism through a spectral analysis of finite-time population gradient flow in an RKHS. With an early-stopped weak guide, we prove that a nonempty range of extrapolation strengths yields strictly lower posterior-mean error than matched standard training, despite shifting the regression target away from the posterior mean. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-256 demonstrate improvements across model scales and representations. In one ImageNet-256 configuration, Swift surpasses the baseline's best FID using roughly half the total training compute, including guide preparation and training overhead. On RAEv2, it achieves FID 1.15 without inference-time guidance and 0.99 with guidance. These results establish target extrapolation as an effective approach to weak-to-strong diffusion training with efficient single-model inference.

</details>


### [277] [ACE-Trader: Adversarial Co-Evolution for Generator-Critic Trading Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25847)
*Chang Zhou,Jiarun Zhou,Xingtong Yu,Hong Cheng,Hui Zhang,Xinming Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ACE‑Trader, a new framework for jointly training an LLM trader and its red‑team critic via context‑dependent memory selectors and co‑evolution, beats all 22 baselines on U.S. stock trading metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable an LLM‑based trader and its red‑team critic to learn jointly from experience, addressing the challenge that improving individual decisions does not guarantee co‑evolution, and that useful experiences are context‑dependent and hard to attribute to each agent.

Method: 1. Build separate context‑dependent memory selectors for the Trader and Red‑Team that pick relevant past trading records. 2. Provide both selectors with shared feedback from net‑return differences between memory‑assisted and memory‑free trading, with online updates emphasizing recent outcomes. 3. Introduce a co‑evolution mechanism linking the agents via shared trading records: use these to evaluate Red‑Team risk assessments and the impact of Trader revisions, then assign derived experiences to each agent’s memory.

Result: On U.S. stock data, ACE‑Trader surpasses 22 active baselines, achieving the highest cumulative return, best Sharpe ratio, and lowest maximum drawdown among both active methods and passive benchmarks.

Conclusion: Co‑evolution of trader and critic through context‑dependent memory selection and shared feedback enables superior trading performance, proving the effectiveness of ACE‑Trader.

Abstract: Generator--critic trading systems pair an LLM-based trader that makes trading decisions with a red team agent that evaluates these decisions and provides feedback for revision. However, improving individual trading decisions does not ensure that both agents learn from experience and improve together over time. This raises an important question: how can the trader and red-Team select useful experience and learn from each other's evolving behavior? On one hand, experience that benefits decisions under one market condition may become ineffective or harmful under another. On the other hand, trading outcomes reflect both agents' actions, making it difficult to derive appropriate experience for each role. In this work, we propose ACE-Trader, an adversarial co-evolution trading framework. Specifically, we first develop separate context-dependent memory selectors that learn to identify useful experience for the current trading decision. Both selectors receive shared feedback from net-return differences between complete memory-assisted and memory-free trading pipelines, with online updates emphasizing recent outcomes. We further design a co-evolution mechanism that links role-specific memories through shared trading records. We use these records to evaluate the Red-Team's risk assessments and the effects of the Trader's revisions against realized market outcomes, then derive experience for their respective memories. By linking this experience to the corresponding records, we enable the Trader to improve its proposals based on prior critiques and the Red-Team to adjust its review focus as the Trader's behavior changes. Experiments on U.S. stock trading show that ACE-Trader outperforms all 22 active baselines in cumulative return and achieves the highest Sharpe ratio and lowest maximum drawdown across both active methods and passive benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Rubrics are widely used to evaluate open-ended question answering by breaking overall quality into explicit criteria. These explicit criteria make rubrics a rich source of learning guidance, yet their potential for guiding the training process has been largely overlooked. Existing methods use rubrics mainly as direct feedback signals, without explicitly modeling the capabilities behind their criteria. This leaves their capability structure largely unused in guiding the training process. We introduce Rubric-Structured Reinforcement Learning (RSRL), an RL framework that unlocks the full potential of rubrics by exploiting their structure throughout the training process, rather than treating them simply as direct reward signals. At its core, RSRL builds on three key designs: (1) Rubric-to-Capability Structuring first recovers the capability structure hidden in rubric criteria, (2) Self-Sufficient Capability Learning then uses this structure to bootstrap underdeveloped capabilities into effective learning, (3) Capability-Residual Credit Routing finally preserves capability-specific gains during optimization. Experiments show that RSRL consistently improves both overall performance and underdeveloped capabilities, boosting downstream scientific question-answering performance by up to 7.43 over the initial policy and 7.60 over non-rubric structured RL baselines, showing that reinforcement learning can substantially benefit from the capability structure encoded in rubrics.

</details>

Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) learns from pairwise preferences through a logistic objective whose score-difference parameterization follows the Bradley--Terry model. We study how failure of this modeling assumption limits DPO, distinguishing structural misspecification from limited model capacity and finite-sample estimation. Our main contribution is a characterization of structural approximation error through the triangle-curl of the preference log-odds, which vanishes exactly when preferences admit a Bradley--Terry representation. We upper bound the achievable excess logistic risk by a score-class approximation term and a triangle-curl term, and establish a lower bound proportional to triangle-curl under additional boundedness assumptions. For ReLU score models, including clipped models, we derive explicit approximation rates showing that increasing capacity can eliminate the score-approximation term while leaving the structural obstruction unchanged. We further establish finite-sample bounds for clipped empirical risk minimizers that separate network approximation, structural misspecification, and estimation error. We place these results within a surrogate-risk framework by identifying contextual preference accuracy (CPA) as a natural measure of agreement between preference judgments and changes in pairwise probability ratios relative to the reference policy. Standard logistic consistency and calibration results connect DPO's objective to this policy property and transfer our excess-risk upper bounds to CPA, without treating CPA as a complete measure of generated-response quality.

</details>

Abstract: Allocating limited trainable capacity across convolutional layers is a central challenge in parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) of pretrained three-dimensional (3D) networks. We propose Residual-Guided Adaptive Tucker Rank Allocation, which jointly selects input- and output-channel ranks under a fixed adapter-parameter budget while preserving the spatial kernel modes. To guide rank selection, we learn task-specific proxy updates from downstream training data and approximate them using candidate Tucker rank pairs. A perturbation analysis bounds the output discrepancy between the fixed proxy network and its Tucker approximation in terms of layerwise reconstruction residuals, motivating residual-based allocation. We normalize each squared residual by the corresponding proxy-update energy to compare relative approximation errors across layers. Dynamic programming minimizes their sum under a global budget constraint that accounts for both channel factors and the Tucker core. This yields a globally optimal allocation over the specified candidate rank pairs. We evaluate the method on the Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (ADNI) cohort for predicting conversion from mild cognitive impairment to Alzheimer's disease within 36 months, using hippocampal segmentation pretraining. Across five random seeds, the proposed method achieves a mean area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.7706, compared with 0.7239 for fixed-ratio Tucker adaptation and 0.7430 for full fine-tuning. The total number of trainable parameters is limited to approximately 0.618 million, accounting for only 1.45% of the full model. Adaptive Tucker rank allocation thus provides an effective approach to parameter-efficient transfer learning in 3D medical imaging.

</details>

Abstract: We study how words in the context support or oppose a language model's response and present DeltaTrace, a novel attribution framework that explains the effect of input tokens on a fixed model response. DeltaTrace measures how the response's log-probability changes when the context tokens being explained are masked. To distribute this difference among source tokens, it constructs operator-specific local rules for finite changes and composes them backward through the computational graph using effective secant slopes. Each token is assigned a signed attribution as the inner product of its embedding displacement and the corresponding backward-propagated slope. Local conservation makes these contributions sum to the measured score difference and preserves their meaning when aggregated over sentences or passages. DeltaTrace supports both attention and linear-attention models, with auxiliary memory growing linearly with sequence length at fixed model dimensions and chunk size. On the evaluated attention model, DeltaTrace achieves the lowest deletion-faithfulness scores (RISE and MAS) on most retrieval and reasoning tasks and the highest needle-in-a-haystack Recall, exceeding FlashTrace by 5.67-24.33 percentage points. On the evaluated hybrid attention and linear-attention model, DeltaTrace lowers RISE on 9 tasks and MAS on 12 relative to FlashTrace.

</details>

Abstract: Unlike on-policy distillation (OPD), which depends on a separate teacher model, on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) leverages a single language model acting simultaneously as the student and the teacher, with the teacher conditioned on privileged information. Despite this architectural simplicity, synchronous rollout generation dominates overall training time, creating a severe systems bottleneck when scaling OPSD. To address this, we introduce Asynchronous On-Policy Self-Distillation (AsyncOPSD), a framework that decouples rollout generation from learner updates to improve training throughput. Since the teacher and student share parameters, AsyncOPSD eliminates the multi-model coordination overhead inherent to OPD architectures in asynchronous training. As the student policy evolves, buffered trajectories generated by older policies become stale, potentially degrading performance. To mitigate this, we introduce a distillation objective based on staleness-aware policy control and token-level entropy weighting. Across three model sizes and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, AsyncOPSD improves the training throughput by $1.96$--$2.25\times$ over synchronous OPSD while increasing average benchmark accuracy.

</details>

Abstract: Large-language-model register-transfer-level (RTL) generation has concentrated on how to train the model: fine-tuning, continued pre-training, and reinforcement learning from a verifier. We argue this is the second-order question. On a bounded, formally specifiable task of generating single-error-correcting, double-error-detecting (SECDED) ECC memory wrappers, we hold the task, data, and correctness oracle fixed and vary two axes independently: the training regime (seven settings) and the output representation (four levels, from fully expanded flat Verilog to an eight-field configuration expanded by a deterministic generator). Because a reference generator exists, correctness is decided by formal equivalence over a design space of approximately ten million points, rather than sampled from a hand-written testbench. Two results follow. First, representation dominates: every representation close to raw RTL reaches a pass rate of at most 0.17, while the configuration reaches 0.83 with no training at all. Once the model emits the configuration, every training regime achieves a pass rate between 0.89 and 1.00. Second, raw RTL hits a hard output-length limit: a 512-bit design requires 44,237 tokens of flat Verilog compared with just 69 tokens for the configuration. Consequently, raw generation becomes infeasible above 32-bit data words rather than merely inaccurate. A model trained from scratch with fewer than 50 million parameters, combined with the verified generator, achieves 0.89 formal equivalence, compared with 1.00 for a 32-billion-parameter fine-tuned model, while using approximately 1600 times less inference energy per correct design. Inference energy also dominates amortized training energy. The lesson is to learn the interface, not the implementation.

</details>

Abstract: Out-of-distribution detection in continual learning (OOD-CL) is highly challenging, as it requires (i) learning a sequence of tasks without catastrophic forgetting, and (ii) identifying unknown samples (OOD) without access to known (in-distribution (ID)) data from past tasks, where standard confidence-based OOD scores tend to degrade. Existing methods largely rely on a rehearsal buffer of past data or assume closed-world settings, limiting their scalability and reliability in real-world scenarios. In this paper, we propose a novel OOD detection framework for rehearsal-free continual learning, inspired by Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KAN), which mitigate catastrophic forgetting via their inherent local neuroplasticity. To better leverage KAN in OOD-CL, we introduce a task-adaptive classifier architecture, TA-KAN, which mitigates recency bias and enhances ID--OOD separability through task-specific control of activation locality. Furthermore, we propose a geometry-guided OOD score that complements TA-KAN classifier confidence without relying on past ID data. Our method significantly improves OOD detection while maintaining strong ID classification performance, achieving state-of-the-art results in OOD-CL.

</details>

Abstract: Dynamic edge classification on text-attributed Web graphs, such as review networks, community forums, and news-derived knowledge graphs, requires high quality, adaptive semantic representations in the context of network evolution. Existing dynamic graph architectures typically initialize text feature vectors with pretrained language models and hold them static during training to preserve their semantic structure, forgoing the potential benefits of allowing them to co-evolve with network topology. To prevent detrimental semantic distortion, a language model maintenance objective anchors the evolving text embeddings to their original semantic structure. Evaluations on the Dynamic Text-Attributed Graph Benchmark demonstrate that this anchored text-graph co-evolution consistently improves upon the edge classification performance of frozen-text baselines across diverse Web-related domains. Representational analyses reveal that the framework reduces anisotropy, improving the linear separability of text embeddings, and effectively encodes structural and temporal information into the semantic space. A computational efficiency analysis confirms that this beneficial semantic and spatio-temporal co-evolution is achieved without incurring the prohibitive computational overhead typical of end-to-end joint training.

Abstract: Lean is increasingly used to verify mathematical proofs and software produced by LLM agents, but writing formal proofs remains hard. Existing prover systems take a divide-and-conquer approach that recursively decomposes hard proof obligations into easier lemmas and splits again any lemma that resists proof. However, a lemma can resist proof for different reasons, including insufficient guidance, a false statement, or a flaw in the decomposition that produced it. Existing systems cannot tell these apart as a failed attempt reports little beyond its failure, and responses are confined to the failing node without considering the rest of the proof or revising the plan above it. The system may thus keep decomposing obligations along a flawed proof direction until the budget runs out, causing the whole proof attempt to fail. We introduce Maestro, a Lean prover harness that handles failures through global coordination. Provers report why they failed, not just that they failed, and a coordinator that sees the whole proof chooses an appropriate response, such as guiding a stuck prover, correcting a false lemma, or retracting a flawed subtree and replanning it. The same harness extends to verified code synthesis, where a checked counterexample sends the code back for repair. We evaluate Maestro with four frontier LLMs on seven mathematics and three software verification benchmarks. It proves more targets than each model's native coding agent (Claude Code or Codex) on every benchmark the agent has not already saturated, with the largest gains on research-level mathematics. With Claude Opus 5, it discovers its own proofs of 34 of the 48 theorems in the June 2026 release of arXivLean, against 19 for the native agent. It also outperforms all published prover systems on every benchmark they share, solving 669/672 PutnamBench, 59/60 Lean-IMO-Bench, and 6/6 IMO 2026.

</details>

Abstract: As language models are increasingly deployed in real-world scenarios with important outcomes, the impact of their mistakes often extends far beyond what correctness can capture. In domains like medicine, a single error can lead to serious consequences and put patient safety at risk. However, mainstream evaluation protocols still treat all errors as equal, overlooking the spectrum of real-world costs. To address this, we propose a utility-based evaluation paradigm that explicitly incorporates the varying consequences of errors. Guided by this principle, we develop UtilityBench, a large-scale benchmark of 15,000 instances with verifiable answers, quantifying error severity using Best-Worst Scaling to elicit meaningful, data-driven cost estimates. These quantified utilities shape when a model should answer or abstain, guiding it to make decisions that align with the true importance of each scenario. As another merit, we introduce UtilityAUC, a metric that measures the area under the utility curve determined by a model's response ordering, thereby enabling utility preference alignment beyond error frequency. Our theoretical analysis demonstrates that UtilityAUC can distinguish between models where standard accuracy and AURC fall short. Empirically, evaluating 20 state-of-the-art models, we find that greater accuracy or more advanced architectures do not reliably translate to higher UtilityAUC, highlighting a gap between problem-solving ability and responsible decision-making in language model evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: Inference-time sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) decoding can improve the reasoning accuracy of masked diffusion language models (diffusion LLMs) without post-training by resampling a population of partial generations according to a reward. Its effectiveness therefore depends on the resampling step, which must concentrate computation on promising candidates while retaining enough diversity to explore alternative reasoning paths. The reward can be internal, the decoder's own confidence on the committed prefix, or external, a process reward model scoring that prefix. Under either reward, multinomial resampling can disrupt this balance by duplicating high-weight particles and discarding lineages that may still reach a correct solution. The effective sample size (ESS) does not register this loss: multinomial resampling resets it to its maximum even as distinct lineages disappear. To retain such lineages, we introduce lineage-preserving SMC (LP-SMC), which combines Chopthin resampling with an input weight floor. Instead of equalizing weights, Chopthin bounds the ratio between the largest and smallest weights it returns and carries the unequal weights forward. The floor limits how far below the maximum a weight may fall before resampling so that lineages with low early weight keep a chance to recover. Across mathematical-reasoning and code-generation benchmarks on two base models, LP-SMC raises oracle coverage, the fraction of problems with at least one correct particle, in every setting, by 1.8 to 25.6 points, and raises end-to-end accuracy under a common reward-model selector in eleven of twelve settings, by up to 7.9 points on LLaDA-1.5 and 18.3 on Dream-7B. The coverage gain persists under the external reward model, which suggests that diversity-preserving resampling and the guidance signal contribute separately to inference-time search.

</details>

Abstract: Offline compaction of the key--value cache (KV-cache) reduces the cost of repeatedly querying a fixed context by constructing a smaller cache once and reusing it subsequently. The central challenge of compaction is deciding which cache entries to delete and which to retain. Existing methods score each candidate for deletion on its own, with the rest of the cache being held fixed. However, softmax renormalization of the attention operation couples these decisions in a nonlinear manner: removing one entry changes the attention weights of every remaining one. In this work, we introduce KV-Surgeon, which uses calibration data to take interactions into account in a tractable way. We derive a quadratic surrogate that admits closed-form expressions for jointly making the optimal greedy removal decision as well as compensation within the remaining entries. On QuALITY, LongHealth, and Long Code Arena, and at every evaluated compaction ratio, KV-Surgeon substantially advances the cost--accuracy Pareto frontier of dense and mixture-of-experts models from 4B to 30B parameters. At $50\times$ compaction, KV-Surgeon improves question-answering accuracy by up to four points over attention matching (Zweiger et al., 2026), the state of the art, at comparable cost. Lastly, we introduce an even faster variant, which also exceeds the state of the art while requiring only two fifths of its cost.

</details>

Abstract: Graph-to-table systems combine three design choices: an encoder that maps relational evidence to feature rows, a context constructor that selects labelled examples, and a tabular decoder. We investigate whether these components can be evaluated independently through matched comparisons that change one component while holding the rest of the pipeline and the evaluation constant. Across logistic regression, CATBOOST, and TABPFN-3 on CLUTRR, 57 GraphLog worlds, and 57 knowledge graphs, we show that conclusions about one component can change when the others change. Abstract: Plug-and-Play (PnP) methods using continuous-time deep generative models as learned data distribution priors enable synthesizing high-quality solutions of model-based inverse problems. The quality of the solutions depends critically on the interplay and consensus between the inverse problem solver and the generative model sampler. We revisit a recently introduced flow-based PnP framework, which we refer to as PnP-CF (Consensus Flow), and extend its application to nonlinear inverse problems, including those with learned neural-network forward operators. PnP-CF combines a flow-matching denoiser with (i) ADMM-like dual variable updates and (ii) a time-adaptive pullback onto the intermediate flow probability path that combines a stochastic and a deterministic component. We discuss how these two features stabilize the algorithm and promote consensus, and demonstrate on a set of experiments that PnP-CF not only solves standard linear inverse problems with quality at least comparable to state-of-the-art baselines, but also extends gracefully to nonlinear inverse problems, including forward operators realized by pretrained neural networks, for which existing methods produce unsatisfactory results or fail completely.

Abstract: Looped transformers reuse a shared block of layers to increase computational depth without increasing the number of trainable parameters. They are trained with backpropagation through time (BPTT). To save training compute, BPTT is often truncated to the last few loops, although this may weaken the benefit of looping. To study this tradeoff systematically, we jointly vary model size, training tokens, and forward and backward loop counts across nearly 600 training runs and fit a unified scaling law that captures the effects of looping and truncation through effective parameters. Despite the training-compute savings of truncation, our fitted law favors full BPTT when forward and backward loop counts are jointly optimized. In matched-compute runs, full BPTT through fewer loops consistently outperforms truncated settings of the same compute. The law further shows that at fixed model size, the compute-optimal loop count and training-token budget both grow with compute. When model size is also free, the law identifies two fully trained loops as the best looped setting. Experimentally, this setting achieves lower loss than the compute-optimal non-looped model with about 27% fewer non-embedding parameters and only 8% more training compute.

Experiments with 20 LLM-agent configurations show that public search tools are necessary but insufficient: performance drops sharply as hop depth increases, the best model remains far below human experts, and process-aware scoring reveals partial reasoning progress hidden by final-answer-only evaluation. Abstract: Adversarial vulnerability persists across modern vision architectures from CNNs to vision language models (VLMs), yet existing detection methods rely on heuristics without theoretical guarantees. We address the fundamental question of when adversarial perturbations can be provably detected from a geometric perspective. Our key insight is that adversarial perturbations cannot simultaneously preserve geometric structure across spaces with fundamentally different properties. Accordingly, we construct two such complementary metric spaces. First, we use a standard CNN embedding space $Z$, where adversarial samples exhibit significant displacement patterns. Second, we build a novel prime-quantized space $P$, that absorbs small perturbations through number-theoretic discretization, resulting in minimal displacement, while preserving discriminability. We then leverage the geometric discrepancies across spaces $Z$ and $P$ to detect adversarial samples. To the best of our knowledge, we establish the first rigorous separation theory for adversarial detection, proving that adversarial samples create unavoidable geometric inconsistencies across both spaces. Our framework provides theoretical guarantees including pixel-level absorption bounds, neighborhood diameter concentration, Gromov-Wasserstein (GW) separation theorems, and practical risk control. Extensive experiments validate our theoretical predictions and achieve consistently strong detection performance across a wide range of attack types and model families.

</details>

Abstract: Classical inverse bandit methods typically aim to recover true reward parameters. In contrast, we infer a demonstrator's subjective beliefs, specifically its internal estimates of suboptimality gaps. We consider no-regret demonstrators that follow randomized policies, such as Thompson sampling, and characterize a family of algorithms for which per-step inversion is possible. This enables gap estimates to be recovered directly from the arm-selection distribution. Our analysis identifies an inherent stability-coverage trade-off: inversion becomes ill-conditioned as the demonstrator concentrates its probability mass on a single arm, while the number of informative rounds depends on the rate at which its policy converges. We establish conditions under which inversion is reliable and provide theoretical guarantees on the resulting estimation error. Experiments on simulated data support the analysis and illustrate how these conditions affect gap recovery in practice.

Abstract: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) have achieved notable progress in classification tasks through large-scale pretraining. However, their post-training practice mainly focuses on implicit parameter fine-tuning, where task-relevant information is entangled with abundant irrelevant knowledge in vast parameter spaces. This may hinder TSFMs from precisely activating useful knowledge for downstream classification. To this end, we propose Retrieval-to-Recognition (R2R), a plug-and-play post-training paradigm that adapts pretrained TSFMs to time series classification. Rather than pretraining a new foundation model, R2R improves how existing TSFMs are post-trained: it introduces a decoupled query encoder to extract discriminative patterns and retrieve instance-level numerical knowledge from a constructed knowledge base. The retrieved explicit knowledge is then fused with the TSFM's implicit parametric embeddings, enabling joint knowledge transfer at both the parameter and feature levels. In this way, R2R allows TSFMs to recall task-relevant patterns learned during pretraining, improving the efficiency of knowledge utilization in downstream classification transfer. Experiments on 158 time series datasets show that R2R consistently improves the classification performance of state-of-the-art TSFMs. Ablation and knowledge-enhanced studies further validate the effectiveness of the proposed framework.

Abstract: Low reward variance reduces estimation cost, but does not eliminate monitoring of arms that may improve while unobserved. We refine the variance--variation tradeoff and characterize how both costs depend on supplied variance and variation budgets in bounded, non-stationary multi-armed bandits. Let $A$ be the number of arms, $T$ the horizon, $B$ a bound on consecutive mean variation excluding the initial vector, and $Q$ a bound on the sum of the largest per-round reward variances. For every admissible pair $Q,B$, the minimax expected dynamic regret is, within a single logarithmic factor, \[
  \min\{T,\;A+\sqrt{AQ}+(AQBT)^{1/3}
  +\sqrt{AT}\min\{B,\sqrt B\}\}. \] The deterministic boundary $Q=0$ is characterized without logarithms. For small positive budgets, a safe calibration phase identifies a common interval containing all future means of surviving arms. Clipping observations to a wider interval reduces the cost of restarting a prior hybrid static-regret algorithm; an original-mean analytical reference controls its residuals without charging the variation of clipped means. Balancing clipping bias and monitoring closes the small-budget noisy regime. Fixed oblivious constructions give the matching lower bound. We also quantify adaptation costs: on a disclosed deterministic one-improvement family, a stationary-regret budget has a matching worst-case change-regret frontier, including a finite improvement pulse. This implies an $Ω((T/A)^{1/4})$ loss when one policy competes with all supplied-budget deterministic oracle rates. The budget-free low-noise scheduling problem remains separate.

Abstract: Multi-label classification is particularly challenging in streaming scenarios, where data arrives sequentially. Online kernel methods offer a promising solution. However, prevailing methods rely on a single preselected kernel, making them prone to suboptimal choices since the global data distribution is unknown in advance in online settings. Although the recent method, Online Kernel Selection for Online Multi-label Classification (OKS-OMC), enables data-driven kernel selection, it fails to fully harness the complementary information from multiple kernels and encounters the curse of kernelization. To address these limitations, this paper introduces a novel online multi-kernel combination method for large-scale multi-label classification. First, an efficient and robust online single-kernel multi-label method is designed. Second, learning an optimal convex combination of multiple multi-label kernel classifiers is formalized as a structural risk minimization problem. An improved alternating optimization procedure is developed, in which the combination coefficients are updated by directly minimizing the objective function, while each kernel classifier is updated by minimizing a surrogate upper bound. This procedure decouples the learning of kernel classifiers from combination coefficients, allowing independent classifier updates via our single-kernel method and accelerating convergence to high-quality solutions. Our multi-kernel method is proven to asymptotically converge to the optimal fixed convex combination of the best fixed single-kernel models chosen in hindsight. Ablation studies confirm that our method's components outperform their counterparts in OKS-OMC. Comprehensive evaluations across 11 datasets and seven metrics demonstrate our method's overall superiority over existing online multi-label methods.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) aggregates the capabilities of multiple domain-expert teachers into a single student model. However, all domains in MOPD share the student's finite budget of optimization steps and rollouts. Fixed uses constant domain sampling ratios based on corpus sizes; by contrast, D$^3$-MOPD adjusts the ratios using the teacher-student reverse-KL divergence and its recent decrease. However, favoring large recent decreases can direct more training toward domains that already improve quickly, while domains that improve more slowly may receive too few examples. To address inflexible allocation and the risk of giving insufficient training to domains that improve more slowly, we propose Nash-MOPD, an adaptive domain scheduler based on a local minimax game using existing reverse-KL statistics. The equilibrium maximizes the smallest predicted proportional reduction under the local model and gives more prompts to domains that need more examples for comparable improvement. Consequently, Nash-MOPD responds to changing learning progress and supports domains that improve more slowly without extra teacher queries or rollouts. At step 100, Nash-MOPD exceeds both baselines' final mathematics and code checkpoint scores on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B and reaches Fixed's on Qwen3-8B-Base. Furthermore, it leads eight of nine reported metrics and is best or tied best on all six mathematics and code accuracy metrics, respectively.

Abstract: We investigate whether cyclically varying the class composition of training batches can modify both the performance and the internal organization of neural networks. Motivated by nonequilibrium systems, in which cycling between related sub-goals can make switching between their solutions increasingly efficient, we introduce Dynamical Batching (DB), a sampling protocol that periodically emphasizes each class while leaving the loss and the optimizer unchanged, and compare it with Uniform Batching (UB), in which every mini-batch has equal class proportions. In the regime where standard training fails, DB changes the outcome: on a three-arm spiral, a capacity-limited MLP that plateaus far below perfect accuracy under UB fits the training data under DB. Tracking individual weights, we find that under DB the change between class-specialized states is carried by a smaller set of weights than under UB, and that this set is markedly more persistent from cycle to cycle, on every synthetic dataset we test and in a wider network that fits the data under both protocols. This organization is a signature of the protocol rather than a predictor of its benefit: it appears equally where DB leaves accuracy unchanged. On CIFAR-10, the same protocol modestly improves the test accuracy of a channel-narrowed ResNet-10, a standard residual architecture, and of a second capacity-limited convolutional network. These results show that how class emphasis is distributed in time can shape both the solution a network reaches and the internal structure of the network.

</details>

Each treatment coordinate is represented as a query token, allowing cross-attention to estimate the response to the full treatment vector. It was subsequently rolled out to all marketplace traffic and has operated at full scale for several months as of October 2026. The deployment illustrates how multidimensional treatment modeling and observational pretraining, combined with adaptation using RCT data, can support effective joint discount decisions in production.

Abstract: Target propagation (TP) is a backpropagation alternative in which local targets carry the global learning signal. Prior works claim that TP performs Gauss-Newton (GN) optimisation on these targets, but the correspondence has only been established under unrealistic assumptions where hitting them exactly is trivial. Practical variants instead descend on a least-squares problem via a single gradient step. In this paper, we rederive TP from a trust region under an implicit, previously unexamined norm, and introduce CGTP (Conjugate Gradient Target Propagation), which uses conjugate gradients to solve the layer-local subproblems in parallel. A single adaptive parameter jointly controls this trust region and a matching bound on the weight update; KFAC emerges exactly as the special case where the per-sample Jacobians are replaced by their batch mean, giving its factored damping a variational grounding. On MNIST and Fashion-MNIST MLPs, CGTP matches the accuracy of ADAM and KFAC while needing orders of magnitude fewer outer iterations than ADAM (about 10x fewer backward-pass-equivalents overall), while remaining stable across depth.

</details>

Method: 采用递归图神经网络框架，单一共享GNN模型多次迭代更新当前迭代点；通过轨迹规约训练收敛至更精确解。

Conclusion: SOCPNet通过利用求解器轨迹实现迭代式优化，使得在多次迭代后最终解误差大幅下降且可在混合求解器中作为起始点使用，显著减少求解器迭代次数。

Abstract: Second-order cone programs (SOCPs) are a fundamental class of convex optimization problems widely applied across engineering domains. Classical interior-point methods are robust and accurate, but their iterative nature can be costly in real-time or repeated-solve settings. This has motivated learning-based solvers that approximate optimizer solutions with neural networks. Recent work has increasingly studied graph neural networks (GNNs) for linear, quadratic, and second-order cone optimization, leveraging their ability to model sparse relational structure among variables, constraints, and objective functions. Most existing GNN-based solvers compress the optimization process into a single forward pass, predicting the optimizer directly without using the intermediate trajectory produced by classical solvers. We introduce SOCPNet, a recursive GNN framework that uses solver trajectories to learn an iterative SOCP solution procedure. A shared GNN model is applied repeatedly, refining the current iterate at each step and enabling inference for a variable number of iterations. Rather than predicting the solution in a single pass, SOCPNet learns from intermediate iterates to progressively refine its estimates using a trajectory-regularized training procedure. We evaluate SOCPNet on multiple datasets spanning standard SOCP problem classes and show that it consistently reduces the final solution error relative to a SOCP-GNN baseline. Finally, we demonstrate how trained SOCPNet models can be used in a hybrid optimization pipeline by warm-starting a classical interior-point solver, reducing solver iterations without loss of accuracy.

</details>

Abstract: Deep generative design can produce complex geometries, but unconstrained representations may create undercuts that prevent release from a two-part mold. We present CAST, which reformulates three-dimensional shape generation under a selected mold-opening constraint as a two-dimensional image generation problem using paired top and bottom depth images. Each valid depth pair defines one material interval along a selected mold-opening direction, so reconstructions exclude undercuts in that direction by construction. Diffusion models generate candidate shapes in this representation, and a finite element analysis (FEA) guided optimizer refines the same two boundaries. We evaluate diffusion-based generation with limited training data comprising 211 SimJEB bracket designs. On SimJEB brackets, FEA-guided refinement with a volume penalty reduced compliance in 193 of 200 generated candidates. The median reduction across all candidates was 36.8 percent in the optimization FEA model. Reconstruction of existing brackets revealed a mass--stiffness trade-off: filling occluded regions increased material volume and reduced displacement under the evaluated loads. Paired depth images thus connect generative exploration and structural refinement in a shared design space that preserves the selected pull-direction restriction.

Abstract: Neural operators offer an efficient data-driven approach to rapid prediction of complex automotive aerodynamic flows. However, spectral bias and other factors limit accurate reconstruction of fine-scale flow variations near complex geometries. Existing studies improve the representation and fitting of fine-scale information mainly through information extraction and training objectives. Further study is needed to determine how extracted local details can be used continually in subsequent operator computations and contribute effectively to the overall flow-field representation. We propose CIS-Transolver, a cross-layer incremental-state neural operator that extends fine-scale modeling from local information extraction to its use across layers. The method extracts fine-scale information through local multiscale analysis and encodes it as geometric moment states that are propagated and updated across layers. By combining information from previous layers with feedback to the value representations in Physics-Attention, the method allows local details to contribute continually to subsequent modeling of global physical correlations. We systematically evaluate CIS-Transolver on three public automotive aerodynamics benchmarks, ShapeNet-Car, AhmedML, and DrivAerNet++, covering surface pressure fields and volumetric pressure and velocity fields. CIS-Transolver achieves the lowest mean relative L2 error among the evaluated methods for all five prediction metrics across the three benchmarks. The relative error reductions average 8.72% across the five metrics compared with the best baseline for each metric, reaching 16.38% for ShapeNet-Car pressure prediction and 15.60% for AhmedML velocity prediction. These results provide a new approach to fine-scale flow prediction for complex geometries.

Abstract: Non-invasive brain-computer interfaces are in practice limited by signal-to-noise ratio, and recent state-of-the-art methods address this limitation by aggregating over repeated trials of the same stimulus. Yet multi-trial aggregation has been treated inconsistently across the literature: some work averages brain signals at the model's input, other work averages predictions at the model's output, and neither treats aggregation as an architectural choice in its own right. We provide the first systematic comparison of aggregation strategies for non-invasive neural decoding, evaluating six paradigms (including logit averaging during training and three attention-based variants we introduce) across three datasets, four tasks, and two group sizes. We find that aggregation choice matters substantially: training-time logit averaging, the simplest method we propose, outperforms all others in 6 of 7 settings. On the phoneme classification setting of the 2025 PNPL competition, it sets a new state of the art, significantly outperforming the competition winner by 17.4 absolute percentage points in balanced accuracy. We further draw a theoretical connection between logit averaging and joint ensemble training, which has been shown to underperform independent ensembling in the deep learning literature; our results call into question recent explanations of why joint ensemble training fails. Together, these findings establish multi-trial aggregation as a key design choice for non-invasive neural decoding and identify training-time logit averaging as a simple, effective default.

Abstract: Irregular multivariate time series forecasting (IMTSF) requires capturing temporal dynamics and cross-variable dependencies from sparse, asynchronous observations. Existing methods often rely on learned temporal encoders, coupling historical representation construction with model optimisation and repeatedly processing the same observations. We propose CTLite, a lightweight forecasting framework that decouples historical summarisation from learned prediction. Specifically, a parameter-free continuous-time moment encoder summarises local levels and trends at multiple temporal scales while suppressing poorly supported slope estimates. A compact readout combines damped local extrapolation with a low-rank cross-variable correction that accounts for observation freshness, target availability, and forecast horizon. Since the historical summaries are independent of trainable parameters and forecast queries, they can be reused across optimisation steps and repeated predictions for an unchanged history without altering predictions or parameter gradients. Experiments on four real-world benchmarks show that CTLite achieves the best or second-best mean MSE and MAE among the evaluated methods with only 88-2,537 trainable parameters. Under matched GPU workloads at batch size 32, CTLite reduces inference latency by 18.6-87.3% compared with the latest models, including the cost of historical encoding. Reusing summaries further reduces total runtime by approximately 52% over 16 forecast calls on the same history compared with recomputing the encoder for every call, including the initial encoding cost. The code is at: https://github.com/SSMa913/NICLab-CTLite.

Abstract: This paper considers the Bayesian top-$k$ identification problem, wherein a decision maker is tasked with selecting $k$ items from a pool of items of unknown quality. To assess the items, the decision maker adaptively allocates a fixed sampling budget, where each sample of an item yields a noisy observation of its quality, with the objective of minimizing the expected opportunity cost of the selected set. We propose an approach that allocates each batch of samples by minimizing a surrogate objective derived from an upper bound on the expected opportunity cost. The approach is general: samples can be allocated in batches, the selection size $k$ is arbitrary, and it applies to many sampling distributions. It is also simple and intuitive. We prove that the method's posterior expected opportunity cost decays at an exponential rate of at least half the best rate achievable by any sampling rule. In simulations across a wide range of settings, the method is competitive with, and often improves upon, the best existing algorithm in each setting.

Abstract: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on downstream tasks often weakens their previously aligned safety behavior, creating an inherent trade-off between task adaptation and safety preservation. Existing approaches attempt to mitigate this issue by identifying safety-relevant neurons, layers, or update directions in the original parameter space. However, safety-related information is often distributed across many directions in this space, causing downstream updates to overwrite parameters that encode safety behavior. We propose WSR-Tune, a Weight Space Reparameterization-based fine-tuning framework that preserves safety during downstream adaptation. Our approach constructs a safety-conditioned basis and reparameterizes the weight matrices such that safety-relevant information becomes concentrated in a small subset of directions. We then identify safety-critical directions and freeze them in the reparameterized space, while updating the remaining complementary directions for downstream adaptation. By explicitly structuring the parameter space to disentangle safety and task information, our method reduces interference between objectives and enables simultaneous preservation of safety and improvement in downstream performance. Experimental results show that our method achieves a more favorable trade-off between downstream performance and safety retention, demonstrating its effectiveness for reliable LLM fine-tuning.

</details>

Abstract: Expand-and-sparsify ($\texttt{EaS}$) representations lift an input to a much higher dimension through a linear map and keep only the $k$ largest coordinates. Existing theory builds that map at random and needs the expanded dimension to grow exponentially in the input dimension, which puts the construction out of practical reach. We ask what is gained by learning the map by gradient descent instead, and how that gain depends on how wide the expansion is. We sweep $73$ OpenML classification tasks and $48$ regression tasks under a protocol that holds the datasets, expanded dimensions, sparsity levels and splits fixed across every method, and we summarise each comparison by a median over datasets and a Wilcoxon signed rank test. We report four findings. First, learning the expansion helps most when the expansion is narrowest, and its advantage over a random map decays to nothing by the widest expansions we run, so a learned $\texttt{EaS}$ layer can be made small. Second, with the map learned, a top-$k$ representation that keeps at most a tenth of its coordinates is indistinguishable from a dense $\texttt{ReLU}$ layer of the same expanded dimension at every expansion factor we test, while GELU and SiLU are not. Third, when the representation is forced to be binary the sparse code is the better of the two, and the reason is that binarization costs it nothing while it costs the dense layer accuracy. Fourth, how sparse the representation can be is governed by how wide it is: the sparsity level that is free rises as the expansion grows, so there is no fixed limit on how sparse it can be. The regression tasks reproduce the first finding and qualify the second and third.

</details>

Selecting test cases based on target observations is known to favor forecasts that match the selected distribution rather than the intended deployment distribution. This result is replicated outside the model's training domain after a seven-year gap. Abstract: Changing sparse autoencoder (SAE) codes can alter both intervention direction and magnitude, so comparing intervention effects requires a strength control specifying what is held equal. Norm matching and output calibration provide different controls, but whether interventions yield different effects across codes after strength matching remains unclear. With one coefficient per input, full-output Kullback-Leibler (KL) calibration leaves smaller mean effect gaps from the encoder-based reference intervention than total-norm matching for nonnegative least-squares (NNLS) refits. On new Llama inputs, total-norm matching loses 11 reference transfers, whereas full-output calibration preserves every transfer decision. (2) Positionwise scaling. On a small matched gender-agreement subset, positionwise norm matching leaves smaller observed mean gaps than full-output calibration. (3) Answer exclusion. Compared with unscaled NNLS, answer-excluded calibration reduces gaps in the measured subject-verb agreement comparisons but increases them for gender refits. Full-output KL calibration also reduces larger gaps for norm-matched constructed candidates, although matching coverage is incomplete in several settings. Abstract: Consolidating specialized foundation models into a unified generalist is a central goal of post-training. Two approaches are in common use: model merging, which is fast but treated as a heuristic, and multi-teacher on-policy distillation, which matches distributions but demands heavy rollout compute and suffers from cross-task gradient conflict. We show that the two are one objective seen at different orders. Within a shared basin of homologous specialists, the multi-teacher on-policy distillation objective is, to second order, exactly the Fisher-merging objective. Its minimizer is therefore the Fisher merge, a classic model-merging method given by a closed-form combination of the specialists, so merging is optimal, to second order, as a closed-form stage of distillation. Building on this identification, we propose Residual-Governed Branch-and-Merge(RGBM), a two-phase consolidation procedure. Concretely, a full-parameter Fisher merge is intractable on foundation models, so Phase~1 restricts the merge to the task-vector subspace. Extending tangent-space analyses of merging, we show that the same coefficients that mix task vectors also mix implicit rewards, which lets a residual program on unlabelled prompts solve for them. The program minimizes the worst per-domain residual and reports its energy-normalized values as the domain residual spectrum. Phase~2 lets this spectrum govern what may be added: RGBM passivates domains with low normalized residuals, trains isolated branches only for domains above the spectral gap, and writes back the admitted updates with calibrated shrinkage. On two open-source consolidation suites, the Phase~1 merge alone matches or outperforms fully trained multi-teacher distillation, and RGBM improves on it further with zero extra joint-training steps, whereas every un-gated write-back, including continued joint distillation from the merge, falls below it.

</details>

Abstract: Jailbreak methods are commonly compared using Attack Success Rate (ASR), but these comparisons depend on how responses are generated and judged. In this paper, we examine eight attacks against three open-weight language models using three evaluation procedures. Low-resource language attacks achieve a mean ASR of 96.0% under keyword matching but 2.7% under a Qwen3 evaluator following StrongREJECT scoring instructions. Changing the number and arrangement of attempts reverses the ranking of DeepInception and rewriting. In a separate experiment, sampling 100 responses for each unchanged request at Ollama's default temperature of 0.8 yields 100% ASR under keyword matching but 50% under the Qwen3 evaluator. Beyond evaluator sensitivity, these findings raise a safety concern: repeated sampling can expose residual unsafe behavior despite an initial refusal, suggesting probabilistic suppression rather than robust exclusion. We recommend explicit success definitions, matched response budgets, validated evaluators, and reporting cumulative failure under repeated interaction rather than relying on single-generation tests.

Abstract: Background: Existing dementia risk scores rely on fixed, pre-selected features and linear models, or ignore bias from informative missingness in cohort data. Here we introduce the Istanbul Technical University Dementia Risk Score (ITU-DESC), an explainable machine-learning score for incident all-cause dementia that combines broad feature selection across roughly 8,000 candidate features with explicit control of missingness bias.
  Results: We studied approximately 500,000 UK Biobank participants with a median follow-up of 16.6 years. We withheld missingness patterns from the model using unconditional random hot-deck imputation. We stabilized feature selection by repeating BorutaSHAP-based feature selection with ten random-imputation seeds and selecting predictors chosen in at least eight of the ten seeds, which yielded 38 predictors. We trained a CatBoost gradient-boosted decision-tree model and evaluated it on a holdout test set of 50,041 participants. For all-cause incident dementia, ITU-DESC achieved an average precision of 0.138 (95% confidence interval 0.123-0.156) and an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (ROC AUC) of 0.853 (0.844-0.863), significantly outperforming other risk scores, including the strongest comparator, the UK Biobank Dementia Risk Prediction model (0.118 and 0.841). We identified dementia risk predictors absent from established scores, including leukocyte telomere length and liver enzymes. Conclusions: ITU-DESC is the first dementia risk score to combine broad feature selection with explicit control of missingness bias.

Abstract: Neural networks that respect the symmetries of point clouds commonly compute high-order representations of the rotation group to improve their expressivity. Such models are known to be universal, and thus able to separate any pair of point clouds, when using representations of sufficiently high order, yet no precise bound on this order, as a function of the number of points, was previously known. We establish the first quadratic lower and upper degree bounds for polynomials that separate point clouds of n points up to rotations and permutations. By showing Tensor Field Networks and MACE can express all invariant polynomials of a given degree with sufficient representation order, we additionally derive the first explicit, quadratic bound on the representation order sufficient for these architectures to be complete, replacing previous existence results with concrete guarantees. We further show that, under local regularity assumptions, point cloud separation can be achieved with a message-passing-based model using invariants whose degree depends only on the size of a local neighborhood, rather than on the size of the full point cloud. Finally, we provide counterexamples showing MACE must compute high order irreducible representations to separate locally regular point clouds.

In particular, VaDar is the first algorithm to solve challenging, long-horizon tasks in the RoboCasa suite, even when starting from a policy pretrained from behavior cloning with near-zero initial task success. Taken together, VaDar's successes suggest that subgoal information benefits actor-critic reinforcement learning by enhancing the efficacy of critic learning, whereas policy goal-conditioning is often unnecessary.

Abstract: Pretraining filtering is one of the most common safeguards for open-weight language models. Here we study its effectiveness, durability and scalability using the ESM-2 family of protein language models. We find that filtering viral proteins from the pretraining data meaningfully degrades masked language modelling, mutation effect prediction and long-range contact prediction on viral proteins, while largely preserving performance on non-viral proteins. These effects are concentrated among proteins from human-infecting viruses, and grow as we scale models from 8M to 3B parameters. As models increase in size, we find that stringent filtering becomes more effective. However, fine-tuning on sequences from the removed viral families restores the lost performance. Although the cost to recover performance increases with model scale, even for the largest models it costs about 80 USD of H100 time. This work establishes pretraining filtering as moderately effective at selectively degrading performance on viral proteins, but not durable against fine-tuning with modest computational resources and access to relevant data.

Abstract: Multi-agent approaches have been investigated recently as a means of improving the scalability of reinforcement learning for neural combinatorial optimization. Existing methods assume that all problem instance information is known upfront. However, real-world problems are often dynamic: relevant information is progressively revealed as the agents take actions (e.g., arriving at an edge in a routing problem only to discover it is untraversable). In such cases, existing methods fail to coordinate agents to explore and exploit the progressively revealed information. To address the arising coordination challenge, we introduce PACT (Plan-Augmented Coordination via Trace-graphs). PACT augments the environment graph with auxiliary nodes representing the plans of agents, which are connected to the resource nodes to indicate future dependencies. The proposed message-passing scheme over the augmented graph grounds inter-agent coordination in the environment and enables proactive, rather than reactive, conflict resolution. We show that PACT improves solution quality in dynamic problems relative to baselines and exhibits the best transfer to larger instances on which, remarkably, it outperforms centralized oracles with privileged access to instance uncertainty.

</details>

Abstract: LLM agentic workflows increasingly employ different models for specialized roles. Users may prefer different trade-offs between task quality and deployment cost, sometimes also considering latency. Finding good assignments incurs a separate search cost from evaluating multiple candidates. We introduce Cost-Coupled Gittins (CC-Gittins), a multi-objective extension of single-objective cost-aware Gittins search for Pareto recommendation. It exploits the coupling between evaluation and deployment costs: assignments that cost more to deploy also cost more to evaluate. The search alternates between seeking higher quality and lower deployment cost, reusing each evaluation to inform both searches. After each batch, CC-Gittins recommends assignments that are nondominated under uncertainty-adjusted estimates of quality and deployment cost. We evaluate CC-Gittins on eight quality--cost matrices from workflows with two to five roles and up to 1,681 candidate assignments. We also evaluate the method with latency as a third objective. Across the eight benchmarks, CC-Gittins recovers a substantial fraction of the true Pareto frontier at roughly 10% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost, and recovers most of the frontier with few false positives by 30%.

</details>

Abstract: Rubric hacking limits both performance and training robustness in rubric-based reinforcement learning. By decomposing rubrics into sub-dimensions, we reveal the Intra-Rubric Reward Divergence: as the total rubric score increases, certain sub-dimension scores systematically decrease, suggesting the model sacrifices specific quality aspects to optimize the aggregated objective. From the sub-dimension perspective, we observe two phenomena: (1) dimension imbalance, causing the policy to favor heavily covered dimensions; (2) dimension absence, leading to a systematic gap between rewards and true quality. To mitigate these issues, we propose Fair-Race Dimension Aggregation (FRDA), which has generations compete within each dimension separately to mitigate rubric imbalance, and Residual Semantic Dimension Completion (RSDC), which mines evaluation dimensions implicitly encoded in a holistic preference signal to complete rubric coverage. Experiments in chat, science, and medical domains indicate that FRDA mitigates rubric hacking with more stable improvements, while RSDC outperforms both single-reward training and naive multi-reward aggregation, yielding generally higher-quality responses.

</details>

Abstract: Autoregressive language models emit one token per forward pass. This causes high inference latency, in particular as models are scaled up for quality. In this paper, we introduce Parallel Language Models (PLMs), which generate mutually consistent tokens in one forward pass by routing dependencies through latent auxiliary variables. PLMs are trained using a variational bound that reduces to an autoregressive loss plus a parallel decoding loss. Empirically, PLMs are on a faster and better Pareto frontier than autoregressive and discrete diffusion baselines. Pretrained PLMs achieve near-autoregressive pretraining loss on model scales of 80M to 3B parameters, and speedup follows a power law in model size. When post-trained, our 1.3B model matches or exceeds an identically trained autoregressive model on math, coding, and instruction-following benchmarks, speeding up inference $2.3\text{-}5.7\times$. Together, we show that parallel generation can be trained from a single training objective at autoregressive quality. We will make our code available at https://github.com/mandt-lab/plm.

### [337] [GeoSplit: Selective Edge--Server Map Acquisition for Cross-User Travel-Mode Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26707)

TL;DR: GeoSplit 将轨迹推断与地图上下文分离，在边缘–服务器环境下实现高效的行驶模式识别：通过选择性查询减少60%负载、对未见用户宏F1提升至0.786，证明其通信效率与准确性兼顾。

Motivation: 网络化移动感知需将本地轨迹推断与远程地理空间上下文结合，以使通信决策成为预测系统的一部分，提升移动感知的智能水平。

Method: GeoSplit 通过拆分图证据的作用、训练时迁移以及运行时选择性获取，实现对行驶模式的识别。采用 10% 标记的 GeoLife 数据集，在用户-天和用户-离散分组下评估模型；使用完整地图上下文提升宏F1；对运动信息进行蒸馏得到仅运动的学生模型；通过熵门控的紧凑查询接口实现 39.5% 的轨迹查询并减少 60.5% 的负载。

Result: 在未见用户下，使用完整地图上下文将宏F1从0.7154提升至0.7864；图信息蒸馏得到的仅运动学生模型宏F1为0.7225；熵门控查询得到0.7729宏F1（全查询0.7738），查询率仅为39.5%；在用户-天分组下，查询率降至21.6%。学习值估计与概率融合未见一致提升。

Conclusion: 结果表明 GeoSplit 架构在边缘–服务器推理中实现了通信效率和准确性兼顾，可在仅采用部分轨迹查询的情况下保持接近全查询性能，显著降低数据开销，并提升对未见用户的宏F1分数。

Abstract: Networked mobility sensing couples local trajectory inference with remote geospatial context, making communication decisions part of the prediction system. We present GeoSplit, an edge--server framework for travel-mode recognition that separates the utility of map evidence, its training-time transfer, and its selective acquisition at runtime. On GeoLife with 10% labels, we evaluate these components under user-day and user-disjoint grouping. For unseen users, aligned full-map context raises macro-F1 from 0.7154 to 0.7864, while map-informed distillation yields a motion-only student at 0.7225. Through a compact query interface, entropy gating achieves 0.7729 macro-F1 versus 0.7738 for all queries, querying 39.5% of trajectories and reducing application payload by 60.5%. Under user-day grouping, selective and all-query scores are similarly close at a 21.6% query rate. Learned value estimation and probability fusion offer no consistent improvement. These results illustrate communication-efficient edge--server inference for an intelligent transportation sensing task.

</details>


### [338] [Doubly Robust Learning of Heterogeneous Survival Treatment Effects under Time-Varying Treatments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26710)

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 新双重稳健学习器处理时变治疗和删失，理论证明具备稳健性和效率，实验显示优于传统方法。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 在医学等领域中，时变治疗所导致的异质生存治疗效应估计至关重要，但传统方法仅处理基线治疗或无删失假设。

Method: 提出新的双重稳健学习器，拆分干预窗口与生存视野，使用机器学习估计混杂与删失函数，具备 Neyman‑正交性、双重稳健与准 oracle 效率。

Result: 数值实验验证该学习器优于现有方法，在时变治疗和历史相关删失情境下表现更佳。

Conclusion: 该框架提供了在时间变治疗下估计异质生存效应的可行且理论上具备优越性的工具，并可与现有机器学习技术无缝集成。

Abstract: Estimating heterogeneous survival treatment effects (HSTEs) from time-to-event data under time-varying treatments is important in fields such as medicine (e.g., to decide which sequence of treatments is better in terms of survival probability). However, this task is challenging because treatment decisions, patient characteristics, and censoring probability often evolve together, requiring sequential adjustment for both treatments and censoring. Existing survival learners mostly consider a single baseline treatment assignment, while longitudinal treatment-effect learners typically assume uncensored outcomes. In this paper, we introduce a novel doubly robust learner for heterogeneous survival treatment effects under time-varying treatments and history-dependent censoring. Our framework separates the intervention window from the survival horizon, with treatment prescribed for a finite period, after which care follows its natural course. We further establish the favorable theoretical properties of Neyman-orthogonality, rate double robustness and quasi-oracle efficiency. The framework accommodates flexible machine learning models for nuisance estimation and treatment-effect regression. We evaluate our learners through several numerical experiments, demonstrating their effectiveness.

</details>

Abstract: Frontier LLMs can copy random text almost flawlessly, yet they often overrun or truncate long blocks of repeated characters. In repeated strings, matching local content no longer reliably identifies where copying should continue or when it should stop. Nevertheless, we show that a two-layer transformer with $O(\log\log N)$-bit precision and $O(\log N/\log\log N)$ width can copy every string up to a chosen design horizon $N$ and terminate correctly under arbitrary repetition. We use RoPE-style rotations of attention values and outputs (VO-rotations) with small prime periods to encode distances from delimiter anchors modulo those primes. By the Chinese Remainder Theorem, these periodic encodings jointly distinguish all required distances, allowing one retrieval rule to select either the next source token or the end marker. In contrast, we prove a width-independent impossibility result for copying under sequential finite-precision softmax accumulation. It applies to transformers whose only positional input is an additive attention bias that eventually becomes constant, including NoPE and a recursive implementation of ALiBi. Once an identical-token run exceeds the resulting saturation threshold, further repetitions leave the logits unchanged. If this threshold grows sublinearly with $N$, these models cannot both complete a long all-identical copy and stop correctly, a pattern resembling the termination errors of frontier LLMs. In synthetic copying experiments, baseline transformer models fail on long repeated strings even within the training range. Trained VO models, by contrast, generalize beyond the training lengths with near-perfect accuracy up to length $\approx 5005$, the selected primes' period product.

</details>

Abstract: Neural Posterior Estimation (NPE) fits an amortized conditional density estimator to a simulated training set of complete-data realizations. NPE's training objective, log-likelihood, favors posterior approximations that assign arbitrarily high density to the training points, and typical families of amortized conditional density estimators (e.g., normalizing flows, Gaussian encoders) are flexible enough to oblige. The resulting policy of extreme overconfidence translates poorly to held-out data. Early stopping and other regularizers can prevent this degeneracy, but they are limited to selecting posterior approximations along an unfavorable optimization trajectory set by in-sample residuals, where the posterior mean is underfit while the posterior variance is overfit. To avoid this trade-off, we propose Cross-Fitting NPE (CF-NPE), which uses sample splitting to separate conditional-mean estimation from the estimation of the posterior's higher moments. Using $K$-fold cross-fitting, CF-NPE fits $K$ posterior-mean regressors and then fits a conditional density estimator to the resulting out-of-fold residuals. In experiments with synthetic data and in a cosmological application, CF-NPE achieves higher held-out log-likelihood than standard NPE and typically improves calibration. CF-NPE is most useful in high-dimensional, simulation-limited settings.

</details>

Abstract: Complementary-label learning (CLL) trains a classifier from labels that name a class an instance does not belong to. Such labels are cheap to collect from crowdworkers or vision-language models (VLMs), making them attractive for large-scale annotation. However, both sources can mistakenly assign the true class as the complementary label. Such annotation errors are particularly harmful in CLL because the model is explicitly trained to suppress the correct answer. Optimal transport (OT) data valuation identifies harmful samples by comparing training data with a clean validation set, but transferring it directly to CLL creates a semantic mismatch: label $c$ means not class $c$ in the training but class $c$ in the validation. We show that this makes the OT label groups noisy mixtures whose behavior depends on the noise rate. The transferred score is informative only at sufficiently high noise, becomes uninformative at the crossover rate $1/M$ for $M$ classes, and reverses below it, where practical CLL annotation typically operates. We propose CAVA, which introduces a complementary label cost and a label margin that isolates the contribution of each complementary label to the transport potential. The margin keeps a consistent direction across noise rates, detects diagonal noise with a parameter-free threshold of closed-form precision, and allows detected samples to be recycled as ordinary-label supervision. On synthetic and human-annotated benchmarks, CAVA separates diagonal noise almost perfectly where directly transferred OT valuation performs worse than random. With only a fraction of the clean validation data, it also improves every CLL objective we evaluate, showing that OT-based valuation becomes reliable once the semantics of complementary supervision are modeled explicitly.

</details>

Abstract: Protein language models (pLMs) are powerful tools for protein annotation and mutation-effect prediction, but progress has largely depended on scaling model size and data, making them expensive and prone to memorization. Test-time scaling offers a complementary path, but methods developed for autoregressive LLMs do not transfer directly: masked encoders lack a generation loop, and amino acids do not verbalize in a reasoning language. Instead, we adapt recursive pretraining to masked pLMs, where a weight-tied Transformer core refines residue-aligned hidden states over an adjustable number of cycles. We instantiate this recipe as recAMPLIFY, a pLM with 155M unique parameters trained on the same 2T tokens as AMPLIFY-350M. Despite 56% fewer parameters, it improves CASP contact recovery and three out of four supervised tasks, notably raising fold accuracy by 22.5%. Because optimal depth for recAMPLIFY varies across tasks and assays, we then introduce Gated Mixture Recurrence (GMR), a framework that decodes each protein from a probability-weighted mixture of hidden states visited during recursion. GMR employs a learned residual gate at scalar, low-rank, or per-dimension resolution to control how much of each cycle's proposed update is accepted into the state. This unifies halting and gating into a single adaptive computation objective while recovering PonderNet-style halting as a special case without gates. Layered on top of recAMPLIFY, GMR further reduces expected layer depth by up to 37%, although the accuracy trade-off varies by task. Mechanistic analyses show that residual gating controls recurrence stability beyond the trained depth and that halt depth tracks biological properties.

</details>

Abstract: Adapting pre-trained continuous-time generative models to sequentially incoming data without forgetting earlier tasks is a central challenge in lifelong learning. A leading strategy, generative replay, uses a generative model trained on historical tasks to augment its own continual update with synthetic samples from experience. However, it requires full trajectory simulations at every training step, which accumulate discretization errors and can degrade the replayed historical distribution. Here, we propose trajectory replay, which bypasses sample generation by training an updated velocity field to match both a frozen historical velocity along its trajectories and the incoming task's velocity field, weighted by a stability-plasticity coefficient. Through the lens of continuous-time transport, we show the resulting optimum averages the two task velocities by their respective densities and generates the mixture of their probability paths. Combined with mean velocity distillation, trajectory replay accesses historical trajectories using only a few network evaluations per continual update. Empirically, it outperforms generative replay and distillation baselines on class-incremental image generation. In two biological case studies, trajectory replay extends generative perturbation models to new contexts and experimental batches across two data modalities while preserving treatment-effect prediction.

</details>

Abstract: Harness development plays a crucial role in building capable AI agents. Recent approaches improve harnesses through recursive self-improvement (RSI), but their performance gains often come with higher execution costs. We introduce HarnessPro, a label-free method for evolving harnesses to improve task performance while reducing execution cost. In this setting, candidate harnesses are evaluated using LLM-generated tests without access to ground-truth solutions or official tests. Specialized agents diagnose execution trajectories, plan updates with different performance-cost priorities, and implement them as candidate harnesses. Rather than returning a single harness, HarnessPro uses normalized weighted squared-distance selection to provide cost-focused (lite), balanced (medium), and performance-focused (heavy) harnesses for different deployment budgets. Selected harnesses enter the next iteration, where execution feedback guides further updates. Our experiments cover Terminal-Bench 2, SWE-Bench Pro, and ProgramBench, using GPT-5.5 with Codex and GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Across these benchmarks using GPT-5.5 with Codex, the heavy mode of HarnessPro achieves an average pass-rate gain of 2.0 percentage points and an average per-task cost reduction of 26.1% relative to RHO.
