Abstract: Multi-agent systems can differ under identical agents and communication budgets when they support different forms of joint interaction. We introduce Topological Sheaves of Irreducibility (TSI), a sheaf-theoretic framework separating task-level compatibility obstructions from system capability. A task is represented by an interaction complex $K$ and a capacity sheaf $\mathcal{F}$; nonzero $H^n(K;\mathcal{F})$ defines a structural signature, while registered interventions test capability. For cocycle data, cochain realization is solvable exactly when the represented cohomology class vanishes. Examples show that the signature is sheaf-relative, and the degree-1 reduction yields cycle-space witnesses and observation requirements. We evaluate two finite one-round models. Across 15,625 degree-1 inputs, an all-agent additive primitive solves decision and repair exactly; the optimal pairwise system reaches 88.80% on decision, one input above the 88.79% majority-class baseline, and 26.63% on repair. On a degree-2 tetrahedral-boundary task over 531,441 inputs, the same-2-skeleton system retains all four three-agent primitives yet matches the pairwise-only optimum throughout the registered separation window, whereas adding the class-filling four-agent primitive makes both tasks exact. Status-preserving robustness checks retain exact performance but are not cost-matched to the class-restoring ablation. Conditional on the registered primitives and cost models, TSI provides task-relative, falsifiable tests of higher-order interaction.

</details>

We find that GBDTs and MLP-based models show broadly similar scaling trends, with gains from both additional training data and algorithmic improvements. Tabular foundation models show more varied scaling behavior, with recent versions benefiting more consistently from larger contexts even under distribution shift. However, scaling training data under distribution shift introduces new challenges: in-distribution and out-of-distribution performance mostly improve together at smaller scales but can diverge at larger scales. We examine this through MLP capacity sweeps and comparisons of the validation distributions used for tuning and early stopping in MLPs and GBDTs. We also compare common practices of using validation data, contrasting its use for model selection in MLP pipelines with using it for in-context fitting in tabular foundation model pipelines. Under stronger shifts, MLPs selected using validation representative of the evaluation conditions generally maintain or improve performance as training data grows. In contrast, including such examples in context improves foundation-model performance, but does not prevent deterioration with increasing training size in our experiments.

</details>

Abstract: Every continuous piecewise linear (CPWL) function is a finite signed sum of maxima of affine functions, making maximum representation a central problem in the study of the expressiveness of ReLU networks. (2026) that two hidden layers suffice for general CPWL functions, we give the Caterpillar Net, a nonrecursive construction for the maximum of $N$ inputs with two hidden layers of widths $O(N^2)$ and $2^{O(N\log N)}$. The construction has a direct geometric interpretation.

Abstract: In decentralized federated learning, heterogeneous clients may develop complementary expertise, yet each query is typically evaluated only by the client where it arrives. To exploit this distributed expertise without global coordination, we formulate inference-time collaboration as budgeted local expert referral, a graph-constrained routing process in which a query can move only between neighboring clients and only visited clients contribute to its final prediction. EdgeDRM implements this framework by learning a density-based routing potential for each client: differences between neighboring potentials provide the comparisons used to select successive referrals, while local task models generate predictions that are aggregated along the resulting route. Both training and inference are decentralized, using only neighbor-local information without a central router or graph-wide client scoring. We establish estimation guarantees for the learned comparisons and characterize how local improvement and graph structure govern prior-weighted density gains from referral. Experiments on benchmark and real-world medical data show that EdgeDRM outperforms graph-matched decentralized baselines in most settings, with the clearest benefits under sparse connectivity.

</details>

Abstract: Brain encoding models aim to predict neural responses to stimuli. The most widely used approach fits readouts from frozen pretrained backbones. Using neural data to train large models from scratch or fully fine-tune them after task-pretraining has proven difficult because neural data is costly and limited. We show that parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) enables us to effectively adapt very large pretrained backbones for brain encoding with a small number of trainable parameters. To make it computationally practical, we exploit the backbone's causal structure to predict multiple timepoints in a single pass instead of using sliding windows, significantly reducing feature-extraction cost. This enables efficient fine-tuning of multimodal backbones with up to 104B parameters, the largest model adapted for brain encoding to our knowledge. Trained with a low-rank adapter (LoRA) and a lightweight readout, our approach consistently improves brain encoding performance across nine diverse multimodal backbones. On the same Qwen3-Omni backbone as the prior state-of-the-art (SoTA), our method BrainLoRA achieves the best published performance on the Algonauts 2025 Challenge. We also show the adapted multimodal backbones remain interpretable: modality attribution recovers the canonical sensory topography that prior work often obtains by construction from separate unimodal encoders, and neural adaptation increases the contribution of audio inputs and cross-modal mixing within the backbone. To scale out the number of adapted models, we develop a multi-LoRA kernel that accelerates joint training of multiple LoRAs on a shared backbone. This enables an ensemble of 200+ adapted models that ranks first on the public Algonauts post-challenge leaderboard at the time of writing. Together, our results highlight PEFT as an efficient and powerful paradigm for brain encoding.

</details>

Abstract: Next Best Action (NBA) systems must select among customer interventions (e.g., discounts, promotions, and lifecycle actions), maximize long-term economic value, satisfy complex operational constraints, and remain causally grounded. Existing approaches lack a rigorous framework for meeting these requirements jointly. We address this gap by formulating NBA as a Constrained Markov Decision Process (CMDP). We then integrate off-policy causal correction of the estimated customer dynamics directly into the Linear Programming (LP) formulation of the CMDP. The resulting policy selects actions based on customer states to maximize long-term value while explicitly enforcing business guardrails. Furthermore, the LP formulation yields shadow prices that quantify the economic trade-offs of operational constraints (e.g., fairness and user welfare), enhancing policy interpretability. We validate our proposed offline Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework on an industry case study involving rideshare coupon targeting, where customer states are defined by Recency, Frequency, and Monetary (RFM) stages. We evaluate performance using model-based off-policy evaluation, with causal dynamics estimated from a temporal holdout. Under the observed coupon budget and RFM state representation, our optimized policy improves customer lifetime value (LTV) by 19% over the estimated behavior policy and 8% over a myopic baseline. Although the demonstrated action space is binary, the proposed framework is action-agnostic, yielding a general-purpose decision module for targeting systems that manage multiple interventions.

Abstract: We propose KronLSTM, a linear attention architecture based on the Kronecker Delta Rule, a novel update rule for associative matrix memories. KronLSTM extends the xLSTM family with a memory-revision mechanism that corrects targeted associations while modifying the memory only along a single direction in matrix space. This minimally invasive memory revision reduces interference with previously stored information compared with delta-rule updates, making it particularly well suited for long-context language modeling. The resulting memory dynamics can be mathematically described as a Markov chain that converges to a steady-state distribution, while the expected norm of the memory matrix remains uniformly bounded throughout the entire input sequence. Hence, KronLSTM ensures stable memory dynamics without requiring additional normalization and stabilization mechanisms for the memory state. Experiments show that KronLSTM is competitive with, and often outperforms, existing linear attention architectures, including models of the (Gated) DeltaNet family, with particularly strong performance on long context benchmarks. These results establish the Kronecker Delta Rule as an effective and analytically tractable foundation for stable associative memory in recurrent linear attention architectures. Our code (https://github.com/NX-AI/KronLSTM) and models (https://huggingface.co/collections/NX-AI/kronlstm) are open-source.

</details>

Abstract: Long-horizon agents must learn heterogeneous capabilities from interleaved trajectories. Updating shared parameters to learn new capabilities can degrade previously acquired ones, resulting in catastrophic forgetting. As a trajectory unfolds, different stages may require different capabilities, yet conventional joint training and static adapter merging maintain a fixed parameter configuration throughout execution. We introduce WeAct (Weight-level Internal Actions), which trains independent capability-specific LoRA adapters over a frozen backbone and adds adapter selection to the agent's action space. These internal actions use the model's native tool-calling interface for on-demand capability composition in a shared context. Across eight deep-research benchmarks, Qwen3-32B with WeAct improves average accuracy by 6.2% relative to full fine-tuning counterparts despite using less compute, and by 27.5% relative to the strongest static LoRA-merging method. WeAct also supports modular continual adaptation, by adding a new code computation adapter to expand the agent's capabilities and updating the existing writing adapter to improve report quality. Both updates leave the backbone and other adapters unchanged, supporting modular continual adaptation in agents.

Abstract: AI agents are growing more capable and persistent, and are being deployed in ever larger numbers. As more start collaborating, AI control measures must remain effective when they covertly collude on a harmful objective. We introduce MiniLab, a synthetic series of live services and codebases (5 interconnected services, 249 benign tasks, 8 attack objectives), adapted from internal AI deployment infrastructure. We demonstrate distributed attacks, in which different agents carry out separate attack fragments, and how their covertness evades frontier monitors when reading their trajectories individually. We show how distributed attacks spread across diverse contexts and harmful objectives through 16 human-optimized attacks (weight exfiltration, unmonitored rogue deployment, permission elevation, and more). A frontier monitor, GPT-5.6 Sol, reading each agent separately flags only 7% of successful distributed attacks, compared with 87% when a single agent carries out the same side task. Individual attacks can also be composed into longer attack sequences toward a greater loss-of-control outcome, without any individual agent being flagged. Finally, we tested whether models can plan these covert attacks: even with execution and monitor feedback, the best model-planned attacks succeed covertly against a single-trajectory GPT-5.6 Sol monitor in at most 25% of executions, compared with 80% for human-crafted plans, though this may scale with model capabilities. By turning worst-case coordinated attacks into reproducible red-team cases, our work provides a foundation for preventing locally benign agent actions from accumulating into system-level loss of control.

### [356] [ToolRosetta: Surface-Form Invariance of Tool-Call Guards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24055)
*Hasan Demirkiran,Jens Ernstberger*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: 代码驱动代理会导致现有守护在字符串表面形式下性能恶化，尤其是含推理的守护；统一的渲染工具（ToolRosetta）可测量并对比不同表面形式下的误报/误拒。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 多数研究通过 tidy JSON 表示工具调用，而现实部署的代理使用 execute_bash/execute_code，导致安全守护在不同表面形式下的性能不一致。需要统一评估以判定守护在代码驱动环境中的可靠性。

Method: 构建 ToolRosetta，将代理的每一步行动重现为 Python 调用、Shell 命令以及无推理版本，并使用代码和循环解析验证渲染的正确性；随后在多组基准（AgentDojo、TS-Guard、TS-Bench）中评估十种守护和对照组的误报/误拒变化。

Result: 在十一种守护与两组对照组中，前沿和强开源守护仅偏移 3.4 分；四个专用守护的误报率在 Shell 表达式下升高 21–47 分，阻塞 30% 至 90% 的正常行为；其中 TS-Guard 在其自行基准上误报率攀升 38 分。去掉推理对多数守护影响显著；通过重构解析器可恢复性能。

Conclusion: 在代码驱动的代理环境中（bash/exec），现有的守护机制对工具调用的鲁棒性会显著下降，尤其是在对 Shell 表达式或其它字符串形式的调用进行渲染时; 但基础的开放式和前沿式守护仍保持相对稳定。

Abstract: Step-level guards for tool-using agents are evaluated on tidy JSON tool calls, but deployed coding agents act through execute_bash and execute_code tools whose payloads are strings. We introduce ToolRosetta, which re-renders every candidate action in the AgentDojo and AgentHarm splits of TS-Bench as a Python call, as a shell command, and without the agent's rationale. The renderings are produced by code and verified by round-trip parsing, so the user request, the history, the label and the action's effect stay fixed while only the surface form changes. Across eleven guards with two placebo controls, frontier and strong open-weight guards are stable: no frontier error rate moves by more than 3.4 points. Four of five specialized agent guards are not: under the shell form their false-positive rates rise by 21 to 47 points on AgentDojo, and TS-Guard's by 38 points on its own home benchmark, blocking between a third and nine in ten benign actions; the fifth, StepGuard, does not move. A small open guard drifts five points more permissive. Removing the agent's rationale moves most guards whose input carries it, in guard-specific directions. A request-blind control on one guard shows that the rationale is where intent is read from. A parser that restores the canonical call in front of the guard removes the effect for these renderings, by construction. Benchmark accuracy on JSON calls does not predict a guard's operating point behind a code-acting agent.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved impressive performance in generating text conditioned on inputs from different modalities. In this work, we introduce backdoor infection, a cross-modal attack framework targeting MLLMs. Starting from a model already injected with a backdoor in one modality, our attack constructs a corresponding trigger in another modality, called the infected modality. Exploiting the model's shared latent space, the trigger in the infected modality reliably activates the malicious behavior, without modifying or poisoning the infected modality during training. We further develop a lightweight, constraint-aware optimization method for efficient and practical trigger generation. Extensive experiments across several state-of-the-art MLLMs, including LLaVA, InternVL, Qwen-VL, and Gemma, demonstrate that our approach identifies cross-modal triggers that are more efficient, effective, and less detectable than direct optimization baselines, highlighting a critical new threat vector in multimodal systems.

</details>

Abstract: Machine-learning-based intrusion detection systems (IDSs) for operational technology (OT) are commonly evaluated on benchmark datasets in which normal and attack data are collected in separate recordings. In such settings, high performance may reflect recording-associated differences as well as attack behavior. We introduce a recording-aware evaluation procedure that compares attack windows with background from the same recording, uses prior data for model fitting and calibration, tests temporal alignment against randomized event schedules, and reports event coverage together with alarm burden. On the ProvICS multimodal dataset, cyber modalities strongly distinguish the benign and attack recordings before the first labeled attack. Same-recording evaluation reverses the apparent modality ranking across all studied window sizes: the physical view becomes the strongest individual modality, while cyber-only fusion shows the largest difference between cross-recording and same-recording AUC, reaching 0.54. Operational analysis further shows that permissive event-overlap metrics can overstate detection when a small number of long alarm episodes cover multiple actions. An external analysis on HAI shows smaller and reference-dependent recording sensitivity. These findings show that cross-recording benchmark performance should be interpreted alongside same-recording, forward, temporal, and operational evaluations.

Abstract: Detection rules are typically crafted based on specific observations of an attack. While these rules are effective for particular attack instances, their coverage diminishes when adversaries modify their techniques, such as by employing alternative execution paths or adapting to different telemetry environments while achieving the same objective. To address this challenge, we introduce ARMS, an automated rule mutation solution that generates variant detection rules. ARMS defines a novel three-dimensional proximity space to identify techniques related to the original attack that capture structural relations, functional similarity, and environmental constraints. Rather than relying on ungrounded generation, ARMS leverages verifiable attack execution commands, which are extracted via semantic alignment with the Atomic Red Team repository, to construct a context-aware Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipeline. We evaluated ARMS using verified, public SigmaHQ rules and successfully generated variants, demonstrating ARMS's capability to expand detection coverage. We further evaluated ARMS across multiple research questions, showing the effectiveness of each proximity dimension and infrastructure-aware rule customization. Nearly all generated variant rules successfully compiled into SIEM queries, and ~92% of the newly generated rules detect the original behavior despite attackers' technique variations.

</details>

Abstract: Browser agents automate complex workflows by reading and acting on arbitrary page content, but this flexibility exposes them to prompt injection attacks and unintended data exfiltration. Recent work argues that LLM-driven browser agents erode traditional web security boundaries such as the Same-Origin Policy (SOP) by acting across origins with the user's authority and no built-in isolation. We argue that restoring SOP is neither sufficient (a same-origin public sink can leak private data) nor necessary (legitimate tasks require authorized cross-origin flows) for protecting the security of users. We propose SPIDES, which instead enforces information-flow control by gating primitive browser actions such as typing, clicking and navigating on labels propagated from element-level HTML confidentiality and integrity annotations. Untrusted and confidential content is masked from the agent's context, while policies block unauthorized flows, providing security guarantees against both prompt injection and data exfiltration that hold independently of the model driving the agent. To evaluate SPIDES and baseline defenses under a common threat model, we build MicroNet, a closed ecosystem of 28 interlinked websites with element-level ground-truth labels and paired benign/adversarial tasks. SPIDES achieves 0% attack success rate by design while retaining high task completion rate and autonomy. Our findings demonstrate that information-flow control is an effective technique to extend web security to browser agents, gaining on autonomy and utility against state-of-the-art baselines. The source code and data are openly accessible at https://github.com/microsoft/SPIDES.

</details>

Abstract: Layer 2 (L2) rollups have become the dominant scaling solution in Ethereum. At the core of these systems lies the rollup sequencer, a component that orders, executes, and submits L2 transaction blocks to the main Ethereum blockchain. The centralization of this sequencer introduces security risks, including censorship and malicious manipulation of transactions to extract maximum extractable value (MEV). Trusted execution environments (TEEs) provide strong hardware-enforced confidentiality and integrity guarantees for code and data. TEEs are strong candidates for strengthening security and ensuring verifiable neutrality in rollup sequencer operations, but multiple TEE-based designs are possible, offering different trade-offs among modularity, performance, and security guarantees. Among them, the separation of proposer and builder logic (PBS) into independent services is a strong contender against the monolithic approach that bundles all sequencer logic. Despite several proposed theoretical frameworks, there is a lack of experimental evaluation of the practicality and security trade-offs of integrating TEEs into rollup sequencers. This work addresses this gap through a comprehensive empirical study of TEE-enabled rollup designs under both PBS and monolithic sequencer models. Our experimental evaluations show that the impact of TEEs on rollup performance is small and that PBS does not significantly amplify it. We also show that PBS can lead to significant performance improvements by enabling more specialized, efficient builder logic implementations.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents now place options orders at retail brokers, and developers publish open-source options agents faster than anyone has examined them. Prior security work on trading agents scored academic equities designs by whether an attacker can flip a buy-or-sell decision. We present the first measurement of the options agents that brokers and developers ship, scored by the safeguards they enforce and by the worst loss their documented order paths can reach. We screened 765 GitHub repositories, broker documentation, and the research literature and found 215 eligible repositories and 8 broker-native agent interfaces. We then scored 26 systems, including every broker interface and every open-source project with at least 20 stars, on seven robustness and eleven security safeguards with cell-level evidence. The ecosystem is new: 198 of the 215 repositories were created in 2026, and 129 (60.0%) appeared in the two weeks around one options-trading hackathon. Of the 22 systems that can place orders, 10 enforce a pre-trade limit outside the model, yet none of the 26 authenticate their text sources or cross-check market data. Of the 26, 17 can execute with no mandatory human step and 13 can reach unbounded loss, including 6 of the 8 broker interfaces. Because an attacker who writes the agent's inputs can move an order's strike, expiry, volatility, and legs while its direction stays correct, options agents need loss limits enforced at the order boundary.

Abstract: AI agents increasingly interact with external environments to complete complex user tasks. These interactions can lead to unexpected behavior and expose agents to security threats such as indirect prompt injection. Current agent security systems largely protect agents by restricting their actions, which can result in a substantial security-utility trade-off for complex, open-ended tasks. To address this, we propose a quantitative risk-based approach to access control for AI agents that regulates measurable risks accumulated during task execution. As a concrete realization of this approach, we introduce RATION, a security framework that tracks measurable risks associated with agent actions and enforces quantitative constraints throughout execution. RATION first constructs a task-specific policy by combining explicit constraints derived from the user request with implicit risks calibrated from historical executions of similar tasks. During task execution, RATION continuously tracks the accumulated risks and applies different enforcement strategies depending on the type of risk. Extensive experiments on four security benchmarks show that RATION provides strong security while maintaining high task utility across diverse models and environments.

</details>

In this paper, we introduce MemJoin, a privacy benchmark of 288 synthetic analysis cases in which the complete history entails a sensitive relation, but every individual session is insufficient. These findings show why checking attribute visibility alone is insufficient to evaluate what records from different sessions reveal together.

Each vehicle weights the reports it holds by the trust, proximity and freshness of their senders, fuses them with Yager's rule, and decides only when the fused margin reaches an abstention threshold. Abstention is a waiting state: a vehicle still unsure at the event is resolved by an explicit application policy, which makes the trade-off between missed hazards and unnecessary detours visible. CREST requires only a scalar trust per reporter and is built here on an evidential trust model whose consensus and revocation steps are updated to support the decision layer. A single report is shown to change the decision margin by at most its weight, which bounds the influence of an attacker coalition by its trust. In a pre-registered simulation study with fourteen attacks, including position spoofing and trust-gated camouflage, and with all unspecified parameters of all systems tuned by the same procedure, CREST reduces the decision error of two published trust-based dissemination systems from 0.162 and 0.106 to 0.051. With the best-guess policy it also lowers both missed and unnecessary reroutes and outperforms the forced binary rule on both error types in every cell.

Abstract: Recent work shows that attackers can implant dormant malicious behaviors that remain hidden at release but emerge after benign fine-tuning. As foundation models are used as agents, whether dormant poisoning extends to harmful agent actions and multimodal settings remains unclear. We show two agentic risks: (1) harmful behaviors activated by benign fine-tuning can extend to unseen actions; (2) multiple harmful behaviors can coexist and be jointly activated by benign downstream fine-tuning. Existing defenses often rely on external safe responses, which are hard to obtain for agent actions, or a trusted clean reference model. We exploit a key property of dormant poisoning: the attacker must preserve safe behavior at release to conceal poisoning, and this behavior can serve as a safety reference. Leveraging this property, we automatically construct a Safety Buffer pairing harmful inputs with the released model's responses and benign versions generated by the same model to remove harmful intent. The Safety Buffer can replace external safe responses in existing defenses, but these defenses do not distinguish updates that weaken safety from those that preserve it, often increasing over-refusal or reducing utility. To address this limitation, we introduce LookAhead Defense, which previews benign fine-tuning updates using the Safety Buffer and penalizes only updates predicted to weaken safe behavior on harmful inputs or increase the likelihood of refusals on benign versions in the Safety Buffer. Across language models, language agents, and multimodal agents, LookAhead Defense reduces harmful behavior in agents from up to 93.9% to at most 14.2% and harmful responses in language models from up to 92.5% to at most 2.0% versus vanilla fine-tuning, while maintaining competitive utility and low over-refusal. It remains effective against other poisoning methods.

</details>

Abstract: AI agents in containers act by opening network connections, and the text that drives them can be hostile. Controls placed between the agent and its tools are bypassed by a child process or a direct socket, and their approval is not tied to the connection the kernel later opens. We present connection escrow: every outbound TCP connection from a supervised pod is parked inside the kernel's connect() path, before any packet exists, by a 98-instruction eBPF program on the pod's cgroup. A node interceptor attributes the held socket to its kernel socket cookie, process, executable digest, cgroup and pod identity, parks the first bytes the agent writes, and asks a verifier in a separate container with its own key. The connection is released only on a signed verdict naming that one socket cookie and destination, unexpired and unused; no verdict by the deadline resets it, and a late approval is refused. The agent is unmodified. A 36-check adversarial suite with real cgroups passes on the eBPF hold point (33 of the checks also apply, and pass, on an iptables hold point): refused connections never reach the destination; forged, rebound, replayed, expired and late verdicts are refused; the agent's attempt to reach the verifier is itself escrowed; child-process and renamed-binary bypasses fail; UDP is pinned to the resolver. On a Kubernetes cluster, unmodified pods are held per connection and attributed to their SPIFFE identity, a raw socket that bypasses a sidecar proxy is held, and 600 connections from six pods are gated. The hold adds 4.5 ms median per released connection, of which 24 us is policy. Finally, we gate on request content without terminating TLS: an eBPF uprobe reads the plaintext at the agent's TLS-library boundary (shared OpenSSL, static OpenSSL, Go crypto/tls), a model scores it, and the connection is held on that verdict while TLS stays end-to-end and the enforcer holds no key.

</details>

Because the violations originate partly in the report, we close with an exploratory case study: on four reports, a boundary-aligned, exploit-free report structure lowered a frontier model's score where the narrative invited an unclaimed account takeover, but left the metrics a report does not settle open, and the two published labels disagreed on three of the four reports.

These categories provide a higher-level abstraction over individual CWE weaknesses, organizing them by the security concern they share, such as Input Validation and Injection Attacks, rather than merely grouping related entries. Each category consolidates best practices and secure code examples into a single, prompt-efficient representation. Abstract: Prompt injection is the dominant security risk for large-language-model (LLM) applications and agents, yet the field still lacks a coherent map of how these attacks are constructed. More than twenty public datasets describe prompt injection using incompatible vocabularies, often organized around delivery vector, attacker goal, or generation method. In this Systematization of Knowledge, we present PIAtlas, a taxonomy organized instead around how the injected instruction is constructed to subvert the underlying model. Its six classes, one per subversion mechanism, form the roots of a hierarchy of techniques and sub-techniques that researchers can use to describe attacks consistently and design more systematic evaluations. We derive the taxonomy from how LLMs are trained, prompted, and influenced by human-like framing, and compare it with historical attack data. To ground this comparison, we release PICorpus, to our knowledge the largest license-clean collection of public prompt-injection datasets, and map thousands of its records to PIAtlas under a human-verified protocol. The mapping shows that attacks usually combine several techniques drawn from only a few mechanisms. It also reveals gaps that existing labels obscure: widely used agent benchmarks test only part of the attack space, and adaptive attackers favor mechanisms that curated benchmarks underrepresent. PIAtlas thus provides a common foundation for understanding this attack space, improving benchmark coverage, and guiding red teaming and defenses.

</details>

Abstract: Biometric authentication systems increasingly use privacy-preserving mechanisms to protect sensitive templates. However, the authentication interface itself may still leak information that enables reconstruction attacks. Prior attacks exploit released similarity scores, whereas more advanced systems reveal only the binary accept/reject decision. This binary feedback provides substantially less information, making template reconstruction far more challenging.
  In this work, we introduce query-efficient reconstruction attacks under extremely limited feedback, addressing two key limitations of prior attacks. First, we introduce the first attack that reconstructs protected biometric templates using only authentication success/failure feedback by exploiting the geometric constraints of biometric authentication. Second, we introduce distribution-aware query-efficient reconstruction methods for score-releasing systems, achieving a more realistic threat model by reducing the number of required queries by a factor of $5\times$ compared to recent prior score-based attacks. Finally, we combine these two insights by incorporating a pretrained facial diffusion prior into our geometric binary attack, reducing the number of required queries by a factor of $5\times$.
  Our findings reveal a fundamental security limitation of biometric authentication: independently of the template protection mechanism, the authentication process itself exposes geometric information that can enable perfect template reconstruction up to numerical precision. Moreover, the distribution of biometric templates can be leveraged to make significantly more effective reconstruction attacks.

</details>

Abstract: Cryptographic API misuse in large language model-generated code can compromise software security. While many existing approaches improve code security by modifying model parameters or inputs, how representation changes associated with different cryptographic API repairs can support a shared intervention for safer code generation remains underexplored. Each pair changes a specified API decision while preserving the programming task. Subtracting the misuse program's internal representation from its secure counterpart's gives a repair vector. Across five models, comparisons of mean repair vectors reveal stronger organization by misuse category than by language. These category-specific patterns also support a shared intervention. With model weights fixed, adding one shared direction per model to its internal activations during generation improves aggregate target security rates by 5.28--15.00 percentage points over unsteered generation across languages and misuse categories. These findings connect the shared structure of cryptographic repair representations to a reusable intervention for safer code generation.

Meanwhile, a private digital token controls the random seed and guidance offset to construct positive and negative terminal latent states, enabling coverless message embedding during the final diffusion sampling step. Simulation results on open-source datasets demonstrate that SemVideoSte achieves high embedding capacity, reliable message recovery, and strong semantic consistency under different channel conditions. In addition, eavesdroppers without the correct digital token cannot reliably recover the secret message, and the generated stego videos effectively resist the steganalysis detection. We further establish a prototype testbed to verify the practical feasibility of SemVideoSte.

Abstract: We revisit DM-RSA, an RSA variant that uses two independent moduli, whose original version was broken by Håstad's attack and contained an incorrect compromise-tolerance claim. We give a formal treatment of its repaired variant, DM-RSA-OAEP: (i) a complete IND-CCA2 security proof in the random-oracle model via a two-step hybrid argument, upper-bounding the security of the scheme by that of the weaker underlying RSA-OAEP instance, with an explicit simulation loss; (ii) a fault-detection theorem in an explicit adversary model that covers computation, key, and verification faults, including safe-error attacks, under a clearly stated physical-diversity assumption; (iii) an explicit analysis of why the countermeasure degrades to uselessness when the diversity assumption is violated (common-mode faults on a single chip). We position the scheme against the countermeasure landscape and against the broken dual-modulus literature. The contribution is deliberately framed as a fault countermeasure with a provable detection guarantee, not as a computationally stronger RSA. We conclude with a concrete instantiation and a detailed measurement protocol.

Abstract: Malware image classification (MIC), in which binary files are converted into visual representations and deep neural networks are trained to identify malware families, has recently emerged as an effective approach for malware detection. Federated learning (FL) enables malware image classifiers to be trained collaboratively by multiple clients without sharing raw data. However, a fundamental challenge in FL is data heterogeneity (non-IID), which is particularly relevant but understudied in the malware context. In this paper, we address this gap via three contributions. First, we present an empirical study of federated MIC under varying degrees of data heterogeneity across three popular malware image datasets (MalImg, Virus-MNIST, and MalNet). We show that while FL achieves strong accuracy under IID conditions, both accuracy and training stability degrade as data becomes increasingly non-IID. Second, we introduce a set of round-level statistics that characterize the data contributed by participating clients in each FL round, and demonstrate that class entropy (diversity of class labels present in a given round) exhibits strong positive correlation with global model accuracy. Third, leveraging this insight, we propose an entropy-aware client selection strategy that filters candidate client subsets based on class entropy, and show that our strategy outperforms standard random selection. Our results highlight the role of client composition in federated MIC and underscore the importance of distribution-aware classifier training under data heterogeneity.

</details>

Abstract: Cloud-assisted image classification enables many mobile AI applications, but transmitting raw images to the cloud raises significant privacy concerns. Homomorphic encryption (HE) allows computation over encrypted data; however, existing end-to-end HE-based inference pipelines incur prohibitive latency and computational overhead, limiting their feasibility for latency-sensitive mobile systems. This paper addresses how to enable efficient encrypted image classification in mobile edge-cloud environments without sacrificing accuracy. We propose PRICE, a three-tier edge-cloud architecture that partitions deep neural network inference between a trusted edge and an untrusted cloud. The edge performs lightweight feature extraction in plaintext, while only compact embeddings are encrypted using the Cheon-Kim-Kim-Song (CKKS) scheme and offloaded to the cloud for encrypted final-layer inference. This design avoids homomorphic evaluation of deep backbone layers, significantly reducing computation depth, ciphertext size, and end-to-end latency. To further improve efficiency, we develop RowHyDiag, a row-packed hybrid diagonal encoding with hoisted-rotation-aware encrypted matrix-vector computation, which minimizes rotation operations and improves slot utilization. Extensive experiments on real devices across five datasets demonstrate that, under the evaluated 128-bit-security configurations, RowHyDiag achieves up to 31.8$\times$ faster cloud inference, 85.3$\times$ fewer rotations, and 83.0$\times$ lower setup payload than the conventional Halevi-Shoup diagonal method. RowHyDiag also outperforms the PEGASUS linear-transformation baseline, providing 1.1-4.5$\times$ faster cloud inference and 1.2-3.4$\times$ lower setup payload. The results show that PRICE enables deployment-ready, low-latency encrypted inference for mobile edge-cloud systems under a trusted-edge threat model.

</details>

Abstract: The stream cipher Espresso was designed by E. Dubrova and M. Hell as a hardware-efficient candidate for 5G security. It has two components - a 256-bit binary non-linear feedback shift register (NFSR), with 14 feedback taps, and a 20-variable non-linear output function. However, the security of Espresso with regard to several standard cryptanalytic attacks are estimated using an alternate NFSR, which has only two feedback taps. The above analysis is based on the claim that this alternate NFSR is obtained using a transformation algorithm which preserves the output sequence of the original NFSR. This issue has become all the more significant in the light of recent results on hardware performance evaluations, which assume that the stated equivalence holds.
  This article presents the first successful attempt in settling the above question of Espresso NFSR equivalence. We arrive at our main result by a careful analysis of existing transformation methods relevant to the question. In doing so, we first identify and correct a critical flaw in the proof of a foundational result of E. Dubrova. Next, in the context of Espresso equivalence, we analyze and unify the transformation framework of Dubrova and the one proposed by Yao and Parampalli. Based on our analysis, we have succeeded in transforming the LFSR in an Espresso-equivalent design reported by Yao and Parampalli to the Espresso alternate NFSR. Further, we have obtained a corresponding non-linear output function which achieves output-equivalence with the LFSR design, and hence, with Espresso.

</details>

Abstract: Comparing the predictive performance and computational cost of flow-based intrusion detection experiments requires both quantities to be available for each run. GreenNIDS connects benchmark execution on public NetFlow datasets to a reusable analysis dataset. Model implementations write a common set of outputs, which are ingested into a run-level table with companion tables for model configuration, feature importance, and training history. The run-level table combines predictive metrics with timing, throughput, memory use, and energy estimates based on thermal design power. A CPU-only demonstration comprises 216 runs across four dataset families, three versions, and binary and multiclass tasks. Ingestion produces a 216 x 96 analysis table with no duplicate run identifiers and complete Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC) and F1 coverage. Multiclass ROC-AUC is missing for 36 PyTorch runs, making metric availability an explicit part of subsequent comparisons. The stored records support grouped performance and training-time plots, as well as resource screening after execution. Combining task-specific MCC thresholds with limits on training time, peak RAM, and estimated training energy retains 25.0% of the full run collection. Abstract: Tool poisoning has emerged as a critical security threat to LLM agents, where adversaries embed malicious instructions in tool descriptions or external resources that enter the agent's context, allowing adversaries to manipulate agent's tool selection process. However, existing tool poisoning attacks primarily rely on an idealized assumption that the attacks task effect within a single interaction round. We show that their effectiveness degrades sharply in realistic multi-round agentic settings. We further reveal that this degradation stems from the fact that agents do not consistently surface attack-relevant signals in their reasoning as the planning context evolves, thereby weakening the attack's influence across rounds. To investigate whether tool poisoning remains a practical threat in such realistic settings, we propose SEAL, a two-stage method for persistent tool poisoning. Specifically, SEAL first performs Prefix-Eliciting Trigger Optimization (PETO) to optimize context-specific triggers that elicit attack-relevant reasoning prefixes, improving effectiveness within individual contexts. It then applies Local-to-Universal Trigger Aggregation (L2U) to combine these local triggers into a single universal trigger, improving generalization across different contexts and execution rounds. Extensive experiments across seven representative multi-round scenarios and different models demonstrate the attack effectiveness of SEAL, its transferability across target models, and its resistance to potential defenses.

</details>

Method: 构建可审计的收录机，实时同步多频段IQ数据显示以及解码AIS/GNSS等元数据，采用链式哈希与可信时间锚点生成不可篡改的保存在单一固化封装内。

Result: 原型实现表明，该完整取证框架在多通道捕获率下对存储和计算的开销极小，且能有效检测篡改并验证记录窗。

Conclusion: 提供了一种可审计的无线信号取证方法，能够完整保留海事导航所需的上游射频证据及上下文，支持后续的重新解码和物理层分析。

Abstract: Modern maritime navigation depends on mandatory wireless inputs, most notably GNSS and AIS, whose reception can be spoofed or jammed. When groundings or collisions occur, liability and safety investigations often turn on whether RF interference corrupted bridge instruments, yet the artifacts typically available (VDR outputs, IBS logs, screenshots, decoded messages) are downstream, lossy, and easy to contest in adversarial proceedings. We introduce a forensically sound acquisition methodology that preserves upstream wireless evidence together with the minimal contemporaneous context needed to interpret it. Our system records coherent, multichannel IQ across relevant bands while simultaneously collecting time-aligned receiver and instrument-state metadata (e.g., decoded AIS/GNSS outputs and NMEA sentences), enabling later re-decoding and physical-layer reanalysis tied to what the onboard stack indicated at the time. To make the record independently auditable, we package samples and metadata into a single append-only forensic envelope that cryptographically binds per-stream hash chains with trusted-time anchors and periodic checkpoints, allowing third parties to detect tampering (modification, truncation, splicing, cross-case substitution) and to verify the claimed recording window. A prototype implementation shows the approach is practical: integrity mechanisms add negligible storage overhead and modest compute cost at operationally relevant multiband capture rates.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous cyber-defense agents trained using reinforcement learning (RL) are increasingly adopted for defending enterprise computer networks. However, existing approaches may become brittle as attacker capabilities evolve and enable new attack strategies. In this work, we study the problem of adapting defender policies learned in CAGE Challenge Scenario 2 (CC2), a benchmark enterprise network defense scenario, to an extended network scenario with new attacker capabilities represented by new attacker actions. Effective attack strategies that leverage new actions are initially unknown, and therefore attacker policies must be learned before defender adaptation can occur. To address this challenge, we develop adversarial RL (ARL) with behavior trees (ARLBT), which uses reward behavior trees (RBTs) to define progression-based reward functions and condition attacker policies on RBT execution. The proposed RBT incorporates the new attacker actions into structured attack strategies while leveraging parallel execution to generate rewards across multiple network nodes simultaneously. We evaluate ARLBT for learning attacker policies against two baseline defender policies across network scenarios with different sets of new attacker actions and compare against multiple attacker baselines. The results demonstrate that ARLBT improves attacker success compared to ARL using the original CC2 reward function and that conditioning policies on RBT execution can improve sample efficiency. We further show that defender policies can be adapted against the learned attackers, improving robustness to attacks that leverage new attacker actions.

</details>

Abstract: Differential privacy (DP) is a gold-standard definition of privacy designed to evaluate the privacy guarantee of databases. Rényi differential privacy (RDP) is an important variant of DP that gives tight evaluations of machine learning algorithms. These formulations are easy to understand, but evaluating privacy for actual databases is complicated. Even a small change in the database can break its privacy, and evaluations of DP and RDP themselves are complicated. In particular, RDP evaluations in recent studies have become much harder. Therefore, formal verification of them has been studied for over a decade.
  In this paper, we propose an Isabelle/HOL library for formalizing Rényi differential privacy in a general setting. We first formalize RDP for continuous distributions. As a technical basis, we formalize several properties of convex functions and $f$-divergences for nonnegative convex functions. We formalize basic properties of RDP, conversion laws from RDP to DP, the moment accountants in DP, and the Gaussian mechanism and its RDP. Finally, we discuss the stochastic gradient descent mechanism with Gaussian noise and formalize a rough evaluation of its RDP and DP.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) coding agents, such as Claude Code, are increasingly used for security auditing, with task-specific skills providing reusable vulnerability-analysis procedures. However, their effectiveness in detecting real vulnerabilities and the reasons for their failures remain unclear. We conduct the first large-scale empirical study of 37 security skills using Claude Code with four underlying LLMs on 100 real Linux kernel vulnerabilities across ten classes, totaling 14800 runs. We evaluate vulnerability detection at the function, statement, and vulnerability-class levels. Results show that 24.4 percent of runs locate the correct function, 18.3 percent identify both the function and responsible statement, and 15.7 percent correctly classify the vulnerability. Our failure analysis reveals limitations in evidence acquisition, state-relation reasoning, code interpretation, candidate verification, investigation coverage, and reporting. Based on these findings, we propose SKILL-SIF, a self-improving framework that iteratively evolves security skills by learning from missed vulnerabilities and failure patterns. We evolve five top-performing skills using the original benchmark and evaluate them on 100 previously unseen vulnerabilities. SKILL-SIF improves fully correct vulnerability detection by up to eight times (from 2 percent to 16 percent) on unseen vulnerabilities, while aggregate accuracy across four LLMs increases by 1.85 times (from 6.05 percent to 11.20 percent). These findings highlight the limitations of existing security skills and demonstrate the potential of failure-driven skill evolution to improve vulnerability detection in coding agents.

</details>

Abstract: Trust and reputation mechanisms can reduce the influence of malicious clients in federated learning (FL), but strict update filtering is difficult under non-independent and identically distributed (non-IID) data because honest local updates may temporarily reduce performance on a shared validation proxy. The method is evaluated with 50 MNIST clients, 20% persistent label-flip attackers, Dirichlet heterogeneity levels of 0.5 and 0.1, 100 communication rounds, and five paired random seeds. Under moderate heterogeneity, the proposed method reaches 98.37% final accuracy while rejecting 95.82% of malicious updates and retaining 90.30% of benign updates; the final benign and malicious trust distributions are completely separated (KS=1.00). Under severe heterogeneity, it reaches 95.25% accuracy, rejects 88.55% of malicious updates, and retains 76.90% of benign updates (KS=0.96). Trust weighting alone preserves slightly higher utility in the most heterogeneous condition, but accepts all malicious submis-sions. The results therefore distinguish influence mitigation from explicit containment and quantify the utility-selectivity trade-off introduced by screening. The study provides an empirically validated off-chain governance core that can feed blockchain-based audit and incentive layers in SDFL.

Abstract: Large language models acquire broad knowledge from web-scale corpora during pretraining, including knowledge that could be hazardous if misused. Removing such knowledge from the model itself without degrading general capability is desirable but difficult, as it is deeply entangled with general capability within the same parameters. Existing safety alignment and unlearning methods typically adjust these shared parameters, tending to suppress the expression of hazardous knowledge rather than remove it. As a result, the knowledge remains in the model and is readily recoverable by subsequent fine-tuning. To address this limitation, we propose REX (Relocating into Removable EXperts), a knowledge relocation method that separates hazardous knowledge from general capability at the architectural level. Exploiting the modular structure of Mixture-of-Experts models, REX expands the model with a set of new, physically removable experts designated to hold hazardous knowledge. Through expert expansion, selective gradient masking and routing differentiation, it relocates hazardous knowledge out of the original experts and concentrates it in the new ones. Excising these new experts yields a model suitable for public release. This removal holds consistently across Intern-S2, Qwen3.5-35B-A3B and Gemma-4-26B-A4B; on Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, for instance, excision lowers WMDP-Bio accuracy by 48.4 points while MMLU changes by less than 3 points. The excised model matches the strongest of nine unlearning baselines in removing hazardous knowledge, and it is the only one that preserves this removal under subsequent fine-tuning. After 250M tokens of standard fine-tuning on Qwen3.5, the best baseline rebounds on WMDP-Bio from 44.1% to 78.3%, whereas REX rises only from 32.4% to 42.8%.

</details>

Abstract: The threat of quantum computers breaking classical cryptographic is accelerating the adoption of quantum- resistant cryptographic algorithms. Several government agencies have set a tight time-line for upgradation of all system to support post quantum cryptographs. We study post-quantum migration of IPSec protocols and their impact on network performance. The large key lengths in Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) cannot fit in a single IP packet leading to IP fragmentation. This can cause failure of security associations and degrade performance of IKEv2 under poor network conditions. To tackle this problem, we study a new key- sharing mechanism for IKEv2 and evaluate its performance. Unlike current hybrid key-sharing approaches, which send the classical and quantum keys sequentially over UDP or TCP (as in RFC 9370), the new approach concatenates them into a single composite key and transport them over TCP to ensure a reliable handshake. We implement and benchmark the new composite key-sharing handshake mechanism against existing hybrid algorithms that use either UDP or TCP. Extensive experiments under various network conditions demonstrate that the new approach significantly improves handshake time and energy consumption by avoiding the severe retransmission penalties of the Hybrid handshake mechanism. At 1% packet loss, the handshake time of composite key sharing mechanism is better by atleast 20% than hybrid mechanisms, and the improve- ment get better with higher packet loss reaching 30% at 10% packet loss. Energy consumption follows the same pattern achieving reduction of 29% at 10% packet loss

</details>

Abstract: As jailbreak attacks against large language models (LLMs) become increasingly diverse, red-teaming has come to rely on large libraries of jailbreak prompts and attack strategies to evaluate the robustness of victim models and their defense settings. However, attack effectiveness is highly condition-dependent: an attack that succeeds under one victim-defense setting may fail under another, while exhaustively evaluating the full attack library for each setting incurs substantial token and monetary costs. This motivates studying how jailbreak attacks should be prioritized under a limited attempt budget for efficient robustness evaluation. In this work, we formalize this problem as budgeted attack scheduling over a fixed attack library and investigate three complementary signals under a fixed victim-defense condition: (1) static historical attack success rate (ASR) priors; (2) semantic guidance from LLMs; and (3) online feedback from earlier queries. Building on these signals, we design four scheduling strategies and evaluate them across three victim models and three defense settings on GPT-5.2. Extensive experiments clearly characterize the conditions under which each signal is most effective. Historical ASR provides a strong initialization, but its reliability degrades under defense-induced distribution shifts. Semantic guidance consistently improves or matches static prior-based scheduling in the cold-start regime, while online feedback becomes particularly valuable when transferred priors become stale. Semantic guidance and online feedback can also partially compensate for missing historical priors. Taken together, these findings characterize budgeted red-teaming as an attack scheduling problem in which historical priors, semantic guidance, and online feedback play complementary roles.

</details>

Abstract: As coding models and agent harnesses become more capable, reliable security verification is becoming a central bottleneck. Improving secure code generation requires a signal whose reported progress remains aligned with actual program behavior under optimization; otherwise, an optimizer can improve the signal's verdict without removing the underlying vulnerability. Existing model-based approaches require models to either infer program behavior from source code or specify correct outcomes for adversarial tests, placing substantial reasoning demands on the security signal. We introduce \falcon{}, a counterexample-grounded verification framework that separates attack exploration from behavioral verification and connects them through execution traces of the program under test. In test-time repair, \falcon{} yields substantially stronger held-out functional-security gains than alternative security signals across secure code generation benchmarks, while preserving or improving functionality. As an RL reward, \falcon{} also outperforms static-analysis and learned rewards on held-out functional-security, even when all policies successfully optimize their respective training rewards. Combining \falcon{} with either proxy reward further improves held-out performance, suggesting that execution-grounded feedback can mitigate reward overoptimization while preserving useful guidance from existing security signals.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enables large language models to access external knowledge during inference, but its reliance on external knowledge bases also introduces new attack surfaces. Existing data extraction attacks against RAG systems rely on either prompt injection, which heavily depends on the generator's instruction-following capability, or backdoors that reproduce or paraphrase retrieved documents but struggle to balance extraction fidelity and stealthiness. In this paper, we propose RAGLeak, a supply-chain privacy backdoor attack that decouples information leakage from direct textual exposure. When triggered, the generator reversibly obfuscates retrieved private documents for high-fidelity recovery by an auxiliary LLM. Across three model families and three application domains, RAGLeak achieves up to $100\%$ ASR with limited impact on benign utility and at most a $10$-percentage-point ASR drop under representative detection-based defenses. The backdoor remains effective after clean fine-tuning and across model scales and versions, revealing an underexplored supply-chain privacy risk in RAG systems. The source code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/RAGLeak-4B67.

Abstract: Hosted LLM services increasingly expose model identity as a product claim, but external users and auditors often cannot inspect which model configuration served a request. We study an auditor-assisted regime in which a trusted auditor enrolls claimed identities and prepares verification material before deployment, while audit-time verification uses only the claimed identity and released output, without runtime access to model internals, inference logs, routing decisions, or provider metadata. We propose SUMMARK, a summary-channel watermarking framework that uses user-visible reasoning summaries as a keyed audit surface. During setup, SUMMARK constructs identity-specific vocabulary carriers, trains lightweight summary adapters, and calibrates per-identity thresholds. At deployment time, a keyed statistical test assesses whether the released summary is consistent with the claimed identity and, when needed, attributes the most likely enrolled identity. Across model families, scales, and task domains, SUMMARK reliably detects cross-family and cross-scale substitutions at low false-positive rates while preserving task-facing answer quality. We further study FP16->INT8/INT4 precision-consistency diagnostics and robustness under bounded post-processing, rewriting stress tests, and text-only spoofing, revealing both strong performance and clear failure boundaries. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/trinitr0toluene/Summark.

Returns little: the realised hop falls outside the declared options for 7.9% of decisions and outside the model's top twenty for 9.3%; the most confident 15% holds 91.9% accuracy. Abstract: Code generated by large language models often passes every functional test while remaining insecure. These security hallucinations are common, written with high confidence, and repeated across samples, so neither the generator's own uncertainty nor static analyzers reliably catch them. We introduce SPLASH, which is guided by a new measure called audit pressure. It contrasts teacher-forced log-probabilities for the generated program under a security review and a style review. Aggregated to statements and programs, and grounded in a probabilistic model of competing edits, audit pressure allows SPLASH to choose the secure program among a model's candidate programs and locate the vulnerable statement, with no model training, CWE-specific rules, or internal access to the generating model. On 63,684 programs generated by 12 models for 899 tasks, SPLASH picks secure code in 74% of cases, places the vulnerable statement first in 58% of insecure programs, and chooses better than a frontier LLM judge.

Abstract: LLM-based guardrails are effective against indirect prompt injection (IPI), but their performance depends heavily on model capability: as the guardrail model becomes smaller or weaker, detection degrades substantially. This raises a natural question: can we retain strong IPI defense while relying only on a small LLM? To understand why, we revisit what an LLM guardrail actually does. Detecting IPI requires simultaneously locating potentially injected instructions within heterogeneous tool outputs and judging whether they are authorized by the user's request. We find that the difficulty for small models arises largely from satisfying both requirements jointly, rather than from either subproblem alone. This suggests a different path to lightweight defense: explicitly decoupling localization from decision-making. In particular, localization needs no strong language-model reasoning, since injected instructions are often semantically discontinuous with their surrounding content and can be partitioned into local regions via lightweight unsupervised segmentation. Once the observation is partitioned this way, the remaining authorization check becomes a localized judgment that a small LLM can handle. Building on this insight, we design a lightweight IPI guardrail that performs attack-agnostic localization followed by block-level authorization judgment with a small language model. By separating where to look from what to decide, our approach substantially reduces the capability required of the guardrail model while preserving strong defense performance. Empirically, our guardrail achieves a 0.32% attack success rate with negligible loss in benign utility while remaining effective with substantially smaller models. These results show that strong IPI defense need not rely on strong LLMs when localization and decision-making are explicitly decoupled.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous clinical-modeling agents can optimize predictive performance while violating conditions that make a model usable. LeakBench combines a synthetic task with known failure structure and two real-data settings: MIMIC-IV (430k admissions) and governed multi-hospital inpatient record front-page (MHFP) data. Our primary analysis uses reward hacking descriptively, without attributing intent: an agent selects prediction-time-invalid inputs because they improve its validation reward. A deterministic optimizer and every evaluated unconstrained local-model configuration select a fatal leaking feature. In fully logged September 2026 A/B runs, DeepSeek Flash and GPT-6-astra each leak in 10/10 unconstrained runs and 0/10 runs with an explicit admission-time constraint. This contrast is version-sensitive: a blinded Claude/Fable-5.1 chat run does not leak in either condition, while explicit-constraint violations appear only in a historical August 2026 slice lacking raw trajectories. Replacing AUROC with AUPRC does not remove leakage selection. Renaming or disguising the leaking inputs changes neither selection nor provenance-based detection; a name-based filter fails. A deterministic provenance verifier-in-loop removes the leak, making the LLM repair loop defense-in-depth. Specific verifier feedback clears fatal leakage in a hosted agent, and a three-seed ablation finds principle-only feedback clears decision-level defects, though these mitigation results are limited. LeakBench treats trustworthy clinical prediction as a property of an agent's modeling process, not merely its score; it is an audit testbed with known-failure controls, not yet an expert-validated benchmark.

Abstract: Tactile perception is crucial for precise robotic manipulation, providing an important foundation for dexterous, closed-loop force control. Among various tactile sensing techniques, magnetic tactile sensors stand out thanks to their fine-grained, three-dimensional force perception capability and strong robustness against visual occlusion. In this study, we discover a physical loophole called PhantomTac in magnetic tactile sensors. The attacker can alter the magnetic field, thus spoofing the force interpreted by magnetic tactile sensors. The attacker can stealthily deploy such an attack by simply attaching a thin (the thickness is around 0.5 mm), battery-free magnetic tag to the surface of an object. Hence, the attack tag can be easily concealed by a barcode sticker. Such an attack scheme is "deploy-and-forget" -- the attacker can deploy the tag and leave. When a robotic hand is picking up an object, it may severely damage the object. Specifically, spoofing the normal force can crush the object, whereas spoofing the shear force can lead to a loose grip, thus dropping the object. Compared with directly sabotaging the robotic hand, PhantomTac is much more concealed as the attacker does not need access to the device. We performed extensive reverse-engineering efforts to characterize the sensing mechanism of commercial-off-the-shelf tactile sensors and proposed a practical tag design. End-to-end evaluations conducted on real robots and robotic arms demonstrate that our attack can bypass existing anti-interference mechanisms and effectively hijack their closed-loop control processes, achieving an attack success rate exceeding 95% when the grasping-position offset is bounded within 8 mm. Finally, based on the limitations of existing anti-magnetic interference mechanisms, we propose a multimodal-consistency defense mechanism to mitigate such physical-layer security threats.

</details>

Abstract: Antivirus (AV) signatures encode valuable detection knowledge accumulated through expert analysis, yet repeated black-box queries can expose this knowledge for unauthorized reuse and targeted evasion. We propose Signature Knowledge Shielding (SKS), a design paradigm that protects existing AV rules, termed Protected Signatures (PS), through auxiliary Shielding Signatures (SS). SKS uses AV-supervised importance ranking to discover candidate byte blocks, filters and selects them through negative-sample calibration, and combines SS matches with original AV verdicts through logical OR. SS matching introduces additional positive responses and verdict changes during probing, making detection feedback less directly attributable to PS and increasing the difficulty of localizing signature-related content, while preserving original positive verdicts. Within SKS, we integrate five individually tuned non-learning matching methods and further propose Robust Embedding Matching (REM), a lightweight learned matcher trained with random context and byte perturbations. REM provides stronger and more consistent protection against content modifications and scanning-alignment changes. We develop and open-source a ClamAV-based prototype and evaluate it against five probing methods. With an observed additional test false-positive rate below 0.1%, REM raises the mean query count of state-of-the-art probing to as much as 282.57 times the unprotected baseline, while introducing only 0.67% GPU scanning overhead. These results demonstrate that SKS can substantially increase signature-extraction costs with limited impact on detection utility and scanning efficiency.

</details>

Abstract: Quantum machine-learning (QML) models are increasingly considered for security applications, yet their adversarial robustness in malware analysis remains insufficiently understood. This paper presents Q-MALDetect, a hybrid convolutional and variational-quantum detector for byte-image representations of x86-64 Executable and Linkable Format (ELF) binaries, and evaluates it under feature-space and a range of Grad-CAM-guided, executable-aware evasion attacks. The model achieves 97.2% training accuracy, but its robustness deteriorates as attack strength increases, with the strongest evaluated feature-space attack reaching a 78.7% attack success rate (ASR). Among executable-aware attacks, Grad-CAM-guided no-operation (NOP) insertion produces the highest conditional ASR of 32.9%. Dynamic adversarial training is the most effective evaluated defense, reducing feature-space ASR to as low as 5.0%, executable-aware ASR to 7.7%, and the sequential-attack ASR to 34.0%. In contrast, convolutional-autoencoder preprocessing increases executable-aware ASR to 39.7%, coinciding with a rise in Grad-CAM attribution mass within the mutable .text region from 35.2% to 45.7%. Ablation results reveal no monotonic relationship between qubit count and robustness, while the encoding interface modifies the learned attack surface without neutralizing the strongest attack. These findings demonstrate that the effectiveness of attacks and defenses depends strongly on the perturbation constraints and evaluation conditions. Reliable deployment of QML malware detectors therefore requires assessment against both feature-space and executable-aware attacks rather than reliance on clean accuracy or generic denoising alone.

</details>

Abstract: Transparency systems -- such as Key Transparency (KT), Certificate Transparency, and binary transparency -- require that all honest participants see the same view of an untrusted server's directory, or, failing that, that the misbehaviour is publicly detected. Every existing design closes this gap with an external trust anchor: a publicly available bulletin board (BB), a blockchain, or an out-of-band (OOB) gossip network among auditors.
  We observe that transparency systems already have a built-in resource that has so far been overlooked: the social graph induced by the users themselves. We ask whether it can replace the bulletin board or the external gossip channel entirely, and answer in the affirmative (if occasional out-of-band interactions are allowed).
  We formalise a new primitive, Social-Graph based Forking-Resilient Transparency (sg-FORT), which captures the security guarantees of a BB-free transparency system and we construct Fortress Fork Resistant Transparency logs Relying on Social graph Structure, to our knowledge the first such protocol. In every epoch either all users output the same post, or at least one honest user produces a publicly verifiable Proof of Misbehaviour. Our guarantees hold against an arbitrary malicious server, including a snapshot adversary that may compromise a user and learn its secret state, as long as the server does not compromise close users. In honest executions, OOB interactions occur only when a user resets their secrets, so the total OOB cost approaches the natural lower bound of one call per reset -- dramatically below the full-gossip alternative. We prove security under standard cryptographic assumptions on signatures and dictionary commitment. Under simplifying assumptions (synchronous epochs, always-online users), we view this as a first feasibility result that opens up the design space of social-graph-based transparency systems.

</details>

The research addresses temporal patterns, system-resource relationships, attack vulnerability, and anomaly detection. Results reveal significant temporal variation: Negative Acknowledgment exhibits 7.6-fold hourly variation and 11.5-fold daily variation (p < 0.001), though with small effect sizes (eta^2 < 0.04), indicating that temporal period explains less than 4 percent of total variance. Central Processing Unit (CPU) utilization emerges as the strongest system-level correlate of transport variation (r = 0.617 with NAK rate). Controlled User Datagram Protocol (UDP) flood experiments at 500 packets per second cause CPU increases of 176 to 806 percent, while SRT transport metrics change by only plus or minus 13 percent demonstrating protocol resilience through the Automatic Repeat Request mechanism. An unexpected anomaly reaching 97.7 percent CPU enabled signature-based differentiation between attack types. A Z-score anomaly detection method outperforms fixed thresholds, detecting all 7 security events. This study contributes the longest temporal characterization of SRT metrics reported to date, the first system-SRT correlation analysis, and the first controlled security assessment of SRT infrastructure. These findings provide actionable guidance for capacity planning, security hardening, and multi-metric monitoring of production SRT deployments.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents now trade real capital using news as evidence, yet they cannot reliably tell fabricated headlines from real news. Because they read the same feeds under near-identical policies, a single fabricated post can push them into the same trade at the same moment. We present a systematic attack benchmark for information injection against LLM trading agents. Unlike prompt injection, which targets the model's instructions, information injection targets the evidence the agent trusts. The benchmark spans five injection vectors and seven event categories, evaluated over ~10,000 decisions on 15 U.S. stocks. Five findings emerge. (i) Prompt injection achieves 14% attack success rate (ASR), but fabricated news reaches 62% and two 30-word templates reach 100% on all 15 stocks, inverting the usual LLM-security intuition. (ii) For fact-poisoning templates, surface form matters more than content, since removing credibility cues such as a named source or a "BREAKING" tag drops ASR from 100% to 0% while the fake claim remains identical. (iii) Success depends on the agent's state, with a fake indicator panel scoring 91% against an agent about to hold but 12% against one about to buy. (iv) Attacks generalize across model vendors and remain effective when blended, unlabeled, into real news feeds. (v) Most attacks evade standard defenses, since model scaling stops shallow vectors but not fact poisoning, peer debate fails, and only hierarchical aggregation reduces ASR.

</details>

Abstract: Hidden randomized rounding couples the privacy loss of successive integer releases. We study a rational-feedback mechanism under predictable signed-unit participation and full observation of every physical output. A preparation's lifetime connects three results. First, preserving complete carries until an observed echo synchronizes the worlds gives exact finite-law point compilation, with support-width exponent $3C-2$ at every fixed budget $C\ge2$. Second, releasing commitments earlier gives a monotone hierarchy whose exact endpoint, strict gaps and numerical brackets quantify calibration loss. At one fixed discrete Gaussian, a loss allowance of 0.09 requires all four commitments. Third, legal regeneration yields an unrestricted KL rate with a square-root testing remainder and a complete asymptotic frontier under joint physical-time and participation constraints. At fixed rational parameters, finite-window occupation programs approximate that frontier to error $2^{-b}$ in polynomial bit time in $b$. Verified countable-transcript semantics and independently checked finite certificates support these analyses. A matched static workload illustrates the resulting design tradeoff: a structured finite integer matrix improves error and fresh randomness, while the scalar feedback instance retains fewer noise-state and source-table bits.

Abstract: This paper derives an exact attenuation formula for multivariate cross-moments under differential privacy achieved through randomized response. From a proposed alternative formulation of randomization matrices compliant with differential privacy guarantees we show that, under attribute-level randomization and where the randomization of the whole distribution is characterized by Kronecker products, any cross-moment of the anonymized dataset involving distinct attributes is equal to the original cross-moment multiplied by attenuation parameters, themselves determined by the part of the overall privacy budget allocated to each attribute. This characterization enables explicit and transparent controls of multivariate relationships preservations in differential privacy through privacy budget allocation.
