Abstract: Machine-learning (ML) malware detectors are known to be vulnerable to adversarial samples. Generative adversarial networks (GANs) such as MalGAN, Mal-LSGAN, and our previous Mal-D2GAN can craft adversarial malware to probe and harden these detectors, yet they operate directly on a high-dimensional, discrete binary feature space, which leads to unstable training, vanishing gradients, and adversarial samples of limited diversity. To address these problems we propose Mal-AAEGAN. First, an adversarial autoencoder (AAE) learns a low-dimensional, regularized and continuous latent representation of the $M$-dimensional binary API feature vector, and the generator performs an additive perturbation in this latent space rather than in the raw binary space. Second, training follows the Wasserstein GAN with gradient penalty (WGAN-GP) scheme using two critics: a mimic-black-box critic that inherits the role of the substitute detector and a naive critic that inherits the role of the additional detector. A decode-binarize-OR pipeline guarantees that the original API calls of the malware are preserved. The model is evaluated on two malware datasets of different input dimensionality ($M\in\{139,215\}$) against eight common ML detectors and compared, under an identical protocol, with MalGAN, Mal-LSGAN and Mal-D2GAN. Mal-AAEGAN trains stably in the continuous latent space and attains an evasion rate on par with or higher than these binary-space baselines across both datasets, while its generator stays responsive to the noise input rather than reducing to a single fixed perturbation per malware.

</details>

Abstract: Bitcoin-style Proof-of-Work (PoW) secures permissionless blockchains but spends substantial computation on hash puzzles which have no utility other than extending the chain. At the same time, we have seen that the rise of generative AI has created significant demand for computational resources that are unfortunately available only to a few players. We propose a solution that solves both problems at the same time. In particular, we present Proof-of-ML-Inference (PoML), a consensus protocol in which miners perform user-requested machine-learning inference as part of their consensus algorithm. We identify exactly what computational assumptions are needed for ML inference to safely replace hash work, formalize those assumptions, and give evidence that LLMs and diffusion models satisfy them. We then formally analyse the security of our protocol by reducing the security of our protocol to the security of the Bitcoin Backbone model. Finally, we empirically evaluate block-time stability, showing that, in a simulated environment, PoML maintains a block production rate close to its calibrated block-time target.

</details>

Abstract: Attackers profit from what defenders do not understand. Software security has treated the gap between system behavior and human comprehension as a condition to narrow through analysis and assurance, booking the remainder as accepted risk. Agentic AI systems change that gap: when software reads, writes, explains, and authorizes security-relevant behavior, partial understanding moves faster, grows more opaque, and couples to delegated authority. This paper characterizes the resulting epistemic attack surface and presents a taxonomy of four interacting attack categories, which target the interpretation of intent, the production and consumption of evidence, the scope of delegated authority, and the comprehension of human supervisors. It then defines accountable partial understanding (APU) and specifies six necessary conditions: bounded scope with explicit assumptions, inspectable evidence, assigned responsibility, visible residual unknowns, actionability, and temporal maintenance. It explains why delegating interpretation to software agents differs from distributing knowledge among people and distinguishes APU from risk acceptance, explainable AI, assurance cases, formal verification, and human-in-the-loop oversight. From the conditions, the paper derives five design principles: bounded delegation, decoupled evidence, assumption tracking with invalidation, role-specific accountability views, and intervention-preserving design with residual unknown registers. It introduces two measurable probe dimensions, evidence linkage and invalidation latency, for testing whether accountability mechanisms hold under change and adversarial pressure. Three running examples (an AI coding agent, an autonomous cyber-defense agent, and an AI-mediated insulin delivery update) ground the analysis. Security without full understanding is already here; the task is to make it accountable.

</details>

Abstract: Information exchange can support living organisation without providing a sufficient criterion for life. We develop a perturbational framework that distinguishes causal transmission, directional imbalance, internal retention, response structure and functional regulation. Randomised interventions define Shannon information channels; for deterministic binary recipients, their context-conditioned mutual information is exactly a paired perturbation-response indicator. Spatial and temporal averages of these local channels measure information-exposure density, rather than joint information or conserved information flux. Complementary Coding Theorem Method and Block Decomposition Method (BDM) estimates describe response and trajectory structure. A same-shaped zero-mask reference separates raw BDM from a structural contrast; neither is a channel mutual information. Retained exploratory BDM plots are distinguished from the normalised causal-channel results. Retention-weighted coupling and directionality obey algebraic bounds, and ensemble reciprocity need not imply reciprocity within individual contexts. For a specified binary sensor--internal-channel--actuator task, we derive a positive information lower bound from successful regulation and construct controllers with identical information profiles but opposite functional outcomes. A study of 2,000 ordered elementary-cellular-automaton rule pairs, six detailed examples, recurrence and lossless coding analyses, and a signed-network extension demonstrates dissociations among perturbational responsiveness, structural regularity and recurrence. The results establish causal information as a necessary resource for the stated form of adaptive regulation, while showing why neither causal coupling nor response complexity alone measures agency or life.

Abstract: This paper considers nonlinear digital self-interference (SI) cancellation and uplink-channel estimation for single-antenna in-band full-duplex radios when a desired uplink pilot is present during SI training. In this setting, a rich nonlinear SI dictionary can absorb part of the structured pilot response, while its large and correlated set of basis functions can make reliable SI estimation difficult, particularly with limited training. During SI-coefficient estimation, we treat the unknown uplink channel as a nuisance parameter and eliminate it to obtain an equivalent reduced SI estimation problem on the orthogonal complement of the pilot convolution subspace. Since the projected nonlinear dictionary can remain ill conditioned, we develop two complementary full-dictionary estimators: a pilot-projected ridge estimator with Bayesian-information-criterion tuning (PP-RidgeBIC), which uses global ridge shrinkage, and a pilot-projected group-sparse Bayesian estimator (PP-GSBE), which learns branch-dependent Bayesian shrinkage from the data. These estimators offer two complementary operating points: PP-RidgeBIC favors computational efficiency, whereas PP-GSBE provides greater adaptivity to heterogeneous nonlinear SI components. Simulations show substantial gains in SI cancellation and uplink reception over non-projected and hard-selection alternatives; notably, PP-GSBE suppresses the residual SI below the noise floor with moderate training and consistently provides the strongest performance under nonlinear model mismatch.

Abstract: Random composition trees model the hierarchical semantic structures of meaningful texts as a statistical ensemble generated by recursively splitting texts into coherent chunks. A recent study shows that different text types can be described by random composition trees with distinct branching factors, and that the entropy rates of these trees exhibit a similar dependence across text types as the entropy rates of the actual texts. Motivated by this correspondence, we analyze information-theoretic properties of this stochastic splitting model. First, we demonstrate that the entropy of random composition trees grows linearly with the root size, and we derive an analytical expression for the entropy rate as a function of the branching factor. Second, we prove that large trees exhibit typicality: with high probability, they belong to a typical set in which each tree is approximately equally likely. Our analysis reveals that the random composition tree model shares two key information-theoretic properties of language when modeled as a stationary and ergodic stochastic process: linear entropy growth and typicality.

</details>

Abstract: We study quantization for inner-product estimation when storage and reading have separate budgets. In the static bit-probe model [Yao, 1981], an encoder stores $w$ bits about $x$ before seeing a query $y$, and a reader probes at most $r<w$ bits to estimate $x^\top y$. Both vectors lie in the $d$-dimensional Euclidean unit ball. Protocols may be randomized, and computation is unrestricted. Performance is measured by $d$ times the worst-case mean-squared error over $x,y$, averaged over protocol randomness.
  First, we show that query-independent reading cannot benefit from extra storage. Any such reader is equivalent to fully reading an $r$-bit message, even when later reads depend on previously returned bits. At fixed positive rates $W=w/d$ and $R=r/d$, the normalized minimax error converges to $1/(4^R-1)$, independent of $W$. The upper bound was already known [Ordentlich and Polyanskiy, 2025]. We prove the matching lower bound for arbitrary scalar decoders, extending the result for unbiased compressors [Safaryan et al., 2020].
  Then, we show that query-dependent reading makes extra storage useful. We construct a two-batch protocol using Gaussian projections and random thresholds, with asymptotic normalized error at most $(π+o(1))/[4R\log(W/R)]$ as $W/R\to\infty$. At fixed $R$, this error vanishes as storage grows, giving an unbounded advantage over query-independent reading. We also prove a finite-dimensional lower bound for arbitrary encoders, decoders, and fully branching readers. In the doubly sparse regime $W/R\to\infty$ and $R\log(W/R)\to0$, it gives normalized error at least $(1-o(1))/[2R\log(W/R)]$. Thus, our protocol is optimal within a factor of $π/2$ in the leading constant.

</details>

Abstract: Denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs) have emerged as a powerful framework for generative modeling by transforming standard Gaussian inputs into outputs that approximate a target distribution. While DDPMs have been proved to converge in total variation, KL divergence, and Wasserstein distance, these discrepancy measures share a fundamental limitation of being insensitive to distributional mismatch in low-probability regions. To address this, we adopt the stronger Rényi divergence as the discrepancy measure. Leveraging a Girsanov-type change-of-measure argument, we establish the first Rényi characterization of DDPMs to the best of our knowledge, showing that the total error decomposes into a mixing error, a discretization error, and a score estimation error. We assume the target distribution is sub-Gaussian and characterize the score error via the cumulant generating function, relaxing both the log-Sobolev-inequality and $L^\infty$-score-error conditions used in prior Rényi divergence analyses of inexact Langevin Monte Carlo, a score-based sampling algorithm closely related to DDPM. Our results also establish an iteration complexity for achieving $ε$-accuracy in $α$-Rényi divergence that is asymptotically proportional to $α^3/ε$. Finally, by extending our analysis, we establish the first Rényi divergence convergence guarantee for critically damped DDPMs, a kinetic variant governed by critically damped Langevin dynamics. Similar to existing characterizations of critically damped DDPMs, this result shares the structural scaling of standard DDPMs, differing by at most a logarithmic factor in the mixing error exponent, and supports the conjecture that critically damped DDPMs do not yield a theoretical improvement.

</details>

Abstract: How much mutual information is needed to achieve a target correlation between two copies of a prescribed distribution? We prove a sharp Gaussian extremal inequality for this self-coupling problem: among distributions with a fixed mean and covariance, the Gaussian has the greatest minimum information cost for meeting a positive semidefinite cross-covariance target. The inequality is strict for non-Gaussian distributions when the target is positive definite. The proof uses a midpoint gluing construction. Two Ornstein--Uhlenbeck half-bridges, each started from the prescribed endpoint law, meet at a common intermediate distribution and are joined by conditional gluing. Changing the Gaussian reference then turns the resulting relative-entropy bound into the desired extremal inequality. As a first application, scaling one endpoint of the self-coupling establishes Gaussian extremality for rate-distortion-perception with unlimited common randomness, under quadratic distortion and squared Wasserstein-2 perception measures. We also establish an analogous result for finite common randomness, where the problem involves two mutual information constraints associated with an auxiliary random variable. In that setting, we retain an intermediate observation on one half-bridge and balance the two information budgets simultaneously using the Poincaré-Miranda theorem.

Abstract: An agent can retain information before receiving a future question and later retrieve evidence from an archive. Evaluating this arrangement requires accounting for both retained information and the width of each retrieval response. Decision-tree capacity bounds describe individual sets of distinguishable records, but a memory writer must encode every possible record before the query is known. We study fixed-length retention beside an immutable binary archive, with a worst-case limit on raw bit or word reads. We characterize feasible one-bit-read memory cells and construct encoders that partition the complete record space. Supporting pairwise exclusive disjunction and conjunction with one bit read is equivalent to supporting every Boolean query. For multiple reads, we combine explicit encoders with decision-tree counting and an entropy converse. The word-read bounds differ by at most two retained bits when the read budget is at most half the archive length. Under an explicit condition on word width and archive length, raw-word caching attains the exact optimum among all writers. A related condition preserves this optimum under positive average error. Exhaustive checks validate the implementations. In a matched dialogue-agent study, a categorical sufficient statistic preserves source decisions with less storage and lower decoding latency than the universal encoder. A catalogue-specific index reduces private state further and requires additional shared storage. The results characterize how response width and future query structure determine retention requirements.

</details>

Abstract: Recent advances in artificial intelligence have contributed to several information-theoretic results through genuinely different modes: a language model may suggest a proof, participate in an extended mathematical dialogue, coordinate computation, or operate as one component of a search harness. Treating all of these as "AI-discovered mathematics" obscures distinctions that matter for scientific reliability, attribution, and reproducibility. This article examines a set of recent cases through a four-stage pipeline: how the problem was formulated; how AI participated in finding a solution; how the resulting analytical and computational claims were verified; and how machine-generated outputs were translated into mathematical exposition for human understanding. Based on these cases, we recommend that research publications (1) disclose the nature and extent of AI assistance at each stage, (2) report verification of analytical reasoning separately from certification of numerical computations, and (3) place more emphasis on articulating the underlying ideas, techniques, and interpretations.

</details>

Abstract: Secure aggregation enables a server to compute the sum of users' gradients or model updates without revealing individual values. With user dropouts, correlated random keys protect local gradients while allowing recovery of the desired gradient sum. We consider three phases: key distribution, masked-gradient upload, and mask elimination. Users establish correlated keys, upload masked gradients, and provide additional messages to eliminate residual aggregate masks. Existing information-theoretic schemes typically design mask-elimination communication for a prescribed dropout threshold and evaluate its cost under worst-case dropouts across the masked-gradient upload and mask-elimination phases. Such designs cannot adjust communication to actual dropout scenarios. Moreover, the randomness and communication costs of establishing correlated keys remain largely unexplored. To address these limitations, we formulate an adaptive secure aggregation framework under the T-colluding security constraint and construct a single scheme that supports all feasible dropout scenarios by adapting its communication to the actual number of dropouts. We establish converse bounds on four resources: key randomness, key-distribution communication, masked-gradient communication, and mask-elimination communication. The latter two bounds are characterized for each realized dropout set in the masked-gradient upload phase. The proposed scheme achieves the optimal masked-gradient communication rate for every feasible dropout scenario and the optimal mask-elimination communication rate in almost all feasible regimes. Its key rate and key-distribution communication rate are each optimal or within a factor of two of their respective information-theoretic optima. These results characterize the fundamental randomness and communication costs of secure aggregation with user dropouts.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we investigate the information bottleneck problem for sources that follow discrete-time ergodic finite-state Markov chains. We propose treating observations generated by the time-varying Markov chain as if they were drawn from its unique stationary distribution, and we use the solution to the information bottleneck problem for the stationary process to compress samples from the time-varying process. The proofs are based on intricate analysis using convergence of subsequences along with the derivation of properties of the optimization sets associated with the information bottleneck problem. In addition to the inequalities and convergence results we establish, our analysis offers insights into how simple properties of ergodic Markov chains give rise to results concerning the information bottleneck problem for such chains.

Abstract: We introduce and study the adaptive covering complexity C adapt S (s), defined as the minimum number of contiguous factor blocks required to partition a string s when each block is either a factor of a fixed external dictionary S or a factor of the target's already decoded prefix. This hybrid admissibility model formalizes the structural interaction between static dictionary matching and dynamic nonoverlapping self-referencing. Abstract: Maximum rank-distance codes were introduced independently by Delsarte (1978) and Gabidulin (1985) for unrestricted matrix spaces, and their study was subsequently extended to the classical form spaces of alternating, symmetric, and Hermitian matrices. For Hermitian $d$-codes, Schmidt (2018) established the upper bound and constructed optimal additive codes for all admissible parameters except when $n$ and $d$ are both even and $4\le d\le n-2$. Rather than working directly in the space of Hermitian matrices, we construct our objects within central simple algebras with unitary involution arising from quotients of skew polynomial rings. We also introduce scalar-field and Lyapunov operators in these algebras, leading to new invariants for the equivalence of additive Hermitian codes. Abstract: Let $q=p^f$, where $p$ is prime. We prove that every $q$-ary linear code meeting the Griesmer bound, with positive dimension and minimum distance $d\ge1$, is $p^{\max\{0,ν_p(d)-f+1\}}$-divisible, as asserted in Ward's conjecture. Arbitrary projective column multiplicities are allowed. The geometric ingredient is a bound independent of dimension: a nonzero scalar-invariant function $F:\mathbb{F}_q^k\to\mathbb{F}_p$, with $F(0)=0$ and prime-field algebraic degree at most $(f+s)(p-1)$, vanishes identically on at most $p^s(q-1)$ linear hyperplanes when $0\le s<f$. Abstract: In analog coded computing, encoding basis functions are typically chosen to guarantee exact reconstruction and improve numerical stability. However, the same basis functions also determine the statistical geometry of the observations available to colluding workers, and hence directly influence the privacy of the underlying data. This suggests that basis selection should not be viewed solely as a reconstruction or numerical conditioning problem, but rather as a joint privacy and accuracy co-design problem. Motivated by this observation, we revisit Analog Lagrange Coded Computing (ALCC) and show that the full orthogonality constraints imposed by the conventional Lagrange basis are stronger than what is required for exact recovery. We retain only the interpolation constraints necessary for the data bearing basis functions and require the noise bearing basis functions merely to vanish at the data recovery points. This yields a parameterized family of generalized polynomial encoding bases that preserves exact reconstruction while introducing additional design degrees of freedom. We then derive a basis-dependent characterization of differential privacy for colluding workers and formulate a privacy-calibrated numerical optimization problem that jointly selects the data and noise basis functions to reduce reconstruction error while satisfying a prescribed \((ε,δ)\)-DP requirement. The resulting framework therefore treats the encoding basis itself as a privacy-design variable, rather than selecting a fixed closed-form basis a priori. The same design philosophy is also compatible with alternative privacy metrics such as mutual-information leakage. Numerical results show that, at the same privacy level, optimized basis functions substantially reduce floating-point reconstruction error relative to conventional ALCC.

Abstract: Peres's iteration of von Neumann's procedure is completed into a bijection of $\bits^N$: the recursion runs to exhaustion, nothing is discarded, and every input splits into fair bits and residual bits. The residual strings form a complete prefix code that determines the number of ones of the input, so the fair bits are exactly uniform given the residual for every exchangeable law. For a Bernoulli source the likelihood factorises exactly, in decoding order, into a factor $1/2$ per fair bit and a Bernoulli factor per residual bit, with biases obtained by iterating two explicit maps. The number of fair bits per input symbol tends to the entropy almost surely, also on larger alphabets. Code B stores the fair bits and codes only the residual with an arithmetic coder; its ideal length is that of the two-part code, at most $\log\_2(N+1)+1$ bits above $-\log\_2\Pr\_θ(s)$ for every input $s$ and bias $θ$. Code C uses Rice codes instead. In experiments up to $N=2^{20}$ its mean regret stays below $200$ bits, where a Rice coder on the raw input loses a fixed fraction of the entropy on skewed sources. For sources with memory, the Burrows--Wheeler transform of the reversed string arranges all context orders in one tree of intervals; coding the leaves of a pruned tree as Bernoulli strings adapts to every Markov order up to its depth, and on suffix-closed trees the leaf weights determine the split points.

</details>

Abstract: Binary Goppa codes are important in coding theory and cryptography. It is well-known that the minimum distance of a binary Goppa code defined by a squarefree polynomial of degree $r$ is at least $2r+1$. Though from the classical results of C. J. Moreno, O. Moreno, F. Levy-dit-Vehel and S. Litsyn that the true minimum distances of binary Goppa codes are $2r+1$, when the extension field degree $m$ is larger than $(r-1)^{4r}$. There have only very few families of binary Goppa codes with determined true minimum distances.
  In this paper, we give necessary and sufficient conditions for binary Goppa codes such that their minimum distances are $2r+1$ and $2r+2$. Infinitely many infinite families of binary Goppa codes with minimum distance $2r+1$ are given. In particular, we construct such codes for every Goppa degree $r$ in the range $2^{h-1}<r<2^h$ over $\mathbb F_{2^{2h}}$. For every $2\le r\le\lfloor2^m/3\rfloor$, we also obtain at least $2^m-3r+1$ distinct monic squarefree Goppa polynomials of degree $r$ over $\mathbb F_{2^m}$, such that their related binary Goppa codes have minimum distance $2r+1$. We extend a criterion for $q$-ary Goppa codes to split auxiliary polynomials and obtain some infinite families of $q$-ary Goppa codes attaining their designed distances $r+1$. We also construct infinite families of binary Goppa codes with minimum distance exactly $2r+2$.

</details>

Abstract: The service rate region of a distributed storage system describes the set of concurrent demand vectors that can be served without overloading any individual server, and is a fundamental measure of how effectively redundancy is used. In this paper, we investigate aggregate service rates in distributed data storage systems based on linear codes over arbitrary finite fields. We first derive an upper bound on the aggregate service rate for an arbitrary subset of $t$ data objects in terms of the dual distance and the sizes of their smallest recovery sets. For systematic generator matrices, this bound simplifies to an expression depending only on the code length, the dual distance, and the cardinality of the selected subset. This result extends existing bounds and provides a subset-wise formulation of the First Dual Distance Bound. We then establish conditions under which this upper bound is attained. We apply our results to several important families of linear codes, including MDS codes, Reed-Muller codes, constacyclic codes, and extended Golay codes, and obtain exact values of the maximum aggregate service rates for the corresponding subsets of data objects.

TL;DR: 该论文提出并验证了一种基于Luenberger观测器和Kalman滤波器的工业机械齿轮箱润滑剂温度估算方法，误差仅1.3 K，可用于实现温度相关误差补偿。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 工业机器人的位置和路径精度受到温度变化的显著影响，尤其是黏滞摩擦、磁滞和齿轮箱传动误差；但传统工业机器人缺乏温度传感器，导致温度相关误差难以补偿。

Method: 构建双态热传递模型，并将其与Luenberger观测器及基于舱壳温度度量的Kalman滤波器相结合，形成润滑剂温度估算结构；随后在实验中对齿轮箱进行热学测试以验证估算误差。

Result: 实验验证显示所开发的观测器对齿轮箱润滑剂温度的平均绝对误差为1.3 K，表明其估算精度较高且可用于后续误差补偿。

Conclusion: 本研究通过在齿轮箱内置温度估计器，利用Luenberger观测器和Kalman滤波器有效估算润滑剂温度，验证了其在工业机器人齿轮箱温度监测中的可行性，并为实现温度相关误差补偿奠定了基础。

Abstract: The position and path accuracy of industrial robots is strongly affected by temperature. In particular, temperature-dependent friction and altered hysteresis and transmission errors in the gearboxes are the main causes of these deviations. Since standard industrial robots lack temperature sensors, it is proposed to estimate the gearbox lubricant temperature using observer structures. It is planned to apply the proposed lubricant temperature observer to provide the input for temperature-dependent error compensation for gearboxes in industrial robots. First, a two-state heat transfer model describing the heat transfer from the lubricant through the gearbox housing to the environment is described and identified. It is integrated into a Luenberger observer and a Kalman filter with a correction of the lubricant temperature estimation based on measurements from a housing temperature sensor. Temperature measurements were performed in an experimental setup with a cycloidal gear filled with grease, which is common for industrial robots. Due to its changing viscosity and changing thermal conductivity at different shear rates, it shows a less uniform thermal behavior than gear-oil. For the validation measurements, the developed observers showed a mean absolute error of 1.3 K.

</details>


### [446] [Transmission Error Compensation for Industrial Robots Using Fourier Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23882)
*Christian J. E. Bauer,Simon Nowinski,Valentin Kamm,Lukas Steinle,Armin Lechler,Alexander Verl*

Main category: eess.SY

TL;DR: 提出激光跟踪+傅里叶级数模型的传动误差识别与补偿方法，实验验证显著提升机器人关节精度。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 工业机器人离线编程后的精度受限于关节柔顺性和传动误差，传统方式需要在线教学，需在实验中寻找更高效的误差补偿方案。

Method: 使用激光跟踪测量获取机器人的关节角度误差；构建速度相关的傅里叶级数模型，并在虚拟调试软件中作为功能模拟单元；利用该模型校正CNC设定点，实现在线补偿。

Result: 在 Stäubli TX2-40 工业机器人上实验，模型平均绝对误差为0.114 mrad；补偿后轴2精度提升83.26%，轴3提升49.21%。

Conclusion: 本研究通过基于激光跟踪测量的速度相关傅里叶级数模型，有效识别并补偿工业机器人传动误差，显著提升了机器人驱动轴的定位精度。

Abstract: Due to their low accuracy, industrial robots commonly require online teaching after the offline development of a robot program in simulation. The accuracy is limited by compliance and transmission errors in the robot joints. Transmission errors are defined as errors between the load-side angles of the gearboxes and the motor encoder angles. An approach to identify the transmission errors based on laser tracker measurements is presented. The developed velocity-dependent Fourier series model is integrated in a virtual commissioning software as a co-simulated functional mock-up unit. Additionally, it is used to compensate the transmission errors by correcting the setpoint from the computerized numerical control (CNC). The model identification and the compensation are experimentally evaluated with a Stäubli TX2-40 industrial robot. The model has a mean absolute error of 0.114 mrad, and the compensation improves the accuracy by 83.26 % for axis 2 and 49.21 % for axis 3.

</details>

Abstract: This paper develops a polynomial parametric Koopman operator (PPKO) framework for stochastic model predictive control (SMPC) of nonlinear systems with parametric uncertainty and time-varying stochastic disturbances. The Koopman operator is parameterized by polynomial chaos expansions (PCEs), enabling data-driven nonlinear dynamics to be integrated with PCE-based uncertainty propagation and control. We show that the polynomial parameterization introduces no irreducible approximation gap beyond PCE truncation. We then derive a condensed deterministic SMPC reformulation that handles both time-invariant parametric uncertainty and time-varying disturbances while retaining an online optimization dimension that depends only on the control horizon and input dimension, independent of the lifted-state and PCE dimensions. The resulting formulation yields convex programs with expectation and second-moment constraints that can be solved using standard optimization methods. Numerical studies on the Duffing oscillator and the electrothermal cooling system demonstrate the accuracy, scalability, and closed-loop performance of the proposed framework.

</details>

Abstract: Reduced-order model architectures are often used to decompose complex safety-critical control design tasks into tractable subproblems. In this paradigm, one must ask: when does the safety of a reduced-order model lift to safety of the full system? Taking an invariant manifold perspective, we provide necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of smooth, regular lifts of compact safe sets from reduced-order models. Our characterization relies only on asymptotic stability of an embedding of a reduced-order safe set, without additional robustness or rate conditions. We apply these results to study control barrier functions in reduced-order model architectures and lifts of safe sets through cascades. Further, we use our conditions to obtain topological obstructions to the tracking of safe, kinematic reduced-order models for mechanical systems. We conclude by using these obstructions to inform safe hierarchical control design for an underactuated satellite on SE(3).

</details>

Abstract: This paper studies networked control for nonlinear plants subject to several network-induced constraints, with a particular focus on transmission delays. In the context of general nonlinear networked control systems, a standard approach for guaranteeing stability is to construct a storage function, coupled with a single timer variable, to analyze the maximum allowable transmission interval (MATI) and maximum allowable transmission delay (MAD). In this paper, we propose a new approach that assigns each signal that is either \textit{in transit or most recently updated}, to an individual timer variable. This enables the individual characterization and compensation for the dynamics of each potential updating error. Subsequently, based on a novel construction of timer-coupled storage functions, the dynamics of these timer variables is designed and investigated, leading to less conservative conditions on the MATI and MAD than those by the standard approach while still ensuring closed-loop input-to-state stability with respect to external disturbances. Finally, simulation results are given to illustrate the advantages of these new results.

</details>

Abstract: This paper studies a hybrid discrete-time signed gradient-consensus method with heavy-ball momentum, paired communication delays, and fixed logarithmic quantization. The method preserves the aggregate supply-demand equality at every iteration. For a static connected undirected graph and bounded delays, a Lyapunov-Krasovskii analysis establishes practical stability by isolating the discontinuous quantization error as an additive disturbance and closing a computable invariant-sublevel argument. The resulting ergodic residual bound is quadratic in the quantization resolution, and exact convergence to the smooth penalized equality-constrained optimizer is recovered in the unquantized case. For delay-free unquantized quadratic costs, an exact modal analysis identifies the heavy-ball stability region and optimal parameters, with the predicted rate validated on a fixed sparse network. A reproducible delayed experiment reports all constants and a certificate whose guaranteed steady-state level lies below the reported initialization. A separate mixed generator-battery experiment exercises the signed update directly. Finite smooth penalties approximate, but do not generally enforce, the local box constraints exactly.

Abstract: This paper proposes a distributed resource allocation algorithm in which each agent communicates only the sign of its local cost gradient, a single bit per directed link per iteration. The algorithm is based on Laplacian-gradient dynamics and preserves the equality (demand) constraint at every iterate, guaranteeing anytime feasibility irrespective of termination time.
  First, we provide a complete Lyapunov-based convergence analysis for the fixed-step-size regime: the iterates are shown to enter, in at most O(1/\varepsilon^2) iterations, a neighborhood of the optimizer whose radius scales as O(τ) in the decision variable and O(τ^2) in the cost residual, where $τ$ is the step-size. Crucially, the step-size bound is a time-invariant network constant (depending only on the graph Laplacian spectrum and the cost curvature bounds), so no adaptive tuning is required.
  Second, we characterize the invariant set under B-connected, weight-balanced digraphs, strictly weaker than the all-time connectivity and doubly-stochastic weights assumed in most prior work, and show that any odd, sign-preserving nonlinearity yields the same invariant-set and descent structure, with the single-bit protocol being the extreme communication-minimizing instance of a broader nonlinear class.
  Third, we address local box constraints via smooth penalty relaxation and quantify explicitly how the penalty weight interacts with the step-size bound and the optimality gap.
  Simulations on economic dispatch (1000 generators) and CPU scheduling (500 servers) demonstrate that the single-bit protocol achieves 5-60 times reduction in total communication cost relative to multi-bit baselines, at the price of a tunable and explicitly bounded optimality gap. A diminishing step-size schedule is shown empirically to eliminate this gap; its formal convergence analysis is identified as an open problem.

</details>

Abstract: Understanding how a particular automated vehicle reacts to nearby traffic can improve motion prediction, control development, traffic simulation, and safety evaluation. However, commercial automated driving systems are proprietary, and single encounters may provide only a few seconds of noisy data. Standard physical models are interpretable but inflexible, whereas neural models are often data-hungry. We propose a physics-informed Koopman framework that combines a calibrated car-following law with a correction learned from the observations. A weak-form estimator learns this correction from velocity measurements integrated over time, avoiding noise-sensitive numerical differentiation. The resulting continuous-time model predicts the follower's response and provides interpretable measures of driving behavior. Experiments on OpenACC and CATS-CF evaluate 5-, 7-, and 11-second histories. Averaged over these histories, the constant-time-headway branch reduces speed prediction error by 16.0% and 15.4% on OpenACC and CATS-CF, respectively, relative to the best neural baselines. The PI-Koopman branches achieve the lowest mean speed and gap errors among the physical and neural comparators. Weak-form estimation reduces acceleration-field reconstruction error by 74.5% in noisy known-law simulations and noise-induced field perturbation by 91.6% in paired real-vehicle measurements. The same PI-Koopman system identification supports following-mode recognition with an approximately eleven-fold difference in median residual variance between human drivers and automated vehicles, and a single residual threshold achieves 85.4% balanced accuracy. The branch informed by the intelligent driver model takes a median 17 to 26 ms for identification and forecasting on the tested central processing units, supporting frequent target adaptation.

Abstract: Open radio access network (O-RAN) enables a multi-vendor ecosystem that accelerates xApp development for near-real-time (RT) network management. However, this openness shifts a combinatorial coordination burden to the Non-RT layer, where rApp policies compose xApps from a growing catalog to realize operator intents. Coexisting intents further compound this challenge, as conflicts may arise among xApp actions, rApp policies, and their cross-layer interactions. To maximize intent admission while maintaining key performance indicator (KPI) compliance across incoming and incumbent intents, we propose a large language model-based Multi-Agentic AI framework. A Reasoning Agent screens candidate xApps against KPI contracts and produces a joint intent admission and xApp assignment decision. On post-deployment KPI violations, a Diagnosis Agent identifies the root causes of failure, labels the conflict mechanism, and guides the Reasoning Agent in subsequent attempts. Experimental results show that the framework based on GPT-OSS 120B achieves 28.6% more KPI-compliant cases and admits over 15* as many incoming intents as state-of-the-art baselines. Under the Qwen 72B variant, Diagnosis increases compliant cases by 50.0%, quadruples admitted intents, and reduces deployment attempts by 32.6%. A live deployment conforming to O-RAN specifications further validates the feasibility of the proposed solution for conflict-aware rApp policy orchestration in real networks.

Abstract: The single-degree-of-freedom swing equation with sinusoidal coupling is locally softening: its oscillation frequency decreases with oscillation level near a stable equilibrium. Nonlinear modal interaction, however, can reverse this trend in multimachine systems. This letter gives a necessary-and-sufficient condition for strict leading-order local hardening, measured with respect to canonical action, in two symmetric three-machine operating-point families with equal or opposite power injections at the equal-inertia machines. An exact two-coordinate reduction expresses the condition in terms of a squared modal-frequency ratio and a dimensionless nonlinear coupling measure. The proof retains the induced mean shift and second harmonic and shows that the companion modal family remains locally softening. The criterion recovers both an equal-inertia hardening example and an exact large-inertia threshold. Coherent aggregation recovers the two-machine softening law, whereas the infinite-bus limit retains two dynamic rotor angles and permits hardening. An exact coherent-group extension preserves the selected periodic family, frequency, and canonical action for every finite machine count greater than two. Thus, three finite-inertia machines are the minimum permitting local hardening in the stated undamped, lossless classical model.

Abstract: This paper studies an airport campus where long stay EV parking, airport buildings, and an AI data center share a single grid connection. The campus pays a commercial electricity tariff that charges for both the energy used and the highest power drawn during peak hours. Parked EVs can send power back to the campus through vehicle to grid (V2G) chargers and lower this peak. This paper proposes a two stage method. In the first stage, a linear program schedules EV charging and discharging to maximize the operator's profit and also sets a fixed daily payment to EV owners. In the second stage, a search chooses the share of energy savings paid to owners. The model guarantees that every owner is paid enough to take part, pays for battery wear so that EVs discharge only when it is worth the wear, and assigns charging ports on a first come, first served basis. In the case study, 400 V2G chargers are added to 1200 existing Level 2 chargers, serving 5000 expected sessions under summer and winter rates. In a summer week, V2G raises operator profit by $4978 compared with smart charging and lowers the high peak demand by 4400 kW (9%). The best contract gives owners 70% of the energy savings and earns 21% more than a fixed payment alone. Doubling the V2G ports to 800 lowers the peak further but reduces profit, because owner payments grow faster than the savings. V2G pays back within 10 years if a bidirectional charger costs less than about $1800 more than a standard one. Compared with a 5 MW/10 MWh stationary battery, V2G cuts more peak demand at $409 of investment per kW instead of $1522 and needs no extra land, while the battery does not pay back within its lifetime.

Abstract: Remote wildlife monitoring increasingly depends on high-fidelity cameras and sensors deployed far from grid power and terrestrial networks. Low Earth orbit satellite links can provide connectivity in these settings, but their high power draw and variable bandwidth, combined with intermittent on-site solar harvesting, make continuous backhaul infeasible. Current systems therefore filter at the edge, with motion triggers that waste limited energy and bandwidth on environmental artifacts, or fixed vision models that cannot follow evolving expert objectives.

As a representative case study, we deployed \textsc{ATLAS} for over six months across 18 off-grid salmon-monitoring river sites. \textsc{ATLAS} achieves a mean clip-selection accuracy of $87.4\%$, exceeding mean baseline accuracy by $26.4$ percentage points, while maintaining near-continuous operation through low-solar periods in which baselines incur 16.0\% downtime.

Abstract: We consider a multi-class, multi-pool queueing model of a telephone call center in which a service completion does not always end the call. The caller may be transferred to another agent for a second service, so the assignment of an agent to a call affects not only the caller's waiting time but also whether the call is resolved and how much further work it creates. We model these transfers as Markovian routing from original to rework classes, and the system manager dynamically assigns agent pools to classes so as to maximize the value generated by service, net of transfer penalties and waiting and abandonment costs, over a finite horizon. Focusing on the Halfin-Whitt heavy-traffic regime, we derive an approximating Brownian control problem. Its Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation is a semilinear partial differential equation whose dimension equals the number of classes. Building on the deep splitting method of Beck et al. (2021), we develop a simulation-based computational method that approximates the value function and its gradient by neural networks, and we use it to construct an implementable routing policy for the original (prelimit) system. We calibrate the model to publicly available data from a large U.S. bank call center. In all test problems we consider, the proposed policy outperforms the best available benchmark policy we could find.

Abstract: Modern power grids increasingly rely on power electronic interfaces, requiring steady-state power flow analysis to accommodate diverse operating modes and network structures. Conventional approaches often rely on system-specific formulations and sequential approaches to handle such complexity. However, these approaches may limit applicability to diverse grid configurations and lead to convergence and computational issues. To address these limitations, this paper proposes a generalized and unified Newton-Raphson (NR) power flow framework for AC/DC hybrid grids. For generalized operational modeling, conventional and droop operating modes of generators and interlinking converters are represented through a common set of coefficients. For generalized structural modeling, models of AC transformers and DC/DC converters accommodate different transformer winding configurations and converter operating modes. For a unified solution, augmented variables are introduced to incorporate droop deadbands, output limits, and converter station losses into a single NR formulation, eliminating the need for sequential outer loops. To validate the effectiveness of the proposed framework, its results are compared with those obtained from existing power flow methods and electromagnetic transient simulations across transmission networks, distribution networks, and microgrids. The implementation is made publicly available to facilitate its use in analyzing AC/DC hybrid grids.

</details>

Abstract: The electrification of residential energy systems and adoption of electric vehicles (EVs) create demand-side flexibility. However, random driving behaviour makes it difficult to ensure EV readiness at departure and maintain thermal comfort. To address this, we formulate residential scheduling as a distributionally robust (DR) chance-constrained Markov decision process, whose 2-Wasserstein ambiguity set guards against tail risk and distribution shift in EV behaviour. The intractable DR chance constraint is then replaced by a sufficient tightened expectation constraint under the nominal distribution. The resulting problem is solved by a Lagrangian soft actor--critic with distributional cost critics, and an online boundary guard enforces EV reachability and thermal comfort within the actuator limits at every step. Five controllers are compared over one year in Wellington, New Zealand, under a degraded forecast. The proposed controller is the cheapest controller that meets the EV target on every day and both chance constraints under forecast error and random driving behaviour. Compared with the rule-based baseline, the only other controller to do so, it lowers the annual cost by 8.6% and the comfort violation hours by 69%. The ablation study confirms that the boundary guard ensures pointwise feasibility within the actuator limits, and the DR chance constraint reduces tail risk.

Abstract: Wind farms can provide flexibility by curtailing their output during periods of excess generation in the power grid. This can be done through implicit balancing, where the power setpoints of the farm are adapted to anticipate the imbalance price. By doing so, the wind farm helps reduce the resulting system imbalance while also lowering its own costs. However, implicit balancing is challenging since the imbalance prices are unknown in advance and hard to predict. Furthermore, the control strategy of the wind farm can affect the imbalance price formation. To address both challenges, we develop a control policy with 1-minute setpoints using distributional soft actor-critic. Evaluated on real data of the Belgian grid and an offshore wind farm, we show that our method outperforms a price threshold controller, often used in industry, by 88% and performs on par with soft actor-critic. Furthermore, unlike soft actor-critic, our approach can learn a risk-averse policy by optimizing a risk measure, such as conditional value at risk. The risk-averse policy was found to better account for its own impact on the price, showing a reduction in overshoots, while achieving a similar degree of imbalance cost savings. Last, we find that using 1-minute instead of 15-minute setpoints further reduces imbalance costs for all evaluated control policies.

Abstract: When several generators share a fuel supply, one interruption can disable them together and cause electricity shortages lasting days. Such interruptions can be made less frequent, for example by weatherizing gas supply, or shorter, for example by on-site backup fuel. Yet traditional resource adequacy indices, expected unserved energy and loss-of-load hours, fail to distinguish such measures whenever they remove the same expected time without fuel, because for interruptions independent of system conditions these indices depend on the interruptions only through this time. Their difference is revealed by prolonged-shortage exposure, the expected annual hours in shortages of at least a given duration, whose exact chronological calculation must be repeated for every interruption scenario. To reduce this cost, a fast reassessment method with guaranteed accuracy is proposed. Network calculations are performed once and condensed into a restoration response, the additional prolonged-shortage hours caused by one interruption of each duration, from which any scenario is evaluated as a weighted sum without new simulation. An hour-by-hour error bound determines whether this evaluation is accurate enough, and exact dynamic programming is applied otherwise, so that every result lies within a requested tolerance of the exact value on the same Monte Carlo samples. On the Reliability Test System of the Grid Modernization Laboratory Consortium, most evaluations were accepted, fully accepted scenarios typically ran more than 200 times faster than exact calculation, and total computation time decreased in every workload. Measures against fuel interruptions can thus be screened quickly and accurately by their effect on prolonged shortages.

</details>

Abstract: We study how many output samples match the worst-case state reconstruction error obtainable from a continuous history under bounded measurement noise. For observable scalar-output linear time-varying systems with $n$ states, $n$ samples suffice for exact reconstruction, whereas at most $n^2$ attain the continuous-history minimax error in the $\ell_\infty$ norm. An explicit construction proves sharpness even for real-analytic systems. For time-invariant systems, strict total positivity yields one $n$-sample schedule optimal for every state coordinate; endpoint-observed tridiagonal cooperative chains satisfy this conclusion in physical coordinates. Interior sensing behaves differently: an explicit three-state family requires four samples despite positivity and distinct real eigenvalues. The strict three-state gap persists under small stabilizing damping. A nilpotent four-state example and an interval proof for a six-state mechanical system give gaps. The results identify how temporal sampling and the prescribed state-error geometry determine the measurements required for minimax reconstruction. Time-domain experiments illustrate the resulting estimation bounds.

Abstract: The continuous evolution of next-generation power systems, characterized by the high penetration of renewable energy resources and dynamic topology variations, poses formidable challenges to traditional N-1/N-2 contingency analysis in terms of computational efficiency and scalability. While deep learning-based methods offer accelerated inference, existing studies predominantly treat contingency analysis as a pure regression problem, often overlooking the physical essence of grid operation and engineering requirements of real-world scenarios. To bridge this gap, this paper proposes a physics and prompt-guided graph foundation model (PhyPro-FM) for generalizable and engineering-viable contingency analysis. The proposed architecture integrates graph neural networks with a learnable global query mechanism and, notably, introduces a prompt-guided learning paradigm. By leveraging historical snapshots as few-shot prompts, the model dynamically captures correlations between target and historical operating conditions, significantly enhancing generalization capabilities under unseen topologies. Furthermore, we design a physics-informed training mechanism that prioritizes branch power flow errors over nodal voltage errors, aligning the optimization objective with critical grid security constraints. To ensure engineering applicability, a physics-informed inference mechanism is also developed, incorporating a residual-based correction module and a specialized post-processing strategy for singular branches to circumvent numerical instability. Abstract: The rapid integration of power electronics converters has fundamentally altered the power grid's dynamic landscape, making modularity of operation and interoperability of heterogeneous devices two critical hurdles for power system reliability. In control language, these challenges can be paraphrased as decentralized certificates on the input/output behavior of every device, assured by local control, so that stability and guaranteed performance of the interconnected system emerge. For the power system application, such certificates should eventually be part of the rule book, the so-called grid code, and thus be generic and minimally restrictive for the control architecture of each device. Classical system-theoretic angles of attack to these questions go back to dissipativity concepts, which have historically been co-developed in automatic control, circuit theory, and power systems. Since standard dissipativity (e.g., passivity or small gain) criteria fall short in terms of conservatism, a range of more flexible and advanced tools have been developed to assure less conservative decentralized certificates guaranteeing stability under interconnection as well as dynamic performance following a disturbance. In this article, we review a range of methods that have been put forward to tackle these questions, compare and validate them, and point towards practically relevant research gaps which are amenable to control-theoretic approaches. Moreover, we provide a tutorial exposition of practically relevant power systems/electronics issues accessible to control engineers.

Abstract: Safe AC dispatch requires both feasible operating points and enough ramping capability to reach the next target. This paper constructs convex inner approximations that connect nonlinear network feasibility, constrained device response, and dispatch-interval tracking. A fixed-point certificate enforces AC operating limits under bounded load uncertainty. Componentwise response envelopes convert heterogeneous device dynamics into affine conditions on measured states and power references. A command governor then combines these conditions with available ramping capability and a hard terminal-error bound. It selects an admissible target through a minimum-deviation objective and holds the corresponding reference while reserved fast regulation remains active. The resulting quadratic program has no network-trajectory decision variables. Abstract: Nonlinear state-space system identification has attracted decades of interest, yet remains challenging because of the interplay among unknown parameters, nonlinear dynamics, and latent state variables. Its difficulty increases dramatically when the state and parameter dimensions increase. To address this challenge, we develop an ensemble Kalman inversion (EnKI)-based approach. This approach leverages EnKI to perform maximum a posteriori parameter estimation through successive local Gaussian approximations, enabling an iterative and incremental search for unknown parameters. The search combines Monte Carlo sampling with Kalman-type updates to evolve an ensemble of parameter samples, thereby offering empirical stability and the ability to handle strongly nonlinear models. The EnKI search is coupled with state estimation conditioned on the latest parameter estimates. This enables parameter identification along with latent state recovery. To improve scalability for large datasets, we further develop a cascaded EnKI (CasEnKI) method, which assimilates subdatasets sequentially to accelerate computation. We validate the proposed methods using a lithium-ion battery model with ten state variables and 18 unknown parameters. Simulation results show that the EnKI approach offers high identification accuracy and empirical stability in parameter search. Further, experimental results illustrate the computational efficiency and scalability of the CasEnKI method for large battery datasets.

</details>

Abstract: We develop a model-reduction technique based on Lie group theory for general engineering models described by a system of algebraic, ordinary, and/or partial differential equations with an arbitrary number of inputs, outputs, and constant parameters. The proposed method reduces the parameter space of these models without changing their external behavior, that is, their input-output maps. We apply this method to phase-field models used for high-fidelity simulations in electrochemistry to accelerate the analysis and design of metal-ion batteries.

### [478] [Data Center Oscillation Isolation and Grid Support: Fundamental Limits and Source-Selective Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26812)
*Cheng Feng*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: The grid integration of large data centers poses a growing power-system stability challenge. Rapidly varying computing demand can induce oscillations, while the facilities must provide a prescribed response to grid disturbances. Existing controls address load fluctuations and grid support but do not establish their jointly achievable performance. This paper derives fundamental limits and develops a source-selective controller for simultaneous oscillation isolation and grid support. A coupled closed-loop model gives a condition for distinguishing disturbance sources and reveals a direct isolation-support conflict under grid-import-power feedback alone; additional electrical measurements can remove this pointwise conflict but leave a conditional cross-frequency sensitivity limit. Guided by these limits, we design a local residual-feedback storage controller through convex frequency-domain optimization with stability, isolation, and grid-response constraints across operating points. Tests on a single-generator interconnection and a modified nine-bus network show stronger oscillation isolation than equal-order optimized comparators while meeting the prescribed grid response across operating points.
